1 0 jm iDj1/2iD , jm iDj(cid:2)1/2iD , (3.4)

I 0 S 1 (3.2) becomes 2 3 6 7 6 1 7 jm iDjm i˝jm iD4 5. (3.5)

Then the element from the top of the column v ector representing jm m i has the value 1 and all the other elements have the value 0. In the example (3.5), n D 2. We can use the integers n D n (m m ) to order the basis vectors jm m i. We will think of the index m as labeling a row and m as labeling a column of the rectangular array, jm m i. If we rearrange the basis vectors of this array as a single column vector, with each column of the rectangular array placed below the one on its left, the ordering indices n (m m ) will be in the same order as the integers, 1, 2, 3, .... With this ordering convention we can define the elements of the Kronecker product as

hα|A ⊗ B|α'0i = B(α')A(αα'). (3.6)

Consider the [S] × [S] matrix X with elements hm S|X|jm' S i that represents an operator that couples only the electron sublevels jm S i and jm' S i. Multiplying the uncoupled basis state (3.2) by X and using the mixed-product rule [39],

(A ⊗ B)(C ⊗ D) = (AC) ⊗ (BD), (3.7)

we find

jm I i ⊗ X|jm S i = X J jm m i, (3.8)

S I

where the "grave" matrix is

X J = 1{I} ⊗ X . (3.9)

Here 1{I} denotes the [I] × [I] unit matrix with elements

hm I|1{I}|jm' I i = δ mI , m' I . (3.10)

Similarly, multiplying an uncoupled basis state (3.2) by an [I] × [I] matrix Y that represents an operator that only couples nuclear sublevels, we find

Y|jm I i ⊗ jm S i = Y K jm m i, (3.11)

S I

where the "acute" matrix is

Y K = Y ⊗ 1{S} . (3.12)

Here 1{S} denotes the [S] × [S] unit matrix with elements

hm S|1{S}|jm' S i = δ mS, m' S . (3.13)

When no confusion is likely, we will drop the acute (K) and grave (J) accent superscripts that we have used to distinguish operators in the electron spin or nuclear spin spaces from their larger-dimension representations in the uncoupled product space. For example, the factor I · S in the ground-state Hamiltonian of (2.10) can be interpreted as the [I][S] × [I][S] matrix I K · S J = I K x ⊗ S J x + I K y ⊗ S J y + I K z ⊗ S J z = I x ⊗ S x + I y ⊗ S y + I z ⊗ S z.

3.1.2 Angular Momentum Matrices

When appended to the program ending with Code 2.1 on page 23, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the various spin matrices we discussed above.

Code 3.1 %operators in their own space sIp=diag(sqrt((1:2*I).*(2*I:-1:1)),1); sIj(:,:,1)=(sIp+sIp')/2; sIj(:,:,2)=(sIp-sIp')/(2*i); sIj(:,:,3)=diag(I:-1:-I); sSp=diag(sqrt((1:2*S).*(2*S:-1:1)),1); sSj(:,:,1)=(sSp+sSp')/2; sSj(:,:,2)=(sSp-sSp')/(2*i); sSj(:,:,3)=diag(S:-1:-S); sJp=diag(sqrt((1:2*J).*(2*J:-1:1)),1); sJj(:,:,1)=(sJp+sJp')/2; sJj(:,:,2)=(sJp-sJp')/(2*i); sJj(:,:,3)=diag(J:-1:-J); %operators in uncoupled space for k=1:3 aIjg(:,:,k)=kron(sIj(:,:,k),eye(gS)); gSj(:,:,k)=kron(eye(gI),sSj(:,:,k)); aIje(:,:,k)=kron(sIj(:,:,k),eye(gJ)); gJj(:,:,k)=kron(eye(gI),sJj(:,:,k)); end

Expanded "grave" and "acute" versions of the matrices for the full Schrödinger space of the atom are denoted with an initial letter g or a. Angular momentum operators in their own ("self") space, for example, the space spanned by the simultaneous eigenvectors of I · I and I z , are denoted with an initial letter. The nuclear spin matrices, a I j e and a I j g, will be identical if J D S, as in D1 pumping, but they will have different dimensions if J ≠ S, as in D2 pumping.

## 3.2 Energy States

It is often most convenient to calculate various optical pumping and resonance phenomena with energy basis states rather than with the uncoupled basis states jm m i of (3.2). We assume that the ground-state Hamiltonian of (2.10) is independent of time, and that the magnetic field is directed along the z axis of a Cartesian coordinate system. The time-independent Schrödinger equation,

H g g |μ i = E μ |μ i, (3.14)

gives the eigenvalues, E μ , the shift of the energy of the sublevels |μ i from the mean ground-state energy W g g . We can label the ground-state sublevels with an integer, μ D 1, 2, ..., g g g , where the number of ground-state sublevels is

g g g D [I][S]. (3.15)

Any well-defined ordering of the basis states |μ i is acceptable. The energies, E μ , are often used for ordering, with |μ i D |1 i having the highest energy, E 1 , with |μ i D |2 i having the next-highest energy, E 2 , and so on. When there are energy degeneracies, for example, when the magnetic field is exactly zero, one can list the degenerate states in the same, decreasing, order as their azimuthal quantum numbers, m, along some direction. Similar ordering schemes can be chosen for "accidental" level crossings at specific, nonzero values of the magnetic fields. These can occur for the hyperfine sublevels of atoms in the 2 P 3/2 excited states of alkali-metal atoms, and in appropriate electronic states of other atoms.

Expanding |μ i on the uncoupled basis vectors (3.2), we find

|μ i = Σ |jm m i hm m|μ i. (3.16)

S I m S m I

We will write the expansion coefficients as the elements of an [I][S] × [I][S] unitary matrix, U g g ,

hm m|U g g |μ i D hm m|μ i. (3.17)

S I S I

Explicitly, the eigenvalue equation (3.14) is

Σ hm|H g g |jm' m' ihm' m'|U g g |μ i D hm|U g g |μ i E μ . (3.18)

m' m'

The energy basis vectors |μ N i and energy splittings E N μ N for the excited-state Hamiltonians H e g of (2.12) can be found in like manner,

H e g |μ N i D E μ N |μ N i. (3.19)

We can label the excited-state sublevels with an integer, μ N D 1, 2, ..., g f e g , where the number of excited-state sublevels is

g f e g D [I][J]. (3.20)

The unitary transformation from the uncoupled basis to the energy basis, analogous to (3.17), is

|μ i = Σ |jm m i hm m|U g g |μ i. (3.21)

S I m S m I

Explicitly, the eigenvalue equation (3.14) is

Σ hm|H g g |jm' m' ihm' m'|U g g |μ i D hm|U g g |μ i E μ . (3.22)

m' m'

The energy basis vectors |μ N i and energy splittings E N μ N for the excited-state Hamiltonians H e g of (2.12) can be found in like manner,

H e g |μ N i D E μ N |μ N i. (3.23)

We can label the excited-state sublevels with an integer, μ N D 1, 2, ..., g f e g , where the number of excited-state sublevels is

g f e g D [I][J]. (3.24)

The unitary transformation from the uncoupled basis to the energy basis, analogous to (3.17), is

|μ N i = Σ |jm J i hm J|U f e g |μ N i. (3.25)

m J

Explicitly, the eigenvalue equation (2.12) for the excited-state sublevels is

Σ hm J|H f e g |jm' J' ihm' J'|U f e g |μ N i D hm J|U f e g |μ N i E μ N . (3.26)

m' J' ), is hm m jμfegjμNi D hm m jμNi. (3.21)

For high intensities of pumping light, where the amplitudes of the excited-state density matrix are comparable to those of the ground state, it is often convenient to make no distinction between the labels of excited-state and ground-state sublevels and to write both as jμi, with μ D 1, 2, . . . , gfeg for excited-state sublevels and μ D gfeg C 1, gfeg C 2, . . . , gfeg C gfgg, for ground-state sublevels.

Unless we explicitly state otherwise – for example, for the simple example in Chapter 1 – the energy basis will be used in our discussions of optical pumping. The wave function jψi of an individual atom will be characterized by its projections hμjψi and hμNjψi on the energy basis states jμi of (3.14) and jμNi of (3.19). A ground-state observable Z will be characterized by the elements of the [I][S] ⊗ [I][S] matrix Zfcg with elements Zμ f ν cg D hμjZjνi, where jμi and jνi are solutions of (3.14). When we need to avoid ambiguity, we use the superscript fcg (for “coupled”) to denote a matrix in the energy basis. It will often be convenient to first evaluate the matrix Zfug D hm m jZjm0m0i that represents Z in the uncoupled basis (3.1). The matrix Zfcg is related to the matrix Zfug by Zfcg D Ufgg† Zfug Ufgg, (3.22)

where the unitary matrix Ufgg was given by (3.17). The energy-basis versions of excited-state operators can be defined in like manner. In subsequent discussions, we will drop complicating superscripts or subscripts from symbols denoting matrices whenever the context makes the interpretation clear. For example, three possible interpretations of S are: 1. an [S] ⊗ [S] matrix S operating on the vectors jm i.

2. an [I][S] ⊗ [I][S] matrix SJ D 1fIg ⊗ S operating on the vectors jm m i.

3. an [I][S] ⊗ [I][S] matrix Ufgg† SJ Ufgg, operating on the vectors jμi.

When appended to the program ending with Code 3.1 on page 27, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the energies Eμ and EμN of (3.14) and (3.19) along with the unitary operators Ufgg and Ufeg of (3.17) and (3.21).

30 3 WaveFunctionsandSchrödingerSpace Code 3.2 for k=1:3;%uncoupled magnetic moment operators umug(:,:,k)=-LgS*muB*gSj(:,:,k)+(muI/(I+eps))*aIjg(:,:,k); umue(:,:,k)=-LgJ*muB*gJj(:,:,k)+(muI/(I+eps))*aIje(:,:,k); end uIS=matdot(aIjg,gSj);%uncoupled I.S uIJ=matdot(aIje,gJj);%uncoupled I.J B=input('Static magnetic field in Gauss, B = '); uHg=Ag*uIS-umug(:,:,3)*B;%uncoupled Hamiltonian [Ug,Eg]=eig(uHg);%unsorted eigenvectors and energies [x,n]=sort(-diag(Eg));%sort energies in descending order Hg=Eg(n,n); Eg=diag(Hg); Ug=Ug(:,n);%sorted Hg, Eg and Ug uHe=Ae*uIJ - umue(:,:,3)*B;%Hamiltonian without quadrupole interaction if J>1/2&I>1/2 uHe = uHe+Be*(3*uIJ^2+1.5*uIJ-I*(I+1)*J*(J+1)*eye(ge))...

/(2*I*(2*I-1)*J*(2*J-1));%add quadrupole interaction end [Ue,Ee]=eig(uHe);%unsorted eigenvectors and energies [x,n]=sort(-diag(Ee));%sort energies in descending order He=Ee(n,n); Ee=diag(He); Ue=Ue(:,n);%sorted He, Ee, and Ue The following simple subroutine was used in Code 3.2 to evaluate the dot product of vectors, the elements of which are rectangular matrices, with rows and columns given by the first two indices, and with components along the Cartesian axes x, y, and z specified with the third index.

Code 3.3 function C=matdot(A,B)

C(:,:)=A(:,:,1)*B(:,:,1)+A(:,:,2)*B(:,:,2)+A(:,:,3)*B(:,:,3);

## 3.3 Zero-Field States

In the limit that the externally applied magnetic field vanishes, the spin Hamiltonians H D Hfgg of (2.10) or H D Hfeg of (2.12) must commute with each Cartesian component of the total angular momentum operator F D I C J , (3.23)

and also with its square F·F D I(I C 1) C 2I · J C J(J C 1). (3.24)

For the rest of this section, we will denote the total electronic angular momentum operator by J for either the ground state or the excited state of the atom. At zero magnetic field, the energy states of the atom can be chosen to be the angular momentum eigenstates, jfmi D Cfm jm i ⊗ jm i. (3.25)

Here Cfm is a Clebsch–Gordan coefficient (see Chapter 8 in [15]). A MATLAB subroutine to evaluate Clebsch–Gordan coefficients Ccγabαβ, based on Vander Waerden’s formula (see (3) in Section 8.2.1 in [15]) is Code 3.4 function f=cg(a,al,b,be,c,ga)

if al+be-ga ~= 0|c<abs(a-b)|c>a+b f=0; else w=prod(1:a+al)*prod(1:a-al)*prod(1:b+be)*prod(1:b-be)*...

prod(1:c+ga)*prod(1:c-ga)*(2*c+1); w=sqrt(w); z1=max([0 b-c-al a+be-c]); %greatest lower bound of z z2=min([a+b-c a-al b+be]); %least upper bound of z f=0; for z=z1:z2; f=f+(-1)^z/(prod(1:z)*prod(1:a+b-c-z)*prod(1:a-al-z)*...

prod(1:b+be-z)*prod(1:c-b+al+z)*prod(1:c-a-be+z)); end f=del(a,b,c)*w*f; end Code 3.4 makes use of the Δ symbol (see (1) in Section 8.2.1 in [15]), Δ(a,b,c) D . (3.26)

AsimpleMATLABfunction,del(a,b,c),toevaluateΔis Code 3.5 function y=del(a,b,c)

y=sqrt(prod(1:a+b-c)*prod(1:a-b+c)*prod(1:-a+b+c)/prod(1:a+b+c+1)); The zero-field states (3.25) are simultaneously eigenvectors of F·F, F , and of the zero-field Hamiltonian H, F·FjfmiD f(f C 1)jfmi, (3.27)

F jfmiD mjfmi, (3.28)

HjfmiDλjfmi. (3.29)

32 3 WaveFunctionsandSchrödingerSpace As discussed in connection with (2.12), for an atomic level with nuclear spin quantum number I > 1/2 and t Total electronic spin quantum number J > 1/2, the zero-field energy of the hyperfine multiplet f is

$$ E_f = A \frac{C}{2} + B \frac{3C^2 + C - \frac{4}{3} I(I+1)J(J+1)}{2I(2I-1)J(2J-1)} \tag{3.30} $$

Here A and B are the magnetic-dipole and electric-quadrupole hyperfine coupling coefficients, and the coefficient C is

$$ C = f(f+1) - I(I+1) - J(J+1) \tag{3.31} $$

If I = 1/2 or J = 1/2, the term proportional to B is omitted from (3.30) since there is no contribution of the nuclear quadrupole to the energy splittings in these cases.

When appended to the program ending with Code 3.2 on page 30, the following MATLAB statements will calculate the energies of the zero-field multiplet from (3.30) and store them in the column vectors Eef and Egf for the excited state and the ground state. They will be ordered with decreasing values of the quantum number f, which will also be in order of decreasing energies if Afeg > 0 and Afgg > 0, and if |Bfeg| is not large enough to change the order (large to small) the zero-field energy states would have if Bfeg were zero. This is true for all of the stable alkali-metal atoms.

Code 3.6 ```matlab f=(I+S:-1:abs(I-S))'; C=(f.*(f+1)-I*(I+1)-S*(S+1))/2; Egf=Ag*C;% ground state f=(I+J:-1:abs(I-J))'; C=(f.*(f+1)-I*(I+1)-J*(J+1))/2; Eef=Ae*C;% excited state if I>.5 & J>.5 Eef=Eef+Be*(3*C.^2+1.5*C-I*(I+1)*J*(J+1))/(2*I*(2*I-1)*J*(2*J-1)); end ```

Low-Field Labels. We will often denote the energy states |μ⟩ with the same symbol, |fm⟩, as the zero-field states of (3.25) when the Zeeman interactions with the externally applied magnetic field B are much smaller than the internal hyperfine interactions. Then (3.27) remains approximately valid, so f is almost, but not quite, the total angular momentum quantum number. The azimuthal quantum number m is always exact if the z axis is chosen to point along the magnetic field. Finally, (3.29) remains valid if we make the replacement E_f → E_fm, where E_fm is the true energy eigenvalue of H, given by (3.14) or (3.19) and not the zero-field limit (3.30).

**4 Density Matrix and Liouville Space**

The number densities of optically pumped atoms are normally very large, typically 10^11 cm^{-3} or greater, so the observable quantities will be averaged over many atoms. As reviewed by Fano [40], one of the most convenient ways to describe spin-polarized atoms under such conditions is with the density matrix, ρ. The atoms will be subject to many interactions, for example, the absorption and emission of light, collisions with buffer gas atoms such as He or N_2, collisions with each other, and collisions with the walls. In spite of the many interactions, we assume that the n-th atom can be described by a single-atom wave function |ψ_n⟩ = |ψ_n(t)⟩. Let X be a single-atom observable. For example, we might have X = S_z, where S_z is the longitudinal electron spin operator for ground-state atoms. The mean value of X for the ΔN atoms in the small volume ΔV is

⟨X⟩ = (1/ΔN) Σ_n ⟨ψ_n|X|ψ_n⟩. (4.1)

The wave function of the n-th atom in ΔV is |ψ_n⟩. Because the number density of the atoms is so large, ΔN will be a very large number, even for quite small volume elements ΔV.

We see that we can write (4.1) as

⟨X⟩ = Tr[ρX]. (4.2)

The trace in (4.2) extends over the g = gfeg + gfgg basis states of the atom. The g×g density matrix ρ = ρ(r,t) is

ρ = (1/ΔN) Σ_n |ψ_n⟩⟨ψ_n|. (4.3)

From the definition (4.3), we see that the density matrix is Hermitian:

ρ† = ρ. (4.4)

Consequently, ρ will have g real eigenvalues, λ, and corresponding eigenvectors |λ⟩, defined by

ρ|λ⟩ = λ|λ⟩. (4.5)

The density matrix can therefore be written as

ρ = Σ_λ λ |λ⟩⟨λ|. (4.6)

The eigenvectors |λ⟩ are not necessarily the same as the energy eigenvectors (3.16) of the atom but they can be chosen to be basis vectors for the individual atomic wave functions |ψ_n⟩.

The eigenvalues λ are also non-negative since

λ = ⟨λ|ρ|λ⟩ = (1/ΔN) Σ_n ⟨λ|ψ_n⟩⟨ψ_n|λ⟩ = (1/ΔN) Σ_n |⟨λ|ψ_n⟩|^2 ≥ 0. (4.7)

Summing (4.7) over λ, noting that Σ_λ ⟨ψ_n|λ⟩⟨λ|ψ_n⟩ = 1 by completeness, and that 1 = ΔN/ΔN, we see that the eigenvalues λ sum to unity, so the density matrix has unit trace:

Tr[ρ] = Σ_λ λ = 1. (4.8)

The eigenvalues λ must therefore satisfy the constraint

0 ≤ λ ≤ 1. (4.9)

We can think of λ as the probability that the eigenstate |λ⟩ of the density matrix will be occupied. Normally, both λ and |λ⟩ will be time-dependent.

Populations. Let |μ⟩ be an energy eigenstate, for example, one of those defined by (3.14). Taking the matrix elements of (4.6), we readily see that ρ_μμ = ⟨μ|ρ|μ⟩ is a real number, bounded by

0 ≤ ρ_μμ ≤ 1. (4.10)

We interpret ρ_μμ as the probability that an atom, selected at random from an ensemble, will have energy E_μ.

Coherences. The matrix element, ⟨μ|ρ|ν⟩ = ρ_μν, between two different energy states |μ⟩ and |ν⟩ is called the coherence between the states. The coherence can be a complex number, but since ρ is Hermitian, we must have

ρ_μν = ρ_νμ*. (4.11)

Coherences are often produced by resonant radio-frequency, microwave, or optical fields. Oscillating parts of atomic observables, for example, precessing transverse spins, are proportional to coherences. Large, time-independent coherences can be produced near "level crossings", where the variation of some parameter of 哈密顿量导致两个或更多子能级具有相同的能量。

## 4.1 纯度与熵

根据Fano，我们将密度矩阵的纯度定义为 ⟨ρ⟩ = Tr(ρ²) = Σ λₙ²。 (4.12)

对于最大纯度的密度矩阵，式(4.5)的某个本征矢 |λₙ⟩，例如 |λ₁⟩ = |ψ⟩，其本征值 λ₁ = 1，而所有其他本征值为零。对于纯态， ρ = |ψ⟩⟨ψ|，ρ² = ρ，且 ⟨ρ⟩ = 1。 (4.13)

如果系综(4.3)定义的密度矩阵ρ是一个纯态，那么单个原子波函数必须是 |ψₙ⟩ = e^(iφₙ) |ψ⟩，其中 φₙ 是一个实数相位角，对应于第n个原子。

纯度在非偏振态时达到其最小值， ρ = (1/g) Σₙ |λₙ⟩⟨λₙ|，且 ⟨ρ⟩ = 1/g。 (4.14)

因此纯度的界限为 1/g ≤ ⟨ρ⟩ ≤ 1。 (4.15)

每个原子的熵为 S = -Tr(ρ ln ρ) = -Σₙ λₙ ln λₙ。 (4.16)

具有 ⟨ρ⟩ = 1 的最大纯度密度矩阵的熵为零，S = 0。对于具有最小纯度的状态，即式(4.14)的非偏振态，其中 ⟨ρ⟩ = 1/g，我们有最大可能的熵，S = ln g。

## 4.2 基态、激发态与光相干

对于光泵浦实验，将密度矩阵写成如下分块形式通常是足够的： ρ = [ ρ_ee  ρ_eg ]

[ ρ_ge  ρ_gg ]。 (4.17)

基态密度矩阵 ρ_gg 可以用式(3.14)的能量本征态写为 ρ_gg = Σ_{μν} |μ⟩ ρ_{μν}^{gg} ⟨ν|。 (4.18)

激发态分量 ρ_ee，它是由共振光对基态原子的光学激发产生的，可以用式(3.19)的能量本征态写为 ρ_ee = Σ_{μ_N ν_N} |μ_N⟩ ρ_{μ_N ν_N}^{ee} ⟨ν_N|。 (4.19)

光相干项 ρ_eg 和 ρ_ge 通常与光的电场振幅成正比。它们可以写为 ρ_eg = Σ_{μ_N ν} |μ_N⟩ ρ_{μ_N ν}^{eg} ⟨ν| 和 ρ_ge = Σ_{μ ν_N} |μ⟩ ρ_{μ ν_N}^{ge} ⟨ν_N|。 (4.20)

当碰撞将大量原子转移到未被光泵浦耦合的态时，必须向式(4.17)添加密度矩阵的额外分量。一个例子是，当碱金属原子被光泵浦到2P_{3/2}态时，对2P_{1/2}态的碰撞布居。为了分析冷却和囚禁实验，描述原子质心动量和光束中光子数的额外量子数也可能需要。

## 4.3 列向量与行向量变换

在许多情况下，将密度矩阵表示为 g² × 1 列矢量 |ρ) 在Liouville空间中，而不是 g × g 矩阵 ρ 在Schrödinger空间中，更为方便。Liouville空间有几个重要的优点： * 它比Schrödinger空间更易于适应现代计算机软件程序，如MATLAB和Mathematica。

* 泵浦和弛豫过程在Liouville空间中比在Schrōdinger空间中更容易描述。

* 描述Liouville空间中演化的“超算子”具有重要的对称性和恒等式，这些对于验证计算机代码很有用。

我们将式(4.17)的密度矩阵写为Liouville空间的列矢量： |ρ) = [ |ρ_ee) ]

[ |ρ_eg) ]

[ |ρ_ge) ]

[ |ρ_gg) ]。 (4.21)

4.3.1 列向量变换

列矢量(4.21)的每个元素 |ρ_mn) 本身也是一个列矢量，我们通过将相应的Schrödinger空间矩形矩阵 ρ_mn（有 g_m 行和 g_n 列）的每一列依次放在其左侧列的下方来构建它，如(1.2)和(1.3)所示。为了简化，我们省略上标并令 ρ_mn → ρ，可以写为 |ρ) = Σ_{μν} |μν⟩⟨μν|ρ) = Σ_k |k⟩⟨k|ρ)。 (4.22)

如(4.22)所示，Schrödinger空间的基算符 |μ⟩⟨ν| 和 Liouville空间的基矢量 |μν⟩ = |k⟩ 之间存在一一对应关系——并且厄米共轭也存在类似的对应关系： |μ⟩⟨ν| ↔ |μν⟩ = ||μ⟩⟨ν|| = |k⟩ 且 (|μ⟩⟨ν|)† ↔ ⟨μν| = ⟨k|。 (4.23)

我们称 μ 为左标签，ν 为右标签。有时用单个索引 k 表示 μν（如(4.22)）会更方便。一些简单的(4.23)的例子在讨论(1.5)时已经说明。

密度矩阵的Liouville空间和Schrödinger空间振幅由下式给出： ρ_{μν} = Tr[(|μ⟩⟨ν|)† ρ] = ⟨μν|ρ) = ⟨k|ρ)。 (4.24)

如在(1.16)中所讨论的，我们使用相同的列矢量变换(4.22)将任意的Schrödinger空间矩阵X（而不仅仅是密度矩阵）转换为列矢量： |X) = Σ_{μν} |μν⟩⟨μν|X)。 (4.25)

可以很容易地验证 ⟨μν|X†) = ⟨νμ|X)*。 (4.26)

4.3.2 行向量变换

我们定义行矢量 ⟨X| 作为列矢量 |X) 的厄米共轭。 ⟨X| = |X)† 的元素由下式给出： ⟨X|μν⟩ = ⟨μν|X)*。 (4.27)

行矢量 ⟨X| 的元素通过将 X† 的每一行依次放在其上方行的右侧来找到。一些简单的行矢量变换已在(1.17)中展示。

单位算符的列向量和行向量变换经常遇到。对于原子的完整Liouville空间（基态加激发态加光相干项），我们有 [  1_ee  ]

[  1_eg  ]

[  1_ge  ]

[  1_gg  ]

4.28 \(|1\rangle = \begin{pmatrix} |1\text{feg}\rangle \\ |0\text{feg}\rangle \\ |0\text{egg}\rangle \\ |1\text{egg}\rangle \end{pmatrix}\).

5. (4.28)

The elements of the four column vectors that make up \(|1\rangle\) are \(\langle \mu N \nu N | 1\text{feg} \rangle = \delta_{\mu N \nu N},\)

\(\langle \mu \nu N | 0\text{feg} \rangle = 0,\)

\(\langle \mu N \nu | 0\text{egg} \rangle = 0,\)

and \(\langle \mu \nu | 1\text{egg} \rangle = \delta_{\mu \nu}.\) (4.29)

### 4.3.3 Expectation Values We will denote the expectation value of an atomic observable \(X\) by \(\langle X \rangle = \text{Tr}[X \rho] = \langle 1 | X \rangle_{\text{feg}} | \rho \rangle_{\text{feg}}.\) (4.30)

Note in particular the identity \(\langle 1 \rangle = \text{Tr}[\rho] = \langle 1 | \rho \rangle_{\text{feg}} = 1.\) (4.31)

Here the row vector \(\langle 1 |\) is the Hermitian conjugate of the column vector \(|1\rangle\) of (4.28). The probabilities \(h N\text{feg} i\) and \(h N\text{egg} i\) for finding the atom in the excited state or the ground state are \(h N\text{feg} i = \langle N\text{feg} | \rho \rangle_{\text{feg}}\) and \(h N\text{egg} i = \langle N\text{egg} | \rho \rangle_{\text{feg}},\) (4.32)

where \(|N\text{feg}\rangle = \begin{pmatrix} |1\text{feg}\rangle \\ |0\text{feg}\rangle \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}\) and \(|N\text{egg}\rangle = |1\rangle \langle 1| N\text{feg} \rangle = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ |0\text{egg}\rangle \\ |1\text{egg}\rangle \end{pmatrix}.\) (4.33)

When appended to the program ending with Code 3.6 on page 32, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the excited-state and ground-state projection operators, \(1\text{feg}\) and \(1\text{egg}\), denoted by Pe and Pg, the column vectors \(|1\text{feg}\rangle\), \(|1\text{egg}\rangle\), and \(|1\rangle\), denoted by cPe, cPg, and cP, and the row vectors \(\langle 1\text{feg}|\), \(\langle 1\text{egg}|\), and \(\langle 1|\), denoted by rPe, rPg, and rP. Also evaluated are the column vectors \(|N\text{feg}\rangle\), denoted by cNe, and \(|N\text{egg}\rangle\), denoted by cNg, of (4.33) and their row-vector conjugates, denoted by rNe and rNg. For use in examining or plotting computational results, we have also included LrNe and LcNg, logical variables for the populations of excited-state and ground-state sublevels.

**Code 4.1** ``` Pg = eye(gg); Pe = eye(ge); % projection operators cPe = Pe(:); rPe = cPe'; cPg = Pg(:); rPg = cPg'; gt = (gg + ge)^2; % dimension of full Liouville space cNe = [cPe; zeros(gt - ge*ge, 1)]; rNe = cNe'; LrNe = logical(rNe); cNg = [zeros(gt - gg*gg, 1); cPg]; rNg = cNg'; LrNg = logical(rNg); cP = cNe + cNg; rP = rNe + rNg; ```

### 4.4 Superoperators Operations on the density matrix \(|\rho\rangle\) of Liouville space are described by \(g^2 \times g^2\)

"superoperators" [6]. In this section we will discuss some of the most important properties of superoperators, and how to generate a superoperator for Liouville space from the ordinary operators of Schrödinger space.

#### 4.4.1 Transposition Matrix As a first, simple example of a superoperator, we discuss the transposition matrix \(T\). Recall that the density matrix \(\rho\) must be Hermitian at all times. To discuss the implications of the fact that \(\rho = \rho^\dagger\), it is convenient to introduce a \(g^2 \times g^2\) transposition matrix, \(T\), defined by \(T |\mu \nu\rangle = |\nu \mu\rangle.\) (4.34)

From inspection of (4.34) we see that the elements of \(T\) are either 0 or 1, so \(T\) is real. Also \(T\) is symmetric, so it is Hermitian. Finally, \(T\) is clearly its own inverse. In summary, the simple symmetries of \(T\) are \(T = T^*, T = T^\dagger, \text{ and } T^2 = 1.\) (4.35)

A simple example of a transposition matrix was shown in (1.6). When appended to a program ending with Code 4.1 on page 38, the following MATLAB statements will generate column vectors mu and nu of the left and right labels for the basis states \(|\mu \nu\rangle\) for ground-state atoms with gg sublevels, and they will also evaluate the transposition operator \(T\) of (4.34).

**Code 4.2** ``` mu = (1:gg)' * ones(1, gg); mu = mu(:); nu = ones(gg, 1) * (1:gg); nu = nu(:); T = zeros(gg*gg, gg*gg); for j = 1:gg*gg T(:,j) = mu(j) == nu & nu(j) == mu; end ``` The column-vector transforms of a Hermitian-conjugate pair of Schrödinger-space operators, \(X\) and \(X^\dagger\), satisfy the identity \(|X^\dagger\rangle = T |X^*\rangle, \text{ or } T |X^\dagger\rangle = |X^*\rangle.\) (4.36)

Here \(|X^*\rangle = |X\rangle^*\), the column-vector transform of \(X^*\), denotes a column vector with elements that are the complex conjugate of the elements of \(|X\rangle\). Hermitian operators like the density matrix, \(\rho = \rho^\dagger\), therefore satisfy the identity \(|\rho\rangle = T |\rho^*\rangle, \text{ or } |\rho^*\rangle = T |\rho\rangle.\) (4.37)

#### 4.4.2 Evolution Matrices As discussed in connection with (1.10), optical pumping and atomic collisions usually cause \(|\rho\rangle\) to evolve in time as described by \(|\rho_P\rangle = G |\rho\rangle.\) (4.38)

We can use (4.36) and (4.37) to write the Hermitian conjugate of (4.38) as \(|\rho_P^\dagger\rangle = T |\rho_P^*\rangle = T G^* |\rho^*\rangle = T G^* T |\rho\rangle = T G^* T |\rho\rangle.\) (4.39)

As \(|\rho\rangle\) evolves in time according to (4.38), it must remain Hermitian, that is, we must have \(|\rho_P\rangle = |\rho_P^\dagger\rangle.\) (4.40)

From inspection of (4.38) and (4.39) we see that for (4.40) to be true we must have \(T G^* T = G\). We define the Liouvillian conjugate \(G^\ddagger\) of a Liouville-space matrix \(G\) by \(G^\ddagger = T G^* T.\) (4.41)

Then (4.40) will be valid for all \(|\rho\rangle\) if \(G\) is "Liouvillian", that is, \(G = G^\ddagger.\) (4.42)

The constraint that the Hamiltonian be Hermitian, \(H = H^\dagger\), ensures that the wave function \(|\psi\rangle\) of a single atom remains normalized in Schrödinger space. The constraint that the evolution matrix be Liouvillian, \(G = G^\ddagger\), ensures that \(|\rho\rangle\) remains Hermitian as it evolves according to (4.38). The matrix elements of (4.41) are \(\langle \mu \nu | G^\ddagger | \mu_0 \nu_0 \rangle = \langle \nu \mu | G | \nu_0 \mu_0 \rangle^*.\) (4.43)

From (4.43) we see that if \(G = G^\ddagger\), \(\langle \mu \mu | G | \nu \nu \rangle = \langle \mu \mu | G | \nu \nu \rangle^*.\) (4.44)

The elements of a Liouvillian matrix connecting populations are real.

Besides being Liouvillian, \(G\) must satisfy additional constraints. For example, to ensure that the populations of any sublevel \(|\mu\rangle\) remain bounded between 0 and 1 as the atom evolves according to (4.38) we must have \(\langle \mu \mu | G | \mu \mu \rangle \ge 0,\) (4.45)

and \(\langle \mu \mu | G | \nu \nu \rangle \ge 0, \text{ if } \mu \neq \nu.\) (4.46)

#### 4.5 Eigendecomposition sition of G 41 Differentiating Tr[(ρ)] D ⟨1|ρ⟩ D 1 with respect to time, where |1⟩ was given by (4.28), and using (4.38) for ⟨ρP⟩, we find ⟨1|G|ρ⟩ D 0. Since this must be true for any |ρ⟩, we must have ⟨1|G D 0. (4.47)

The constraint (4.47) ensures that as |ρ⟩ evolves according to (4.38), we will always have Tr[(ρ)] D 1.

For evolution governed by (4.38), the rate of change of the purity (4.12) is given by h(ρ)i D ⟨ρ|ρP⟩ + ⟨ρP|ρ⟩ D ⟨ρ|(G† + G)|ρ⟩. (4.48)

dt The purity will be conserved if G† D (−)G, that is, if G is anti-Hermitian.

## 4.5 Eigendecomposition of G

The matrix G of (4.38) will have eigenvalues γ, right eigenvectors |γ⟩, and left eigenvectors ⟨γ|, which are given by G|γ⟩ D γ|γ⟩ ⟷ |γ⟩ ⟶ |Xγ⟩ and ⟨γ|G D ⟨γ|γ. (4.49)

Here we are using the (possibly complex) eigenvalue γ as a label of the eigenvector |γ⟩, which is the column-vector transform of the Schrödinger-space matrix Xγ, that is, |γ⟩ ⟶ |Xγ⟩.

Multiplying (4.49) on the left by ⟨1|, where |1⟩ was given by (4.28), and using (4.47), we find that γ⟨1|γ⟩ D 0. (4.50)

From (4.50) we see that the eigenvectors |γ⟩ with γ ¤ 0 must have ⟨1|γ⟩ D Tr[Xγ] D 0. Eigenvectors with γ ¤ 0 have no net population and they do not affect the normalization of the density matrix as they relax.

If G represents optical pumping or other processes like spin exchange that can polarize the atoms, the left eigenvectors are not the same as the Hermitian conjugates of the right eigenvectors; that is, in general we will find that ⟨γ| ¤ ⟨γ|†. (4.51)

With the exception of unusual special cases corresponding to "critical damping", the eigenvectors of G form a complete set and can be normalized such that ⟨γ|γ0⟩ D δγγ0 . (4.52)

In such cases we can write the damping operator as G D γ|γ⟩⟨γ|. (4.53)

42 4 Density Matrix and Liouville Space From inspection of the transient solution (1.33), we see that for the density matrix to remain finite with increasing time we must have Reγ (cid:4) 0. (4.54)

Here Reγ is the real part of the complex eigenvalue γ.

If we take the complex conjugate of (4.49), and note that G‡ D G, we find G(ρ)(ρ) D γ(ρ)(ρ), or TGT|γ(ρ)⟩ D γ(ρ)|γ(ρ)⟩, or GT|γ(ρ)⟩ D γ(ρ)T|γ(ρ)⟩. (4.55)

Equation 4.55 implies that if |γ⟩ is an eigenvector, then so is |γ(ρ)⟩ DT|γ(ρ)⟩, (4.56)

which has the eigenvalue γ(ρ), that is, G|γ(ρ)⟩ D γ(ρ)|γ(ρ)⟩. (4.57)

One can readily show that the left eigenvector corresponding to (4.56) is ⟨γ(ρ)| D ⟨(γ|†T . (4.58)

Here the elements of the row vector ⟨γ(ρ)| are the complex conjugates of those of the row vector ⟨γ|. According to (4.56) and (4.36), the two Schrödinger-space matrices, Xγ and Xγ(ρ), corresponding to the two Liouville-space column vectors, |γ⟩ ⟶ |Xγ⟩ and |γ(ρ)⟩ ⟶ |Xγ(ρ)⟩, are Hermitian conjugates of each other, Xγ(ρ) D Xγ †. (4.59)

For nondegenerate, real γ, (4.56) implies that we can normalize |γ⟩ such that T|γ(ρ)⟩ D |γ⟩. (4.60)

Comparing (4.60) with (4.36), we see that for real γ, the Schrödinger-space matrix Xγ corresponding to the Liouville-space column vector |γ⟩ ⟶ |Xγ⟩, is Hermitian, Xγ D Xγ †, if Imγ D 0. (4.61)

In later parts of this book, we will encounter many concrete examples of the abstract concepts summarized in this section.

4.5.1 Nullspace For time-independent G, we will be particularly interested in eigenvalues of G with γ D 0. These correspond to steady-state solutions of the density matrix, which we denote by |X0⟩ ⟶ |ρ∞⟩, the value of the density matrix at infinite time, after all initial transients have damped away. Usually, there is a unique steady state, defined, aside from normalization, by G|X0⟩ D 0. (4.62)

In linear algebra, |X0⟩ is called the nullspace of G, and it is defined to within an overall normalization factor by (4.62). The normalization of |X0⟩ is defined by ⟨1|X0⟩ D Tr[X0] D 1, (4.63)

where |1⟩ was given by (4.28). Equation 4.63 ensures that the population probabilities of the steady-state X0 sum to unity.

4.5.2 Critical Damping For exceptional values of experimental parameters, situations analogous to critical damping of a simple oscillator occur. Then there are not enough independent eigenvectors |γ⟩ to make a complete set. This is not a very common occurrence, but we will discuss it briefly, since critical damping does not occur at all in Schrödinger space. Taking the Laplace transform of (1.10), we find (G + s)|ρ̃⟩ D |ρ0⟩, (4.64)

s where the Laplace transform of the density matrix is |ρ̃⟩ D ∫|ρ⟩e−st dt. (4.65)

s The inverse Laplace transform is |ρ⟩ D ∫|ρ̃⟩est dt. (4.66)

t 2πi C The integration contour, C, is a vertical path in the complex s plane, cutting the real axis at s D a, starting at a − i∞ and ending at a + i∞. The value of a is not important as long as a > − Re(γj) for each eigenvalue γj of G. From inspection of (1.31) we see that we can write the time evolution operator as e−Gt D ∫(G + s)−1est dt. (4.67)

2πi The matrix elements of the resolvent, (G + s)−1, the inverse of G + s, are analytic functions of s except at the singularities s D −γ where G will have poles. We can expand (G + s)−1 in partial fractions. The partial-fraction expansion is particularly simple when there is no critical damping and the right eigenvalues |γj⟩ are linearly independent. Then we find (G + s)−1 D ∑|γj⟩⟨γj| / (s + γj).

D = j\gamma_j | \gamma_j \rangle (4.68)

Substituting (4.68) into (4.67) and evaluating the contour integral by the method of residues, we find e^{-iGt} = \sum_j |\gamma_j \rangle \langle \gamma_j| e^{-i\gamma_j t}, (4.69)

in agreement with (1.33). If \(G\) has \(n\) ordinary eigenvalues \(\gamma_1, \gamma_2, ..., \gamma_n\), each with its own, linearly independent eigenvector \(|\gamma_1\rangle, |\gamma_2\rangle, ..., |\gamma_n\rangle\), and a doubly degenerate eigenvalue \(\gamma_c\) that corresponds to a single eigenvector \(|\gamma_c\rangle\), then the resolvent is R(G - zI)^{-1} = \sum_{j=1}^{n} \frac{|\gamma_j\rangle\langle\gamma_j|}{\gamma_j - z} + \frac{|\gamma_c\rangle\langle\gamma_c|}{\gamma_c - z} + \frac{|B\rangle\langle A|}{(\gamma_c - z)^2}. (4.70)

Substituting (4.70) into (4.67) and evaluating the contour integral with the method of residues, we find e^{-iGt} = \sum_{j=1}^{n} R_j e^{-i\gamma_j t} + A e^{-i\gamma_c t} + B t e^{-i\gamma_c t}. (4.71)

The sum extends over the normal (non-critical) eigenvalues, some of which may be degenerate but still have independent eigenvectors. The matrices \(R_j\), \(A\), and \(B\) can be found as limiting cases of eigenvector products \(|\gamma_j\rangle\langle\gamma_j|\) as some system parameter (e.g., the magnetic field, a collisional damping rate) approaches the value that causes critical damping.

## 4.6 Matrix Transformations from Schrödinger Space to Liouville Space

Here we discuss how the most important matrix operations in Schrödinger space are represented in Liouville space.

4.6.1 Flat and Sharp Superoperators In calculating optical pumping and spin relaxation processes, we frequently need to transform Schrödinger-space matrix products of the form \(A \otimes B\) to Liouville space. Suppose that \(\rho = [\rho_{mn}]\) is a block of the density matrix (4.17) with \(g(m)\) rows and \(g(n)\) columns, where \(m\) and \(n\) can take on the values \(e\) for the excited state or \(g\) for the ground state. Then the matrix \(A\) must have the form \(A = [A_{pm}]\) with \(g(p)\) rows and \(g(m)\) columns, and the matrix \(B\) must have the form \(B = [B_{nq}]\) with \(g(n)\) rows and \(g(q)\) columns. Then we can write \(\langle\mu|A \otimes B|\nu\rangle = \sum_{\mu'\nu'} A_{\mu\mu'} (\otimes)_{\mu'\nu'} B_{\nu'\nu} = \sum_{\mu'\nu'} A_{\mu\mu'} B^T_{\nu\nu'} (\otimes)_{\mu'\nu'}\). (4.72)

Here \(B^T\) denotes the transpose of \(B\). In (4.72) and in many subsequent expressions, we use the Einstein convention that repeated labels like \(\mu'\) and \(\nu'\) are to be summed over. The Liouville-space version of (4.72) is \((\mu\nu|A \otimes B) = (\mu\nu|B^T \otimes A|\mu'\nu')(\mu'\nu'|\rho)\). (4.73)

In ordering the Kronecker product of (4.73), we have assumed that \((\mu'\nu'|\rho)\) and \((\mu\nu|A \otimes B)\) are elements of column-vector transforms of the matrices \(\rho\) and \(A \otimes B\), analogous to (1.2) and (1.3). We can write (4.73) as the identity \(A \otimes B) = B^T \otimes A(|\rho)\). (4.74)

We will frequently encounter the special case of the identity (4.74) for which \(B = I_{[n]}\) and \(A \otimes B) = A \otimes)\). Then (4.74) becomes \(A \otimes) = A [ | \rho)\), (4.75)

where the "flat" matrix is defined by \([A]_{[mn]} = I_{[n]} \otimes A\). (4.76)

In like manner, a "sharp" matrix is defined by \(B ] | \rho) = | \rho B)\), (4.77)

where \(B ]_{[mn]} = (B^T \otimes I_{[m]})\). (4.78)

If we think of \(A \otimes B\) as three successive notes on a piano keyboard, then \(A\) is the flat note for \(\rho\) and \(B\) is the sharp note. Ernst et al. [6] call the flat and sharp matrices "left-translation superoperators" and "right-translation superoperators". From the definition (4.75) we find \(\langle\mu\nu|A [ |\mu'\nu'\rangle = A_{\mu\mu'} \delta_{\nu\nu'}\), (4.79)

and from (4.77) we find \(\langle\mu\nu|B ] |\mu'\nu'\rangle = \delta_{\mu\mu'} B_{\nu'\nu}\). (4.80)

Evidently \(A[\) is diagonal in the right indices, \(\nu\) and \(\nu'\), and \(B]\) is diagonal in the left indices, \(\mu\) and \(\mu'\).

One can readily verify that the Hermitian conjugates of flat and sharp square matrices are \([^\dagger] = A^\dagger [\) and \(]^\dagger = B^\dagger ]\). (4.81)

The Liouville conjugates are \([^\ddagger] = A^\dagger ]\) and \(]^\ddagger = B^\dagger [\). (4.82)

Hermitian conjugation "commutes" with sharp and flat transformations. Liouvilian conjugation replaces a sharp transform of \(A\) with the flat transform of \(A^\dagger\) and vice versa.

As indicated in (4.76) and (4.78), the flat matrix transformation of \(A\) depends on the number of columns of \(\rho\) – or of any other matrix in the same position as \(\rho\) in the symbol \(|A \rho)\). The sharp transformation of \(B\) depends on the number of rows of \(\rho\) or of any other matrix in the same position as \(\rho\) in the symbol \(|\rho B)\). When \(\rho\) does not have the same number of rows as columns – which can happen for optical coherences \(\rho = \rho_{eg}\) where the dimension \(g(e)\) of the excited state may differ from dimension \(g(g)\) of the ground state – we will use a dimension index, like \([n]\) in (4.76) or \([m]\) in (4.78), to indicate the dimension of the unit matrices of the Kronecker products.

4.6.2 Square Matrices Many applications of flat and sharp matrices involve only square matrices of the same dimensions in Schrödinger space. An example is the analysis of ground-state relaxation mechanisms. For such situations, the dimension superscripts are always the same and can be omitted. Evaluating (4.74) for square matrices, we readily find \([A B] = [A] [B]\). (4.83)

Flat and sharp square matrices commute. From the definitions (4.72) and (4.73) we see that for square matrices \((AB) [ = A [ B [, \text{ but } (AB) ] = A ] B ]\). (4.84)

Simple MATLAB functions to evaluate flat and sharp transforms for square matrices are

Code 4.3 function M = flat(S); s = size(S); M = kron(eye(s(1)), S);

and

Code 4.4 function M = sharp(S); s = size(S); M = kron(S', eye(s(1)));

4.6.3 Commutator Superoperators We often encounter situations where \(\dot{\rho}\), the rate of change of the density matrix \(\rho\), is proportional to a matrix like \([X, \rho] = X\rho - \rho X\). For example, we might have \(X = H\), where \(H =\)

H† is a Hermitian Hamiltonian that is the same for all atoms of the ensemble. To deal with such situations, it is convenient to introduce the transform

$\breve{X} = X\otimes\mathbf{1} - \mathbf{1}\otimes X^{\dagger}$, or $\{X\} = [X\otimes\mathbf{1}] - [\mathbf{1}\otimes X^{\dagger}]$. (4.85)

We see from (4.85) that $\{X\}$ is the transformation to Liouville space of a commutator in Schrödinger space. Ernst et al. [6] refer to $\{X\}$ for Hermitian operators $X$ as a “commutator superoperator” or “derivative superoperator”. In most cases of interest to us, $X$ and $\mathbf{1}$ will have the same, square dimensions, as indicated by the simplified notation for the flat and sharp matrices of (4.85). We use the superscript $\{ \}$ to denote the commutator transform, since the c inside the circle is the first letter in “commutator”. One can use (4.82) to show that

$\{X\}^{\dagger} = \{X^{\dagger}\}$. (4.86)

From (4.82) we find

$\{X\}^{\ddagger} = -\mathbf{1}\{X\}$. (4.87)

It is straightforward to verify two other useful identities,

$\breve{X}|1\}) = \breve{X} - X^{\dagger}$ and $(\{1|\{X\} = X^{\dagger} - \breve{X}$. (4.88)

Here $|1\})$ and $(\{1|$ are column and row vectors for populations with $(\mu\nu|1\}) = \delta_{\mu\nu}$.

4.6.4 O-Dot Superoperators

Following the coding convention of MATLAB, we define a dot–star product between two matrices A and B of the same dimensions as

$\langle\mu|A.\star B|\nu\rangle = \langle\mu|A|\nu\rangle \langle\mu|B|\nu\rangle$. (4.89)

There is no sum over the repeated indices $\mu$ and $\nu$ on the right of (4.89). For Liouville space (4.89) becomes

$|A.\star\{\mathbf{1}\}) = \breve{A}|\mathbf{1}\})$. (4.90)

As shown in (4.90), we use the o-dot superscript ($\breve{ }$) to denote the Liouville-space transform of a dot–star matrix product in Schrödinger space. O-dot matrices are always square and diagonal, with the elements

$(\mu\nu|\breve{A}|\mu'\nu'\rangle = \delta_{\mu\mu'} \delta_{\nu\nu'} \langle\mu|A|\nu\rangle$. (4.91)

No sum is implied over the repeated indices of (4.91). The diagonal elements of the matrix $\breve{A}$ are the same as the elements of the column vector, $|A\})$.

4.6.5 Commutator Superoperators

Optical Pumping of Atoms

We turn now to the heart of optical pumping physics, the interaction of light with atoms. The basic interaction, $V = \mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{E}$, is the quantum-mechanical equivalent of the classical orientation energy of the electric dipole moment, $\mathbf{d}$, of the atom in the electric field, $\mathbf{E}$, of the light. In this chapter, we briefly discuss the parts of the electric field $\mathbf{E}$ that are responsible for the absorption or emission of photons from the pumping beam. We then introduce the dimensionless vector operator $\Delta$ and its Hermitian conjugate $\Delta^{\dagger}$, such that $\Delta + \Delta^{\dagger}$ is proportional to the dipole moment $\mathbf{d}$. The dimensionless dipole operators have a number of important symmetries and they are particularly useful for analyzing optical pumping of multilevel atoms. Finally, we show how to use a generalized “rotating coordinate system” to model the interaction $V = \mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{E}$ with optical Bloch equations that are adapted to multilevel atoms.

## 5.1 The Electric Field of Light

It will be convenient to write the time dependence of the classical electric field of light at some position $\mathbf{r}$ in space as the Fourier integral

$\mathbf{E} = \int \mathbf{M}(\omega) e^{-i\omega t} d\omega = \mathbf{E}^+ + \mathbf{E}^-$. (5.1)

Here and subsequently, the limits of integration are from $-\infty$ to $\infty$ unless otherwise specified. As indicated in (5.1), we can write the classical field $\mathbf{E}$ as the sum of two components $\mathbf{E}^+$ and $\mathbf{E}^-$. The component $\mathbf{E}^+$ has only negative Fourier frequencies and can be written as

$\mathbf{E}^+(t) = \int_{-\infty}^{0} \frac{1}{2\pi} \mathbf{M}(\omega) e^{-i\omega t} d\omega = \int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{1}{2\pi} \mathbf{M}^*(\omega) e^{i\omega t} d\omega$. (5.2)

The inverse of (5.2) is

$\mathbf{M}(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \mathbf{E}(t) e^{i\omega t} dt$. (5.3)

In the signal-processing literature, the negative-frequency field $\mathbf{E}^+$ is often called the analytic field or the pre-envelope [41]. From (5.2) we see that

$\mathbf{M}(\omega) = \begin{cases} \mathbf{M}(\omega), & \text{if } \omega > 0, \\ 0, & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases}$ (5.4)

If we were to quantize the electromagnetic field, the complex field $\mathbf{E}^+$ of (5.1) would be represented by a superposition of photon-annihilation operators. Similarly, $\mathbf{E}^- \to \mathbf{E}^{\dagger}$ would be represented by a superposition of photon-creation operators.

## 5.2 The Electric Dipole Moment of Atoms

The interaction between light and atoms is given by the negative dot product, $V = \mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{E}$, of the electric dipole moment operator $\mathbf{d}$ of the atom and the optical electric field $\mathbf{E}$ of (5.1). The electric dipole operator $\mathbf{d}$ has nonzero matrix elements only between states of different parity, for example, between the $2S_{1/2}$ ground state and the $2P$ excited state of an alkali-metal atom. We therefore write the electric dipole moment operator as

$\mathbf{d} = D\Delta + D^*\Delta^{\dagger}$, (5.5)

where

$D\Delta = \sum_{m_S m_J} |S m_S\rangle \langle S m_S| \mathbf{d} |J m_J\rangle \langle J m_J| = D\Delta$. (5.6)

$D^*\Delta^{\dagger} = \sum_{m_S m_J} |J m_J\rangle \langle J m_J| \mathbf{d} |S m_S\rangle \langle S m_S| = D^*\Delta^{\dagger}$. (5.7)

The sums extend over all electronic azimuthal quantum numbers $m_S$ and $m_J$ of the ground state and excited state of the atom, which have total electronic angular momentum quantum numbers $S$ and $J$, respectively. As indicated in (5.6) and (5.7), the operators $\Delta$ and $\Delta^{\dagger}$ operate only on the electronic parts of the atomic wave functions, and they have no direct influence on the nuclear parts. We will discuss the dimensionless dipole moment operator $\Delta$, and its amplitude $D$ in the following sections.

5.2.1 Spherical Tensors

Calculations of matrix elements of the dipole operators (5.6) and (5.7) can be facilitated by writing them as spherical tensors. We will briefly review the most important properties of spherical tensors here. More information can be found in the literature [40].

Recall that an irreducible spherical tensor $T_{lm}$ of angular momentum $l$ has $2l + 1$ components that obey specific transformation properties under rotations.

elements, corresponding to each possible value of the azimuthal quantum number $m$, $-l, -l+1, \dots, l$. The elements of the tensor refer to various physical quantities that are referenced to a spatial coordinate system. The elements can be simple scalar quantities, or vectors, or matrices, or some more complicated structures. The elements of the rotated spherical tensor $R(T_{lm})$ are given by $$R(T_{lm}) = \sum_{m'} D^l_{mm'}(\alpha, \beta, \gamma) T_{lm'}. \tag{5.8}$$ The Wigner $D$-function, $D^l_{mm'} = D^l_{mm'}(\alpha, \beta, \gamma)$, can be parameterized by the three Euler angles, $\alpha$, $\beta$, and $\gamma$, that define the rotation $R$. A simple example of a spherical tensor is the set of three spherical basis vectors, $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m}$, where $$\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,1} = -\frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}} + i\hat{\mathbf{y}}}{\sqrt{2}}, \quad \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,0} = \hat{\mathbf{z}}, \quad \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,-1} = \frac{\hat{\mathbf{x}} - i\hat{\mathbf{y}}}{\sqrt{2}},$$ with $\hat{\mathbf{e}}^\dagger_{1,m} = (-1)^m \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,-m}$. \tag{5.9} Here $\hat{\mathbf{x}}$, $\hat{\mathbf{y}}$, and $\hat{\mathbf{z}}$ are unit vectors along the three Cartesian axes of a right-handed coordinate system. The spherical basis vectors $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m}$ have angular momentum quantum number $l=1$ (there are $2l+1=3$ of them), they are orthonormal and complete, that is $$\hat{\mathbf{e}}^\dagger_{1,m} \cdot \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,n} = \delta_{mn} \quad \text{and} \quad \sum_m \hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m} \hat{\mathbf{e}}^\dagger_{1,m} = \mathbf{1} = \hat{\mathbf{x}}\hat{\mathbf{x}} + \hat{\mathbf{y}}\hat{\mathbf{y}} + \hat{\mathbf{z}}\hat{\mathbf{z}}. \tag{5.10}$$ We will sometimes think of the $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m}$ as the spin basis states of the photon. Other examples of irreducible spherical tensors are the basis states $|j,m_j\rangle$ and $|J,m_J\rangle$ that occur in (5.6) and (5.7).

**5.2.2 Hermitian Conjugates**

We will often encounter expressions that contain the Hermitian conjugate $\tilde{T}^\dagger_{lm}$ of some spherical tensor $T_{lm}$. The Hermitian conjugate of (5.8) is $$R(\tilde{T}^\dagger_{lm}) = \sum_{m'} D^l_{m'm} (\tilde{T}^\dagger_{lm'}). \tag{5.11}$$ When rotated, the Hermitian conjugates of spherical tensors transform with the complex conjugates of the Wigner $D$-functions. Since the powerful recoupling identities of angular momentum algebra are valid for products of spherical tensors, all of which transform with the $D$-functions and not their complex conjugates, it is useful to introduce versions of the Hermitian conjugates that transform as (5.8) instead of as (5.11). We will denote these as $$\tilde{T}^\circ_{lm} = \tilde{T}^\dagger_{l,-m} (-1)^{l+m}. \tag{5.12}$$ Noting that $D^l_{m'm} = (-1)^{m-m'} D^l_{-m',-m}$ (see (2) in Section 4.4 in [15]), we see that the tensors $\tilde{T}^\circ_{lm}$ transform under rotations as (5.8). A simple example of (5.12) is $$\hat{\mathbf{e}}^\circ_{1,m} = \hat{\mathbf{e}}^\dagger_{1,-m} (-1)^{1+m} = -\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m}. \tag{5.13}$$ For tensors that are ket vectors, for example, $\tilde{T}_{Sm_S} = |S,m_S\rangle$, it will be convenient to denote $\tilde{T}^\circ_{Sm_S} \equiv \langle S,m_S|$, where $$\langle S,m_S| = \langle S,-m_S|(-1)^{S+m_S}. \tag{5.14}$$

**5.2.3 Addition of Angular Momentum**

In analogy to (3.25) we define a coupled irreducible tensor as $$\{\tilde{T}_{lm} \tilde{T}'_{l'm'}\}_{LM} = \sum_{m,m'} C^{LM}_{lm,l'm'} \tilde{T}_{lm} \tilde{T}'_{l'm'}. \tag{5.15}$$ Depending on the context, the product symbol $\tilde{T}_{lm} \tilde{T}'_{l'm'}$ on the right of (5.15) could represent a set of $[l][l']$ scalars, column vectors, row vectors, matrices, dyadics, or some more complicated quantities.

For example, important special cases of (5.15) are the dimensionless dipole operators of (5.6) and (5.7). Using (5.9) and (5.10), we can write $$\Delta_m = \sum_p \Delta_p (\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m})^*_p = -\sqrt{3} \{\Delta, \hat{\mathbf{e}}\}_{m0} = -\sqrt{3} \{\Delta, \hat{\mathbf{e}}^\circ\}_{m0}. \tag{5.16}$$ Here $$\Delta_m = \Delta \cdot \hat{\mathbf{e}}^*_{1,m} = \tilde{T}_{f SJg}_{1m}. \tag{5.17}$$ In writing the last two terms of (5.16), we noted that for any angular momentum quantum number $j$, $$C^{j,0}_{j,-m; jm} = (-1)^{j-m} \frac{1}{\sqrt{[j]}}. \tag{5.18}$$ We will discuss the spherical basis tensor $\tilde{T}_{f SJg}_{LM}$ of (5.17) in more detail below.

In analogy to (5.16) we write $$(\Delta^\dagger)_m = \sum_p (\Delta^\dagger)_p (\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1,m})^*_p = -\sqrt{3} \{\Delta^\dagger, \hat{\mathbf{e}}\}_{m0} = -\sqrt{3} \{\Delta^\dagger, \hat{\mathbf{e}}^\circ\}_{m0}. \tag{5.19}$$ Here $$(\Delta^\dagger)_m = \Delta^\dagger \cdot \hat{\mathbf{e}}^*_{1,m} = (-1)^{J-S} \tilde{T}_{f JSg}_{1m}. \tag{5.20}$$

**5.2.4 Spherical Basis Tensors**

A spherical basis tensor, operating between the states $|S,m_S\rangle$ on the right and the states $\langle J,m_J|$ on the left, can be defined by $$\tilde{T}_{f JSg}_{LM} = \langle jJifSjg = \sum_{m_J,m_S} |J m_J\rangle \langle S,M-m_J| C^{LM}_{Jm_J, S(M-m_J)}. \tag{5.21}$$ Following the convention of equation (2) in Section 13.1 in [15], the matrix elements of the tensor operators (5.21) are given by the Wigner–Eckart theorem: $$\langle J m_J | \tilde{T}_{f JS)g}_{LM} | S m_S \rangle = \frac{1}{\sqrt{[J]}} C^{Jm_J}_{SLM; Sm_S} \langle kJkT_{f JS)g} kS \rangle. \tag{5.22}$$ The reduced matrix elements of the spherical tensors (5.21) are $$\langle kJkT_{f JSg} kS \rangle = \sqrt{[L]}. \tag{5.23}$$ The Hermitian conjugate of a spherical basis tensor is $$\tilde{T}_{f JSg}^\dagger = (-1)^{M+J-S} \tilde{T}_{f SJg}_{L,-M}. \tag{5.24}$$ The basis tensors are orthonormal, $$\text{Tr}\left( \tilde{T}_{f JSg}_{LM}^\dagger \tilde{T}_{f J'S'g}_{L'M'} \right) = \delta_{LL'} \delta_{MM'} \delta_{JJ'} \delta_{SS'}, \tag{5.25}$$ or in Liouville space are $$\langle\!\langle \tilde{T}_{f JSg}_{LM} | \tilde{T}_{f J'S'g}_{L'M'} \rangle\!\rangle = \delta_{LL'} \delta_{MM'} \delta_{JJ'} \delta_{SS'}. \tag{5.26}$$ The elements, $\langle \mu \nu | \tilde{T}_{f J'S'g}_{L'M'} \rangle$, of the column vector $|\tilde{T}_{f J'S'g}_{L'M'}\rangle$, are given by (4.24) and the elements, $\langle \tilde{T}_{f JSg}_{LM} | \mu \nu \rangle$, of the row vector $\langle \tilde{T}_{f JSg}_{LM} |$, are given by (4.27).

5.2.6 Amplitude D We will write the amplitude D of (5.6) in terms of the oscillator strength f{ge}[42], which can be defined as

f{ge} = (2ω{ge}/3[S]) (r_e/c²) Σ ⟨Jm_j|D|Sm_s⟩·⟨Sm_s|D|Jm_j⟩. (5.43)

Here the classical electron radius is r_e, the speed of light is c, and the nominal resonance frequency, ω{ge}, of the optical transition was discussed in connection with (2.15).

The spontaneous emission rate Γ{ge} from the excited state e to the ground state g is related to the oscillator strength f{ge} by

Γ{ge} = (2[S]ω{ge}² r_e / c [J]) f{ge}. (5.44)

If there are additional lower electronic states to which the atom can decay, the rate Γ{ge} is only the partial spontaneous decay rate to state g and not the total spontaneous decay rate.

Code5.5 Sl=1e4*input(’flux in mW/c m^2, Sl = input('Intensity in W/m^2, Sl = '); % convert to erg/(s cm^2)

Dw = 2*pi*1e6*input('Detuning in MHz, Dnu = '); % colatitude and azimuthal angles of beam direction in degrees thetaD = input('thetaD = '); % beam colatitude angle in degrees phiD = input('phiD = '); % beam azimuthal angle in degrees theta = thetaD*pi/180; phi = phiD*pi/180; % angles in radians Etheta = input('Etheta = '); % relative field along theta Ephi = input('Ephi = '); % relative field along phi Ej(1) = Etheta*cos(theta)*cos(phi) - Ephi*sin(phi); Ej(2) = Etheta*cos(theta)*sin(phi) + Ephi*cos(phi); Ej(3) = -Etheta*sin(theta); % Cartesian projections tEj = sqrt(2*pi*Sl/c)*Ej/norm(Ej); % field in esu/cm^2 tV = zeros(ge, gg); for j = 1:3 % sum over three Cartesian axes tV = tV - D*Dj(:,:,j)'*tEj(j); end

The projection of the optical electric field amplitude, x EQ, along the jth Cartesian axis is denoted by tEj(j). The field amplitudes are determined by the laser power, in accordance with (5.61).

The unperturbed Hamiltonian (2.15) and the interaction (5.52) will cause the density matrix to evolve at the rate dρ/dt = (1/iℏ)[H0, ρ] + (1/iℏ)(VCV†, ρ). (5.63)

To solve (5.63), it is convenient to introduce the phase-angle operator, Φ = (ω - k·v)Qt. (5.64)

where the "optical spin operator", Q, was defined by (2.16). We will encounter more complicated phase matrices Φ in our discussion of magneto-optical traps. Using (5.64), we can write (5.63) as dρ/dt = (1/iℏ) e^{-iΦ} (H0 + VQ + VQ†) e^{iΦ}, ρ . (5.65)

The "rotation operator", e^{-iΦ}, can be written as e^{-iΦ} = e^{-i(ω - k·v)t/2} |eg><| + e^{i(ω - k·v)t/2} |gg<|. (5.66)

In deriving (5.65) from (5.63), we noted that e^{-iΦ} H0 e^{iΦ} = H0, (5.67)

e^{-iΦ} VQ e^{iΦ} = V, (5.68)

e^{-iΦ} VQ† e^{iΦ} = V†. (5.69)

Let the density matrix in the "rotating frame" be ρ_Q = e^{iΦ} ρ e^{-iΦ}. (5.70)

Differentiating (5.70) with respect to time and using (5.65), we find dρ_Q/dt = (1/iℏ) [K, ρ_Q]. (5.71)

The rotating-frame Hamiltonian, K = K0 + K1, (5.72)

has a "dark part", that is, a part that is independent of the optical electric field, K0 = H0 - ℏΦP, (5.73)

and a part that is proportional to the optical electric field, K1 = VQ + VQ†. (5.74)

Both K0 and K1 are time-independent in the intervals between collisions, which causes abrupt changes in v, r, VQ, and VQ†.

For the simple phase operator, (5.64), we find K = H_{eg} + H_{gg} - ℏ(Δω - k·v)Q = K_{eg} + K_{gg}, (5.75)

where K_{eg} = H_{eg} - ℏ(Δω - k·v) and K_{gg} = H_{gg} + ℏ(Δω - k·v). (5.76)

The nominal detuning is Δω = ω - ω_{egg}. (5.77)

The nominal optical resonance frequency ω_{egg} was discussed in connection with (2.15).

## 5.6 Net Evolution

Writing out the components of (5.71), and adding in the effects (5.46) and (5.47) of spontaneous emission, we find iℏ dρ_{eeg}/dt = E_{eeg} ρ_{eeg} + VQ ρ_{geg} - ρ_{eeg} VQ†, (5.78)

iℏ dρ_{geg}/dt = VQ† ρ_{eeg} + E_{geg} ρ_{geg} - ρ_{geg} VQ†, (5.79)

iℏ dρ_{egg}/dt = -ρ_{eeg} VQ + E_{egg} ρ_{egg} + VQ ρ_{ggg}, (5.80)

iℏ dρ_{ggg}/dt = iℏ P_{ggg} - ρ_{geg} VQ + VQ† ρ_{egg} + E_{ggg} ρ_{ggg}. (5.81)

The elements of the complex energy-difference matrices are E_{eeg} = K_{eeg} - iℏγ_1, (5.82)

E_{geg} = K_{geg} - iℏγ_2, (5.83)

E_{egg} = K_{egg} - iℏγ_2, (5.84)

E_{ggg} = K_{ggg}. (5.85)

The elements of the real energy-difference matrices are K_{eeg} = <μ_N|K0|μ_N> - <μ_{N'}|K0|μ_{N'}> = H_{eeg}, (5.86)

K_{geg} = <μ|K0|μ> - <μ_N|K0|μ_N> = H_{geg} + ℏ(Δω - k·v), (5.87)

K_{egg} = <μ_N|K0|μ_N> - <μ|K0|μ> = H_{egg} - ℏ(Δω - k·v), (5.88)

K_{ggg} = <μ|K0|μ> - <μ_0|K0|μ_0> = H_{ggg}. (5.89)

The resonance-frequency shifts due to hyperfine splittings and applied external fields are accounted for by the matrices g_{eg} = <μ_N|H_{feg}|μ_N> - <μ_{N'}|H_{feg}|μ_{N'}> = E_{μ_N} - E_{μ_{N'}} , (5.90)

g_{ge} = <μ|H_{fgg}|μ> - <μ_N|H_{feg}|μ_N> = E_{μ} - E_{μ_N}, (5.91)

g_{egg} = <μ_N|H_{feg}|μ_N> - <μ|H_{fgg}|μ> = E_{μ_N} - E_{μ}, (5.92)

g_{gg} = <μ|H_{fgg}|μ> - <μ_0|H_{fgg}|μ_0> = E_{μ} - E_{μ_0}. (5.93)

The matrices (5.90)–(5.93), when divided by Planck’s constant ℏ, are the residual Bohr frequencies associated with each element of the density matrix after any optical component, ˙ω, of the frequency has been removed.

For Hermitian Schrödinger-space operators like H_{feg}, H_{fgg}, K_{eg}, and K_{gg}, which are diagonal in the energy basis, and from which we can define the Schrödinger-space operators H_{ggg} of (5.90)–(5.93), or K_{ggg} of (5.86)–(5.89), we will occasionally use identities like H_{ggg} = H_{ggg} and H_{eeg} = H_{eeg}. (5.94)

These can be verified in a straightforward way from the definition (4.85) of commutator transforms and (4.91) for o-dot transforms.

The "longitudinal" and "transverse" relaxation rates of (5.82)–(5.84) are γ_1 = Γ_{geg} and γ_2 = 1/2 Γ_{geg} + Γ_c α_{egg}. (5.95)

The nominal collision rate is Γ, and we use a dimensionless, complex coefficient α_{egg} = α_{geg} - iα_{egg}'' to describe the collision efficiency. The collisional broadening is proportional to Re α_{egg} and the collisional shift in the o- Optical resonance frequency is proportional to Imαfegg. Later, we will include additional terms proportional to Γ in (5.78) and (5.81) to account for spin relaxation in the ground state and the excited state, and for quenching collisions that cause nonradiative transfer of excited atoms to the ground state. Representative values of the contributions of various buffer gases to γ are shown in Table 5.1.

|    | He  | Ne     | Ar     | Kr     | Xe     | N2     | |----|-----|--------|--------|--------|--------|--------| | Li | 15  | 12     | 18     | 24     | 27     | –      | | Na | (16,19) | (8,12) | (21,18) | (23,21) | (26,24) | –      | | K  | (13,18) | (7,10) | (21,16) | (19,19) | (24,24) | (21,21) | | Rb | (18,18) | (8,9) | (16,16) | (16,14) | (18,18) | (19,19) | | Cs | (23,19) | (8,8) | (16,16) | (16,16) | (17,18) | (16,25) |

5.6.1 The Amagat Unit of Density

In quoting experimental values of pressure-dependent quantities, we will use a practical unit of number density, the amagat. The amagat is the density of an ideal gas at a pressure of 1 atm and a temperature of 0°C. The abbreviation for an amagat is amg, and its value (the Loschmidt constant) in the cgs system of units used in this book is 1 amg = 2.6868 × 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ . (5.96)

Most experiments with buffer gases are done with sealed-off cells for a range of temperatures. The density of a nonreacting and noncondensing buffer gas is very nearly constant in such experiments, since there is little change in cell volume with temperature. However, the internal pressure of a sealed-off cell can change substantially with temperature. So it is convenient to quote experimental measurements for a specific density and temperature, rather than pressure and temperature. This is why the amagat unit is so commonly used in the optical pumping literature.

5.6.2 Normalization

With (5.78)–(5.81) one can verify that Tr[dρ/dt](1 – jρ) = 0. (5.97)

We can therefore pick the normalization of ρ such that Tr[ρ](1 – jρ) = 1. (5.98)

Code 5.6 Hee = Ee * ones(1,ge) - ones(ge,1) * Ee'; Hge = Eg * ones(1,ge) - ones(gg,1) * Ee'; Heg = Ee * ones(1,gg) - ones(ge,1) * Eg'; Hgg = Eg * ones(1,gg) - ones(gg,1) * Eg';

5.6.3 Notation and Coding

We will often write expressions like (5.88) symbolically or in computer codes as Kfegg = Hfegg – ℏ(Δω – k · v) . (5.99)

As indicated by (5.88), the expression (5.99) means that to get the elements of the matrix represented by Kfegg, one subtracts the number, ℏ(Δω – k · v), from each element of the matrix, Hfegg, and not only from the diagonal elements of Hfegg, as expressions like (5.99) sometimes mean. In fact, Hfegg can have different numbers of rows and columns, so it would not be clear what its "diagonal elements" are.

## 5.7 Optical Bloch Equations

We will call (5.78)–(5.81) together with (5.98) the "optical Bloch equations". To see the connection to the classical Bloch equation of nuclear magnetic resonance, consider the limit where there is only one sublevel of the excited state and one sublevel of the ground state. Of course this does not occur for real atoms, where one sublevel in the ground state and one in the excited state would correspond to S = 0 and J = 0, for which single-photon transitions are forbidden. Then the operator Q of (2.16) can be written as Q = S = ½ (1 0; 0 -1). (5.100)

Here S = Sz denotes the longitudinal spin operator of an effective spin-1/2 particle, for which spin-up is the excited state and spin-down is the ground state. In view of (5.98) we can write the diagonal elements of the density matrix as ⟨f e|ρ|e⟩ = ½ + ⟨Sz⟩ and ⟨f g|ρ|g⟩ = ½ - ⟨Sz⟩ . (5.101)

In the same spirit we write ⟨f g|ρ|e⟩ = ⟨Sx⟩ + i⟨Sy⟩ and ⟨f e|ρ|g⟩ = ⟨Sx⟩ - i⟨Sy⟩ . (5.102)

The coupling matrix VQ reduces to a single complex number which we write in terms of its real and imaginary parts as VQ = VQ' + iVQ'' . (5.103)

The energy-difference matrices, (5.86) and (5.89), are both zero. Finally, (5.48) implies that dP_ggg/dt = Γfgeg ρ_fee . (5.104)

s s

Using this notation, one can readily show that (5.78)–(5.81) can be summarized by the vector equation d⟨Sz⟩/dt = -γzz – γ'[xx + yy] + [Ω × ⟨S⟩]z - γ . (5.105)

1       2          12

The Larmor frequency in the rotating frame has the Cartesian components Ωx = 2VQ'/ℏ, Ωy = -2VQ''/ℏ and Ωz = -Δω + γ'₀ = ωfegg + γ'₀ - ω. (5.106)

The Rabi frequency is Ω⊥ = Ωx² + Ωy² = 2|VQ|/ℏ . (5.107)

We have ignored any Doppler shift. Equations (5.105)–(5.107) are the classic forms of the Bloch equations used to analyze magnetic resonance of a spin-1/2 particle. Spins are created at a rate γ with an initial value of ⟨Sz⟩ = -z/2. Once created, the spins rotate at the apparent Larmor rate, Ω, of the rotating coordinate system. The longitudinal spin polarization relaxes at the real rate γ. The transverse spin polarization relaxes at the rate γ'. The resonance frequency is shifted from ωfegg to ωfegg + γ'₀.

For real atoms with multiple sublevels in the ground state and excited state, it is necessary to use the generalized optical Bloch equations (5.78)–(5.81).

(5.81). There is a matrix of optical Rabi frequencies, Ω_VQ†, and the positive occupation probabilities of the upper and lower sublevels become excited-state and ground-state density matrices ρ_feg and ρ_fgg. These complications can be readily handled by modern mathematics packages for fast electronic computers.

## 5.8 Liouville Space

For efficient computer calculations, it is convenient to transform the optical Bloch equations to Liouville space. In analogy to (4.21), we define the Liouville-space density matrix as the column vector

|ρ⟩ = ⎡⎣⎢⎢⎢⎣ρ_feg  ρ_egg  ρ_geg  ρ_ggg⎤⎦⎥⎥⎥⎦. (5.108)

Each element, |ρ_fmgn⟩, of (5.108) is a column vector, assembled by placing each column of the |fmg⟩ × ⟨fng| matrix ρ_fmgn below the one to its left. The Liouville-space version of (5.78)–(5.81) is

d/dt |ρ⟩ = G |ρ⟩. (5.109)

The evolution operator is

G = ħ⁻¹⎡⎣⎢⎢⎢⎢⎢⎣iE_feg   Ω_VQ[feg]   −Ω_VQ†[feg]        0                ⎤⎦⎥⎥⎥⎥⎥⎦ Ω_VQ†[feg]   iE_geg   Ω_VQ[fge]   −Ω_VQ†[fge]

−Ω_VQ[feg]   Ω_VQ†[fge]   iE_egg   Ω_VQ[fge]

0              −Ω_VQ[fge]   Ω_VQ†[fge]   iE_ggg − iħΓ_fggc A_fggc

. (5.110)

The “o-dot” (ˇ), “flat” ([), and “sharp” (]) matrix transformations from Schrödinger to Liouville space are defined in Section 4.6. The block-0’s in (5.110) have the appropriate numbers of rows and columns to fill out the matrix.

We have assumed that collisions are slow enough that they need only be taken into account for the ground-state density matrix ρ_fgg, with the factor Γ_fggc A_fggc. We will discuss more realistic types of collisional relaxation in later sections, but for illustrative purposes we will let A_fggc represent “uniform relaxation”, an example of which we already used in (1.13), and which we generalize to

A_fggc = (1_fg/gfgg) (1_fg − ρ_fgg). (5.111)

The physical meaning of (5.111) is that the collision destroys the entire (possibly polarized) density matrix, as represented by the unit matrix

1_fgg = 1_fg [ = 1_fg] = ∑_{μν} |μν⟩(μν|, (5.112)

for the ground state of Liouville space. The term (1_fg − ρ_fgg)/gfgg replaces the polarized atoms with a completely unpolarized density matrix |1_fg⟩/gfgg.

When appended to the program ending with Code 5.6 on page 65, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the damping operator A_fggc of (5.111) and the damping operator G of (5.110) for atomic velocity v = 0.

Code 5.7 Gmc=input('Collision rate Gmc = '); Acgg=eye(gg*gg)-cPg*rPg/gg;%uniform-relaxation matrix %G0=dark, G1=pumping, G2=collisions; dGdw=dG/dw G0=zeros(gt,gt); G1=G0; G2=0;dGdw=G0; %index ranges for blocks n1=1:ge^2; n2=ge^2+1:ge^2+ge*gg; n3=ge^2+ge*gg+1:ge^2+2*ge*gg; ...

n4=ge^2+2*ge*gg+1:(ge+gg)^2; G1(n1,n2)=kron(Pe,tV)*i/hbar;%upper off diagonal elements G1(n1,n3)=-kron(conj(tV),Pe)*i/hbar; G1(n2,n4)=-kron(conj(tV),Pg)*i/hbar; G1(n3,n4)=kron(Pg,tV)*i/hbar; G1=G1-G1';%add antihermitian conjugate G2(n4,n4)=Gmc*Acgg; dGdw(n2,n2)=i*eye(ge*gg);%dG/dw dGdw(n3,n3)=-dGdw(n2,n2); G0(n1,n1)=diag(Hee(:)*i/hbar+1/te);%diagonal elements G0(n2,n2)=diag(Hge(:)*i/hbar+1/(2*te)); G0(n3,n3)=diag(Heg(:)*i/hbar+1/(2*te)); G0(n4,n4)=diag(Hgg(:)*i/hbar); G0(n4,n1)=-Asge/te;%repopulation by stimulated emission G=G0+G1+G2+Dw*dGdw;%total damping matrix

5.8.1 Transients

One can use (5.109) to calculate transients induced by intense optical pulses. For example, suppose that at time t = 0 the atoms are unpolarized and in their ground state with the density matrix

|ρ_0⟩ = ⎡⎣⎢⎢⎢⎣0_feg  0_egg  0_geg  1_fg⎤⎦⎥⎥⎥⎦. (5.113)

gfgg

The solution of (5.109) for t ≥ 0 with the initial condition (5.113) is

|ρ(t)⟩ = exp(−Gt) |ρ_0⟩. (5.114)

When appended to the program ending with Code 5.7, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate and plot the transient response described by (5.109).

Code 5.8 rt=input('relative pulse length, rt=tm/te = '); tm=rt*te; nt=101;%number of time samples th=linspace(0,tm,nt);%time samples rhot=zeros(gt,nt);%initialize density matrix rho0=[zeros(ge^2+2*ge*gg,1);cPg]/gg;%density matrix at t=0 for k=1:nt rhot(:,k)=expm(-G*th(k))*rho0; end Ne=rNe*rhot;%excited-state probability Ng=rNg*rhot;%ground-state probability clf; plot(th/te,real(Ng),'b-'); hold on; plot(th/te,real(Ne),'r-.');grid on; xlabel('Time, \Gamma_f s'); ylabel('Populations'); legend('Ng','Ne')

A typical transient calculated with Code 5.8 on page 68 is shown in Figure 5.3. Note the Rabi oscillations that occur as the atom builds up to a final steady state. The excited-state and ground-state probabilities, Ne and Ng, sum to 1.
