## 6.1 Ground-State Evolution

The part of (5.81) due to optical pumping (the terms proportional to V_Q and V_Q†) is iħ (d/dt) ρ^Q_egg = V_Q† ρ^Q_eeg W_Q - W_Q† ρ^Q_eeg V_Q = V_Q† (W_Q† ρ^Q_eeg - ρ^Q_egg W_Q†) V_Q = [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_ge [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_eg. (6.9)

The transfer of atoms from the excited state to the ground state accompanied by stimulated emission of light is iħ [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_ge = V_Q† ρ^Q_eeg W_Q - W_Q† ρ^Q_eeg V_Q. (6.10)

The transfer of atoms from the ground state to the excited state accompanied by absorption of light is given by iħ [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_gg = δH_Q_egg ρ^Q_egg - ρ^Q_egg δH_Q_egg†. (6.11)

The effective Hamiltonian, δH_egg, is iħ δH_Q_egg = V_Q† W_Q = δE_Q_egg - i δΓ_Q_egg. (6.12)

The Hermitian light-shift operator is δE_Q_egg = (δH_Q_egg + δH_Q_egg†), (6.13)

and the Hermitian light-damping operator is δΓ_Q_egg = (δH_Q_egg - δH_Q_egg†). (6.14)

From inspection of (6.14) and (6.11) we see that Tr [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_gg = -Tr[(ρ^Q_egg δΓ_Q_egg)] = -<δΓ_egg>. (6.15)

The rate of depletion of ground-state atoms by depopulation pumping is the expectation value of the light-damping operator δΓ_egg.

Liouville Space The Liouville-space version of (6.11) is d/dt |ρ^Q_egg)|_gg = -Γ_p_egg A_p_egg |ρ^Q_egg)|_gg. (6.16)

The mean pumping rate for unpolarized, ground-state atoms is Γ_p_egg = (1/g_egg) Tr(δΓ_Q_egg). (6.17)

According to (4.75) and (4.77), the dimensionless operator A_p_egg is A_p_egg = i δH_Q_egg / ħ Γ_p_egg. (6.18)

The Liouville-space version of (6.10) is d/dt |ρ^Q_egg)|_ge = Γ_p_eeg A_p_eeg |ρ^Q_eeg). (6.19)

where the mean pumping rate of unpolarized excited-state atoms can be defined in terms of the rate (6.17) for ground-state atoms by g_eeg Γ_p_eeg = g_egg Γ_p_egg. (6.20)

According to (6.10) and (4.74), the dimensionless operator A_p_eeg is defined by iħ Γ_p_eeg A_p_eeg = W_Q^T† V_Q† - V_Q^T† W_Q†. (6.21)

## 6.2 Excited-State Evolution

The part of (5.78) due to optical pumping is iħ (d/dt) ρ^Q_eeg = V_Q ρ^Q_geg - ρ^Q_egg V_Q† = V_Q (W_Q† ρ^Q_eeg - ρ^Q_egg W_Q†) - (ρ^Q_eeg W_Q - W_Q ρ^Q_egg) V_Q† = [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_ee + [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_eg. (6.22)

The part of (6.22) that describes the transfer of atoms from the ground state to the excited state accompanied by the absorption of light is iħ [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_eg = W_Q ρ^Q_egg V_Q† - V_Q ρ^Q_egg W_Q†. (6.23)

The part of (6.22) that describes the transfer of atoms from the excited state to the ground state accompanied by stimulated emission of light is iħ [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_ee = δH_Q_eeg ρ^Q_eeg - ρ^Q_eeg δH_Q_eeg†. (6.24)

The effective Hamiltonian, δH_eeg, of (6.24) is iħ δH_Q_eeg = V_Q W_Q† = δE_Q_eeg - i δΓ_Q_eeg. (6.25)

The Hermitian light-shift operator is δE_Q_eeg = (δH_Q_eeg + δH_Q_eeg†), (6.26)

and the Hermitian light-damping operator is δΓ_Q_eeg = (δH_Q_eeg - δH_Q_eeg†). (6.27)

From inspection of (6.24) and (6.27) we see that Tr [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_ee = -Tr[(ρ^Q_eeg δΓ_Q_eeg)] = -<δΓ_eeg>. (6.28)

The rate of depletion of excited-state atoms by stimulated emission is the expectation value of the light-damping operator δΓ_eeg.

Comparing the traces of (6.11) and (6.23) or (6.10) and (6.24), we find Tr [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_eg = -Tr [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_ge, (6.29)

Tr [d/dt (ρ^Q_egg)]_ge = -Tr [d/dt (ρ^Q_eeg)]_ee. (6.30)

From (6.29) we see that for every atom removed from the ground state by absorption of a photon, another atom is created in the excited state; from (6.30) we see that for every atom removed from the excited state by stimulated emission of a photon, another atom is created in the ground state.

Liouville Space The Liouville-space version of (6.24) is d/dt |ρ^Q_eeg)|_ee = -Γ_p_eeg A_p_eeg |ρ^Q_eeg)|_ee. (6.31)

The mean pumping rate for unpolarized, excited-state atoms is Γ_p_eeg = (1/g_eeg) Tr(δΓ_Q_eeg). (6.32)

One can readily verify that (6.32) is consistent with (6.20). The dimensionless operator A_p_eeg is defined by A_p_eeg = i δH_Q_eeg / ħ Γ_p_eeg. (6.33)

The Liouville-space version of (6.23) is d/dt |ρ^Q_eeg)|_eg = Γ_p_egg A_p_egg |ρ^Q_egg). (6.34)

The dimensionless operator A_p_egg is defined by iħ Γ_p_egg A_p_egg = V_Q^T† W_Q - W_Q^T† V_Q. (6.35)

## 6.3 Collisions

Let the atoms have a nominal collision rate Γ_c with buffer-gas atoms or molecules. As we shall discuss more extensively in subsequent sections, the collisions will cause the density matrix to evolve at the rate d/dt |ρ^P_eeg) = -Γ_c A_c_eeg |ρ_eeg), (6.36)

d/dt |ρ^P_egg) = Γ_c [A_c_eeg |ρ_eeg) - A_c_egg |ρ_egg)]. (6.37)

The dimensionless matrix A_c_eeg describes the quenching of excited atoms due to collision.

with molecular buffer-gas atoms such as N and also transfer from one sublevel of the excited-state atom to another. The dimensionless matrix A_fgeg describes the number and spin polarization of ground-state atoms created by quenching collisions, and A_fggg describes the collisional transfer of atoms between sub-levels of the ground state. We assume the energies of the excited atoms are so great we can neglect endothermic collisions that produce excited-state atoms from ground-state atoms. The total number of atoms must be conserved by collisions, so we must have

(1feg|P_feg) C (1fgg|P_fggg) D 0. (6.38)

## 6.4 Saturation

We will define a fully saturated density matrix as one for which each sublevel of the ground state is equally likely to be populated, and there are no coherences. Then the non-zero parts of the density matrix are

ρ_fggg D 1fgg and ρ_feeg D 1feg, or |ρ) D (1/g)|1), (6.39)

where |1) was given by (4.28). The total number of sublevels in the ground state and excited state is

g D g_fgg C g_feg. (6.40)

One can readily verify that for the saturated density matrix (6.39), the left sides of (6.9) and (6.22) are zero,

d(ρ_Q_fggg)/dt C d(ρ_Q_fggg)/dt D 0, (6.41)

d(ρ_Q_feeg)/dt C d(ρ_Q_feeg)/dt D 0. (6.42)

Atoms with equal populations of all ground-state and excited-state sublevels, as expressed by (6.39), almost never occur in practice, since polarized or directional laser light of sufficient intensity to equalize the sublevel populations of the ground-state and the excited states is also likely to produce strong spin polarization in both states.

## 6.5 Identities

One of the easiest ways to check the numerical values of the superoperators that govern quasi-steady-state pumping is to be sure they satisfy various identities that we have mentioned explicitly or are implicit in the discussion above. We summarize some of these identities here. The Liouville-space versions of the pumping identities (6.29) and (6.30) are

(1feg|A_fegg D (1fgg|A_fggg, (6.43)

(1fgg|A_fgeg D (1feg|A_feeg. (6.44)

The Liouville-space versions of the saturation identities (6.41) and (6.42) are

A_fggg|1fgg)/g_fgg D A_fgeg|1feg)/g_feg, (6.45)

A_feeg|1feg)/g_feg D A_fegg|1fgg)/g_fgg. (6.46)

Multiplying (6.16) on the left by (1fgg|, and noting that if |ρ_fggg) D |1fgg)/g_fgg we will have (1fgg|P_fggg) D Γ_fgg, and carrying out analogous steps with (6.31), we find

(1fgg|A_fggg|1fgg) D g_fgg and (1feg|A_feeg|1feg) D g_feg. (6.47)

Multiplying (6.43) and (6.44) on the right by |1fgg) and |1feg), respectively, and using (6.47), we find

(1feg|A_fegg|1fgg) D g_fgg and (1fgg|A_fgeg|1feg) D g_feg. (6.48)

The Liouville-space version of the spontaneous-emission identity (5.48) is

(1fgg|A_fgeg D (1feg|, which gives (1fgg|A_fgeg|1feg) D g_feg. (6.49)

Setting ρ_feeg D 1feg in (5.47), evaluating the result with (5.37), and transforming to Liouville space, we find a second spontaneous-emission identity

A_fgeg|1feg)/g_feg D |1fgg)/g_fgg. (6.50)

Using (6.38) with (6.36) and (6.37), we find the collisional-relaxation identities

(1fgg|A_fgeg D (1feg|A_feeg, (6.51)

(1fgg|A_fggg D 0. (6.52)

When using identities like those in this section to check the validity of large matrices, one might, for example, execute code that evaluates the column vector A_feeg|1feg)/g_feg C A_fegg|1fgg)/g_fgg, which should be a column vector whose elements are all zero according to (6.46). Some of the elements of the numerical column vector that is generated may be nonzero, with magnitudes on the order of the round-off error of the computation. This does not mean there is something wrong with the matrices A_feeg and A_fegg.

80 6 Quasi-Steady-State Optical Pumping

From inspection of (5.86) and (5.89) we see that (μμ|K_feegˇ|μμ) D 0 and (μμ|K_fgggˇ|μμ) D 0, so we must have

(1feg|K_feegˇ D 0 and K_feegˇ|1feg) D 0. (6.53)

(1fgg|K_fgggˇ D 0 and K_fgggˇ|1fgg) D 0. (6.54)

There are identities analogous to (6.53) and (6.54) for H_feeg and H_fggg, that is, (5.90) and (5.93).

## 6.6 Net Evolution

It will be convenient to write the excited-state and ground-state components of the density matrix as the single column vector

|ρ_Q) D (|ρ_Q_feeg), |ρ_Q_fggg)). (6.55)

Combining the results of the discussion above, we find that the quasi-steady-state evolution of the excited-state and ground-state density matrices due to optical pumping, collisions, hyperfine couplings, and radiative decay is given by

d|ρ_Q)/dt D G_Q|ρ_Q). (6.56)

Here G_Q has the block elements,

G_Q D (G_Q_feeg  G_Q_fegg; G_Q_fgeg  G_Q_fggg), (6.57)

which couple the components |ρ_Q_feeg) and |ρ_Q_fggg) of (6.55):

G_Q_feeg D iE_feegˇ/„ C Γ_feg A_feeg C Γ_c A_feeg, (6.58)

G_Q_fgeg D Γ_fgeg A_fgeg C Γ_feg A_fgeg C Γ_c A_fgeg, (6.59)

G_Q_fegg D Γ_fgg A_fegg, (6.60)

G_Q_fggg D iE_fgggˇ/„ C Γ_fgg A_fggg C Γ_c A_fggg. (6.61)

The steady-state solution of (6.56) is |ρ_1), given by

G_Q|ρ_Q_1) D 0, (6.62)

where G is the damping matrix (6.57), and the normalization is given by

(1feg|ρ_Q_feeg C (1fgg|ρ_Q_fggg D 1. (6.63)

## 6.7 Negligible Stimulated Emission

We often encounter situations where the optical pumping rates Γ_feg and Γ_fgg of the atom are much slower than the spontaneous dec rate Γ_peg . Under these conditions, the optically pumped atoms will reach a quasi-steady state after a few radiative lifetimes, and this will be followed by a much slower transient approach to equilibrium, with time constants on the order of the optical pumping rates and various ground-state collisional relaxation rates. The first “row” of (6.56) is dρ_peg = -G_peg ρ_peg + G_pe ρ_egg. (6.64)

dt The formal solution of (6.64) is ρ_peg(t) = ∫ dt₀ e^{-G_peg (t - t₀)} G_pe ρ_egg(t₀), (6.65)

where ρ_peg(t) is the value of ρ_peg at time t and ρ_egg(t₀) is the value of ρ_egg at an earlier time t₀. The exponential damping factor of (6.65) will be negligibly small for retarded times t₀ more than a few radiative lifetimes earlier than t. If we assume the damping times of the ground-state atoms and the oscillation periods of any ground-state coherences are much longer than the excited-state damping time, we can make the approximation ρ_egg(t₀) ≈ ρ_egg(t), (6.66)

in (6.65) to find ρ_peg = G_peg^{-1} G_pe ρ_egg. (6.67)

As we will discuss in Chapter 7 on modulation, the approximation (6.66) needs to be modified if either ρ_egg or G_pe or both have components oscillating with periods that are comparable to or shorter than the damping times of the excited state. This is often an issue in optically pumped atomic clocks, where the hyperfine clock frequency can be much larger than the radiative decay rate of excited atoms.

Substituting (6.67) into the second “row” of (6.56), we find that the relatively slow evolution of the ground-state density matrix is described by dρ_egg = -i[H_egg/ħ + Γ A_egg + Γ_pe (A_p - A_r)] ρ_egg. (6.68)

dt Since we have assumed that the stimulated emission of excited atoms is negligibly small compared with the much faster radiative decay and quenching, we can set Γ_pe = 0 in (6.59), so (6.68) becomes dρ_egg = -i[H_egg/ħ + Γ A_egg + Γ_pe (A_p - A_r)] ρ_egg. (6.69)

dt 82 6 Quasi-Steady-State Optical Pumping The depopulation-pumping matrix is A_p = A_egg, (6.70)

and the repopulation-pumping matrix is A_r = (1/Γ_egg) G_pe G_peg^{-1} G_pe = (1/Γ_egg) [Γ_pe A_pe + Γ A_pe − i[H_peg/ħ + Γ_pe A_pe] A_pe]. (6.71)

One can use the identities in Section 6.5 to show that Tr[(A_p − A_r)] = 0. (6.72)

## 6.8 High-Pressure Pumping

The preceding discussion is particularly pertinent for nearly collision-free conditions or low buffer-gas pressures where there is little collisional broadening of the optical absorption lines. We will give some specific examples of this analysis in Section 9.3. Here we discuss a simpler situation, the optical pumping of alkali-metal atoms at high gas pressures, a situation that frequently prevails for spin-exchange optical pumping of the nuclei of noble gas atoms. Saturation of the optical pumping transition is seldom an issue for high-pressure pumping, so we will assume unsaturated conditions (negligible stimulated emission) in the following discussions, and we will set ρ_egg = ρ. For sufficiently high buffer-gas pressure, the damping rate of optical coherence γ can greatly exceed the hyperfine splittings or Doppler shifts, and it will be a good approximation to set E_pe = (−ħω − ω_peg + iγ)^{-1} in (5.84). Then (6.12) becomes δH_egg = (2|jE|^2|D|^2 e^{-(Δ)†(Δ)} e)/(ħ (ω − ω_peg + iγ)). (6.73)

The polarization unit vector of the light is e = D/|E|. (6.74)

For alkali-metal atoms, with S = 1/2, and with J = 1/2 or J = 3/2 we can use (5.41) to write e^{-(Δ)†(Δ)} e = (1/2)((-1)^J (S · s) S), (6.75)

where the mean photon spin is s = i(e × e^*). (6.76)

Substituting (6.75) into (6.73) and using (5.45), we find δH_egg = (E_peg)/(iħ Γ_egg (1 + 4K · S)), (6.77)

where E_peg/(iħ Γ_egg) = (πr c e^2)/(ħ ω_peg (ω − ω_peg + iγ)). (6.78)

Here S, the mean energy flux of the light, was given by (5.61).

It will turn out that high-pressure optical pumping is described by precisely the same evolution equations as spin exchange at the rate Γ_egg with fictitious alkali-metal atoms. The electron spin of the fictitious atoms has the expectation value K = ((-1)^J s (S · S))/[J] (6.79)

This is half the mean photon spin for D1 pumping with J = 1/2 and minus one quarter of the photon spin for D2 pumping with J = 3/2. Substituting (6.77) into (6.11), we find that the depopulation pumping rate is dP/dt = − iħ (4E_peg)/(p [K · S, ρ]) Γ_egg (−2⟨K · S, ρ⟩). (6.80)

Collisions of the polarized excited atoms generated by optical pumping play a central role in high-pressure optical pumping. A more detailed discussion of these issues is given in Section 10.14, but we will briefly summarize the key physics here. The electronic polarization of excited atoms is efficiently destroyed in collisions with buffer-gas atoms or molecules. But a collision has little direct effect on the nuclear spin polarization, since the direct interaction must occur through a modification of the hyperfine coupling interaction during the collisional time. The hyperfine coupling strengths are too small to have any appreciable effect.

the nuclear polarization during the few-picosecond duration of a collision. If the collisions occur at frequent enough intervals, which will be very nearly the case for high-pressure optical pumping, the hyperfine coupling interactions of the nucleus with the electrons will fluctuate so rapidly in sign and orientation that there will be negligible modification of the nuclear polarization, but nearly complete destruction of the electronic polarization, before the atom radiatively decays or undergoes a quenching collision. We will therefore assume that at high gas pressures the electronic polarization of the excited atoms will be destroyed, but the nuclear polarization will be conserved.

As we will discuss in more detail in connection with (11.20), the part of the density matrix with only nuclear polarization is ρ/4CsS. We have assumed that the atoms lost by depopulation pumping will be returned from the excited state with the original nuclear polarization intact, so the repopulation pumping rate will be ρ_P^{rp} = 1/4 ρ_P^{dp} S^{+}ρ^{S}P S . (6.81)

The total optical pumping rate is therefore ρ_P^{op} = ρ_P^{dp} + ρ_P^{rp} = 1/4 ρ_P^{dp} S^{+}ρ^{S}P S . (6.82)

Substituting the depopulation pumping rate (6.80) into (6.82) and simplifying the result with the aid of the identity δ_i S_j S_k = i/4 ε_{ijk} S_k + 1/4 (δ_{ij}S_k + ...) (6.83)

for the angular momentum operators of a spin-1/2 particle, we find ρ_P^{op} = Γ_{fgg}^{p} 3/4 [ρ^{+}S^{+}ρ^{S}K^{+}[fS,(ρ^{+}g^{+}2iS^{+}ρ^{S}]

+ ρ^{+}2i^{+}[K^{+}S,(ρ^{+})] . (6.84)

The shift parameter is κ = 2E_{fgg}^{p} / ΔΓ_{fgg}^{p} . (6.85)

Equation 6.84 is formally identical to the basic evolution equation for spin exchange, (10.253). So (6.84) describes spin-exchange collisions (at the rate Γ_{fgg}^{p} and with a frequency-shift parameter κ) of the real alkali-metal atoms with fictitious alkali-metal atoms of electron spin polarization K. The fictitious atoms are the photons of the pumping light.

**6.8.1 Liouville Space** Adding in the evolution due to the ground-state Hamiltonian to the high-pressure optical pumping of (6.84), we find that the damping operator G of (1.10) becomes G = -i/ħ H_{fgg}^{©} + Γ_{fgg}^{p} A^{op} , (6.86)

where the optical pumping matrix is A^{op} = A^{sd} - K^{+}A^{ex} - 2iκK^{+}S^{©} . (6.87)

Here H_{fgg}^{©} and S^{©} are commutator superoperators, generated in accordance with (4.85) for the ground-state Hamiltonian H_{fgg} of (2.10) and the ground-state electronic spin operator S. The S-damping operator is A^{sd} = 1/4 S^{©+}S^{©} = 1/4 (S^{©+}S^{©}) . (6.88)

For (6.88), the unit matrix, 1_{fgg}, for the ground state of Liouville space was given by (5.112), and we noted that S^{©+}[S^{©+}] = DS^{©+}S^{©}] = 1_{fgg}. (6.89)

The spin-exchange operator is A^{ex} = S^{©+}[C^{+}S^{©}] - 2iS^{©+}[D^{+}S^{©}] . (6.90)

When appended to the program ending with Code 5.3 on page 57, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the transients and steady state for high-pressure optical pumping.

**Code 6.1** ```matlab Gmp=input(’Mean pumping rate, Gmp = ’); kappa=input(’Shift parameter, kappa = ’); Kj=input(’Fictitious spin, [Kx Ky Kz] = ’); for k=1:3 sharpS(:,:,k)=sharp(Sj(:,:,k)); %sharp electron spin matrices flatS(:,:,k)=flat(Sj(:,:,k)); %flat electron spin matrices end SC=flatS-sharpS; %Liouville-space spin matrices Asd=matdot(SC,SC)/2; %S-damping matrix Aex=flatS+sharpS-2*i*matcross(flatS,sharpS); %exchange matrix %high-pressure optical pumping matrix Aop=Asd-Kj(1)*Aex(:,:,1)-Kj(2)*Aex(:,:,2)-Kj(3)*Aex(:,:,3)...

-2*i*kappa*(Kj(1)*SC(:,:,1)+Kj(2)*SC(:,:,2)+Kj(3)*SC(:,:,3)); HgC=flat(Hg)-sharp(Hg); %Liouville-space Hamiltonian G=i*HgC/hbar+Gmp*Aop; %static damping operator Lp=logical(cPg); %logical variable for populations nt=100; %number of sample points t=linspace(0,40,nt); %sample times in units of 1/Gmp rhoc=zeros(gg,nt); %initialize compactified density matrix for k=1:nt %evaluate transient rhoc(:,k)=expm(-t(k)*G(Lp,Lp)/Gmp)*cPg(Lp)/gg; end clf; plot(t,rhoc); grid on; hold on rhocin=null(G(Lp,Lp));rhocin=rhocin/sum(rhocin); %steady state plot(t,rhocin*ones(1,nt), ’-.’)

xlabel(’Time in units of 1/\Gamma_p^\fg\gg’)

ylabel(’Sublevel populations’)

```

Subroutines to generate flat and sharp supermatrices were given by Code 4.3 and Code 4.4 on page 46. The cross product of the spin matrices was evaluated with the following simple subroutine.

**Code 6.2** ```matlab function C=matcross(A,B)

C(:,:,1)=A(:,:,2)*B(:,:,3)-A(:,:,3)*B(:,:,2); C(:,:,2)=A(:,:,3)*B(:,:,1)-A(:,:,1)*B(:,:,3); C(:,:,3)=A(:,:,1)*B(:,:,2)-A(:,:,2)*B(:,:,1); ```

In Figure 6.1 we show an optical pumping transient calculated with code that ends with Code 6.1 on page 85. Except at very small magnetic fields, the coherences of the density matrix will be negligibly small, and the problem has been compactified to populations. High-pressure pumping is equivalent to spin exchange with a fictitious second species of alkali-metal atom with mean spin polarization ⟨S⟩ = K. The equilibrium populations of sublevels of azimuthal quantum number m evaluated with the code ending with ith = 0.5, B = 1, Gmp = 1000, kappa = 0, and Code 6.1 on page 85. The equilibrium populations are indicated by the horizontal lines. The [Kx Ky Kz] = [0 0 0.25].

ber m, the late-time asymptotes of the figure, are proportional to e^{δm}. Here δ = ln([1-Kz]/[1-2Kz]) is the spin-temperature parameter. Since we used Kz = 0.25 to generate Figure 6.1, the steady state corresponds to δ = ln 3, or a population ratio e^{δ} = 3. Each late-time asymptote of Figure 6.1 is a factor of 3 larger than the next-smaller one. We will discuss issues related to spin exchange in more detail in Section 10.12.

## 6.9 Spectral Width of Pumping Light

So far we have discussed monochromatic light that pumps atoms with a well-defined velocity v. As shown in (5.95), we have modeled collisions with buffer-gas atoms by adding a collisional contribution, Γ_c = α_f ε_gg, to the damping rate, γ, of the optical coherence. The homogeneous linewidth resulting from γ can be power-broadened at sufficiently high light intensities. Here we discuss two potentially important inhomogeneous line-broadening mechanisms, the spectral width of the light and Doppler broadening. Suppose that the light source has a well-characterized spectral width, ΔΩ. Then we will write the optical interaction amplitude of (5.63) as the time-dependent operator

V_Q = V_Q(Ω_n) e^{-iΩ_n t}. (6.91)

We think of the amplitudes V_Q(Ω_n) as variables that have the same stationary statistics as the pumping light. The amplitude products have the ensemble averages

⟨V_Q†(Ω_m) V_Q(Ω_n)⟩ = δ_{mn} f_n |V_Q|^2. (6.92)

Here V_Q† and V_Q are the amplitudes for purely monochromatic light of the same average power as the broadband light, and f_n is the probability that the light will have frequency ω + Ω_n. As indicated by the factor δ_{mn} in (6.92), we assume that the amplitudes of the different frequencies are uncorrelated, so they are unable to drive coherent population trapping resonances in the atom. In Chapter 7 we will discuss light that has been intentionally modulated to drive coherent population trapping resonances. We assume that

f_n = 1. (6.93)

We can think of (6.91) as a discrete Fourier transform with frequencies

Ω_n = 2πn / τ, with n = 0, ±1, ±2, ... (6.94)

We assume that

δΩ ≪ ΔΩ, (6.95)

so there are many sampling waves within the spectral bandwidth of the light.

For steady-state conditions, we can use (6.91) in (6.4) to find

W_{Q μ N_μ} = ∑_n W_{Q μ N_μ n} e^{-iΩ_n t}. (6.96)

The energy shifts, E_{μ N_μ gg}, due to hyperfine structure, magnetic fields, and the Doppler shift, were given by (5.84). Then we can account for the spectral width of the light in coupling terms that contain products like V†W, for example, the effective Hamiltonian (6.12) of ground-state atoms,

δH = ∑_{μ N} ⟨μ gg| D = ⟨V_Q ν† μ N V_Q μ N⟩ E_{μ N} f_{μ gg} (E_{μ gg} - ħΩ)^{-1}, (6.97)

by replacing the complex energy-difference matrix (E_{μ N_μ gg} - ħΩ)^{-1} by the spectrally averaged matrix

⟨(E_{μ N_μ gg} - ħΩ)^{-1}⟩ = ∑_n f_n (E_{μ N_μ gg} - ħΩ_n)^{-1}. (6.98)

6.9.1 Gaussian Spectral Profiles

The spectral widths of lasers can often be modeled adequately with Gaussian spectral profiles. Then the probability that the laser frequency is displaced from the mean frequency ω by an amount between Ω and Ω + dΩ is

f(Ω)dΩ = (1 / (√(2π) σ_ω)) e^{-Ω^2 / (2σ_ω^2)} dΩ. (6.99)

Letting f_n → f(Ω)dΩ in (6.98) and using (6.99), we find

⟨(E_{μ N_μ gg} - ħΩ)^{-1}⟩ = (1 / (√(2π) σ_ω)) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} (e^{-Ω^2 / (2σ_ω^2)} / (E_{μ N_μ gg} + ħΩ)) dΩ. (6.100)

Making the change of variable Ω = √(2) σ_ω t, we find

⟨(E_{μ N_μ gg} - ħΩ)^{-1}⟩ = (1 / (ħ√(2) σ_ω)) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} (e^{-t^2} / (t - z_{μ N_μ})) dt = Z(z_{μ N_μ}) / (ħ√(2) σ_ω). (6.101)

The plasma dispersion function [47], Z(z_{μ N_μ}) of (6.101), has the complex argument

z_{μ N_μ} = E_{μ N_μ gg} / (ħ√(2) σ_ω). (6.102)

6.9.2 Plasma Dispersion Function

We conclude this section by summarizing a few useful properties of the plasma dispersion function

Z(z) = (1 / √π) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} (e^{-t^2} / (t - z)) dt, for Im(z) > 0. (6.103)

Let the argument z = x + iy have a real part x and an imaginary part iy. The "area" of Z(z) is

∫_{-∞}^{∞} Z(x + iy) dx = iπ. (6.104)

A useful symmetry is

Z^*(x + iy) = Z(-x + iy). (6.105)

For |z| ≫ 1, it is often convenient to use the asymptotic expression

Z(z) ≈ -1/z. (6.106)

The plasma dispersion function Z(z) is a simple multiple of the convenient Faddeeva function, w(z), where

Z(z) = i√π w(z). (6.107)

An expression for the Faddeeva function, valid for all finite z, is

w(z) = e^{-z^2} erfc(-iz) = (1 / √π) ∫_{0}^{∞} e^{izu} e^{-u^2/4} du. (6.108)

The integral on du in (6.108) extends over all real nonnegative values of u. The complementary error function is defined for the complex number z by

erfc(z) = (2 / √π) ∫_{z}^{∞} e^{-t^2} dt. (6.109)

The integral starts from t = z in the complex t-plane and extends to t = ∞ along the positive real axis.

An efficient MATLAB code due to Weideman et al. [48] to evaluate the Faddeeva function is

Code 6.3 function f = w(z); % Computes the function w(z)=exp(-z^2)erfc(-iz) using a rational % series with N terms. It is assumed that Im(z)>0 or Im(z)=0.

% We use N=10, but higher accuracy can be obtained with larger N = 10; M = 2*N; M2 = 2*M; k = [-M+1:1:M-1]'; % M2 = no. of sampling points.

L = sqrt(N/sqrt(2)); % Optimal choice of L.

theta = k*pi/M; t = L*tan(theta/2); % Variables theta and t.

f = exp(-t.^2).*(L^2+t.^2); f=[0;f]; % Function to be transformed.

a = real(fft(fftshift(f)))/M2; % Coefficients of transform.

a = flipud(a(2:N+1)); % Reorder coefficients.

Z = (L+i*z)./(L-i*z); p = polyval(a,Z); % Polynomial evaluation.

f = 2*p./(L-i*z).^2+(1/sqrt(pi))./(L-i*z); % Evaluate w(z).

## 6.10 Doppler Broadening

Let the atoms have a Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution of velocities, characterized by an absolute temperature T, and let v be the component of the atomic velocity along the direction of propagation of the light. The probability of finding the velocity between v and v+dv is Gaussian, and is given by W(v)dv = (1/(σ_v √(2π))) exp(-v^2 / (2σ_v^2)) dv, (6.110)

where the velocity variance is σ_v^2 = k_B T / M. (6.111)

Here k_B is Boltzmann’s constant and M is the atomic mass. Assuming broadening from both the velocity distribution of the atoms and the spectral width of the pumping light, we can write the averaged resonance factor as ⟨(Ω - (ω_fge + kv)) / (Ω - (ω_fge + kv) + iγ)⟩ = ∫∫ dΩ dv W(v) (Ω - ω_fge - kv) / (Ω - ω_fge - kv + iγ) * (1/(√(2π) σ_Ω)) exp(-Ω^2 / (2σ_Ω^2)). (6.112)

Introducing the new variables x = v / (σ_v √2) and y = Ω / (σ_Ω √2), (6.113)

we rewrite (6.112) as ⟨...⟩ = (1/π) ∫∫ dx dy exp(-(x^2 + y^2)) * (√2 (kσ_v x + σ_Ω y)) / (√2 (kσ_v x + σ_Ω y) + iγ). (6.114)

Changing variables once more to t = √2 (kσ_v x + σ_Ω y)/σ and s = √2 (-σ_ω x + kσ_v y)/σ, where the net frequency variance is σ^2 = (kσ_v)^2 + σ_Ω^2, (6.115)

we find in analogy to (6.101)

⟨...⟩ = (1/(σ√2)) Z( (ω_fge + iμ_N) / (σ√2) ) / μ_N. (6.116)

In analogy to (6.102), the argument of the plasma dispersion function is z = (ω_fge + iμ_N) / (σ√2) / μ_N. (6.117)

In (6.117) it is to be understood that the matrix ⟨Ω_fge⟩ of (5.84) is to be evaluated at v = 0. For atoms with a Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution of velocities, illuminated by light with a Gaussian spectral profile, the Doppler width and laser width add in quadrature, as shown in (6.115).

93 Modulation Resonances are often induced in optically pumped systems (e.g., atomic clocks or magnetometers) by subjecting them to oscillating (modulated) magnetic fields or to modulated light. If the oscillation frequency is nearly equal to the Bohr frequency for a pair of sublevels, transitions of populations between the sublevels and large modulated coherences can be induced. For clarity, we will focus on the ground state and set ω_fge = ω and H_fg = H, but excited atoms respond to modulation in an analogous way. We will write the density matrix as the generalized Fourier series, ρ = Σ_m ρ(m) e^{-im·Ωt}, or |ρ⟩ = Σ_m |ρ(m)⟩ e^{-im·Ωt}. (7.1)

To account for several different drive frequencies we introduce a drive-frequency vector, Ω, and a set of harmonic-index vectors m. For example, if an alkali-metal atom is subject to one magnetic resonance field oscillating at a radio frequency, Ω_{f1}, that is nearly resonant for low-field Zeeman resonances, and a second magnetic resonance field oscillating at a microwave frequency, Ω_{f2}, that is nearly resonant for the 0–0 microwave clock transition, we can write Ω = [Ω_{f1}; Ω_{f2}]. (7.2)

A typical index vector for this case would be m = [m_{f1}, m_{f2}] = [2, 1]. (7.3)

These magnetic resonance fields could induce the coherence (k|ρ|) = ⟨a2|ρ|b0⟩ to oscillate at the frequency m·Ω = 2Ω_{f1} + Ω_{f2}, which is close to the Bohr frequency, ω, of the coherence. Here we are using low-field labels for the ground-state sublevels, |f m⟩, of an alkali-metal atom, with a = I + 1/2 and b = I - 1/2. Alternatively, we use the single label k, such that |k⟩ = |a2 b0⟩. The coherence is created when the atom simultaneously absorbs (or emits) m_{f1} = 2 photons of frequency Ω_{f1} and m_{f2} = 1 photon of frequency Ω_{f2}. In (7.1) and elsewhere, a quantity with the superscript (m) denotes the amplitude of a term that oscillates as e^{-im·Ωt}. Since ρ is Hermitian, we must have ρ(m)† = ρ(-m). (7.4)

7 Modulation Anticipating a point we will discuss later, in many cases the Fourier series for the element (k|ρ(m)|) will contain only one potentially large term with m = n, where n is the "multiple quantum index" of the basis state |k⟩. Neglecting all Fourier amplitudes, (k|ρ(m)|), except for (k|ρ(n)|) is called the secular approximation.

## 7.1 Magnetic Resonance

A common way to create coherence is to apply a magnetic field oscillating at a frequency or frequencies close to that of one or more Bohr frequencies of the atom. We write the field as B = Σ_m B(m) e^{-im·Ωt}. (7.5)

Since B is real, we must have B(m)* = B(-m). (7.6)

The Schrödinger-space Hamiltonian has a Fourier series analogous to (7.1), H = Σ_m H(m) e^{-im·Ωt}. (7.7)

The Fourier amplitudes of (7.7), with m≠0, are H(m) = -μ_fg · B(m), (7.8)

where the magnetic-moment operator, μ_fg, was defined by (2.11). The Hermitian conjugates are H(m)† = H(-m). (7.9)

The Liouville-space versions of (7.7)–(7.9) are H© = Σ_m H(m)© e^{-im·Ωt}, where H(m)© = H(m)[•, •] - [•, H(m)]. (7.10)

We will give examples of how to use the magnetic resonance interaction (7.10) to calculate single- and multiple-quantum resonance spectra at the end of this chapter.

## 7.2 Modulated Light

Light that is modulated in amplitude, frequency, or polarization is often used to excite resonances in optically pumped atoms. These are often called “coherent population trapping” (CPT) resonances.

7.2.1 High Pressure For the simple case of high-pressure optical pumping that we discussed in Section 6.8, we can represent intensity modulation of the light by writing the mean pumping rate as Γ_fgg f(t), (7.11)

where Γ_fgg is time-independent and where the modulation factor f(t) has the Fourier series f(t) = Σ_m f(m) e^{-imΩt}, with f(0)=1 and f(-m) = f(m)^*. (7.12)

The amplitudes f(m) must be such that f is never negative. For a single modulation frequency, such that mΩ = m_0Ω, a useful model for f is f = e^{α cos Ωt} / J_0(iα), with f(m) = J_m(iα) / J_0(iα). (7.13)

Here J_m(z) is the m-th order Bessel function of the complex argument z. The Fourier amplitudes, f(m), of f can be found with the well-known generating function [19], sometimes used to define Bessel functions, e^{i z sin φ} = Σ_m e^{imφ} J_m(z). (7.14)

The individual peaks of the pulse sequence (7.12) get narrower as the positive exponential coefficient, α, gets bigger. The Fourier amplitudes, f(m), of practical devices for light modulation, for example, Mach–Zehnder electro-optical modulators, depend on m in a way that is qualitatively similar to the simple model of (7.13), but the quantitative details are different. For the situation described by (7.11), the Fourier amplitudes of the optical pumping matrix are A(m)_op = A_op f(m), (7.15)

with A_op given by (6.87). At the end of this chapter, we will give an example of CPT resonances excited by intensity-modulated light for high-pressure optical pumping.

7.2.2 Lower Pressure At lower buffer-gas pressures, the more detailed features of the optical absorption line must be taken into account. As an example of how to analyze this situation, consider a superposition of plane waves with different frequencies but all propagating along the unit vector n of (5.57), E = Σ_p E(p) e^{i(k_p·r - ω_p t)}. (7.16)

This is analogous to the monochromatic wave (5.55). The temporal frequencies ω_p and the spatial frequencies k_p for the optical sideband p are ω_p = ω + pΩ and k_p = p n. (7.17)

In analogy to (7.3), we write the modulation indices as p = [p_{f1}, p_{f2}, ...]. (7.18)

The elements p_{fj} will normally be integers, as in (7.3). With (7.16) we can generalize (5.62) to V = Σ_p V(p). (7.19)

The Fourier components are V(p) = V(p) e^{i(k_p·v - ω_p)t}, where V(p) = -i D^†(p) E(p) e^{i k_p·r_0}. (7.20)

We can also generalize (6.8) to W = Σ_p W(p). (7.21)

The Fourier components are W(p) = W(p) e^{i(k_p·v - ω_p)t}, where W(p) = V(p) / E_fegg. (7.22)

In analogy to (5.84), the energy-difference matrix is Δ(p)_fegg = H_fegg + i (Δω_p - k_p·v - iγ/2), where Δω_p = ω_p - ω_fegg. (7.23)

7.2.3 Modulated Optical Pumping Matrices Products of V^† and W or of V and W^† are used to construct the coupling matrices for optical pumping in Liouville space. Therefore, the coupling matrices will have terms oscillating at frequencies mΩ, where m = p - p' is the difference in two light modulation indices, p and p'. To account for Doppler shifts of atoms moving with velocity v, the drive frequency vector must be modified to Ω → Ω(1 - n·v/c). (7.24)

The light propagates along the unit vector n of (5.57). For example, the effective Hamiltonian of the ground state, (6.12), will become δH^Q_fgg = Σ_m δH^Q_fgg(m) e^{-imΩt}, (7.25)

where δH^Q_fgg(m) = Σ_p V^†(p) W(m+p). (7.26)

In analogy to (6.17) and (6.14), we write the mean pumping rate for ground-state atoms as Γ_fgg = 1/2 (δΓ_fgg(0) |1_fgg), where δΓ_fgg(0) = (δH_fgg(0) - δH_fgg(0)^†). (7.27)

We can readily use (7.19) and (7.21) to construct Fourier series analogous to (7.25) for the other coupling matrices. For example, the coupling coefficient of (6.34) becomes A_fegg = Σ_m A_fegg(m) e^{-imΩt}, (7.28)

where in analogy to (6.35), the Fourier amplitudes are given by iΓ_feg A_fegg(m) = Σ_p (V(p)·W(m+p) - W(p)·V(m+p)). (7.29)

The analysis of low-pressure optical pumping has much in common with the analysis of the physics of magneto-optical traps, which we discuss in Section 9.3.

## 7.3 Secular Approximation

Having discussed the two most important ways to induce modulated coherence in optically pumped atoms, magnetic resonance and pumping with modulated light, we turn to the most convenient methods for analyzing this situation. In analogy to (4.38), the rate of change of the density matrix is given by dρ/dt = -i [H(0)/ℏ, C] + G ρ. (7.30)

Here H(0) is the Fourier amplitude (7.10) with m = 0, that is, the unmodulated part of the Hamiltonian. The energy basis states of Liouville space are eigenvectors of H(0), and the eigenvalues are proportional to the Bohr frequencies H(0)|jk) = ℏω_jk|jk), where |jk) = |μν) and ω_jk = ω_μν = (E_μ - E_ν)/ℏ. (7.31)

The remaining part of the damping operator of (7.30) has the Fourier series G = Σ_m G(m) e^{-imΩt}. (7.32)

The Fourier amplitudes are G(0) = Σ_x Γ_x A_x(0) and G(m) = H(m) + Σ_x Γ_x A_x(m), for m≠0. (7.33)

The relaxation process x (e.g., optical pumping or some form of collisional spin relaxation) has a characteristic relaxation rate Γ and a dimensionless coupling matrix A_xx = Σ A(m) e^{-imΩt} . (7.34)

Most collisional relaxation processes are not modulated and have only terms with m=0. However, if the light is modulated at the frequency mΩ, we would expect some Fourier amplitudes, A(m)_op, of the optical pumping matrix to be non-zero for m ≠ 0.

Multiplying (7.30) on the left by the row vector ⟨k|, we find d/dt (⟨k|ρ) = -i ω_k ⟨k|ρ) - ⟨k|Λ|ρ) . (7.35)

We will restrict our discussion of modulated excitation to situations where the Bohr frequencies, ω_k, of coherences are much bigger than any relaxation rates or magnetic resonance coupling rates. Then the first term on the right of (7.35) will be much bigger than the second, which represents relaxation mechanisms or magnetic resonance couplings. If we ignored the second term on the right of (7.35) entirely, the solution would be (⟨k|ρ(t)) = (⟨k|ρ(0)) e^{-i ω_k t}. This is the basic idea of the secular equation which consists in setting (⟨k|ρ(m)) = 0, unless ω_k ≈ mΩ, for some m = n_k . (7.36)

Equation 7.36 defines a (possibly vector) "multiple-quantum number", n_k, and a secular-approximation column vector, |l), with elements given by the logical equation (⟨k|l) = (ω_k ≈ mΩ, for some m = n_k ) . (7.37)

We will always have (⟨k|l) = 1 (true) for populations, since ω_k = 0 and therefore n_k = 0 for populations. If (⟨k|l) = 0 (false), we will assume that the corresponding coherence (⟨k|ρ) is negligibly small, and we will drop it from the density matrix, which we compactify to a secular subspace, as shown in Figure 7.1. In evaluating (7.37), one should interpret the symbol ≈ to mean "equal to within a few natural linewidths". The results are not very sensitive to how many linewidths are specified, but the computer codes will run faster the fewer "true" elements |l) contains. For states |k) for which (7.37) is true we define the element of the density matrix in the "rotating" frame by (⟨k|ρ_Q) = (⟨k|ρ(n_k) . (7.38)

Figure 7.1 Omitting negligibly small coherences from the density matrix, ρ, can substantially speed up numerical calculations, with negligible loss in accuracy. Here we assume that an oscillating magnetic resonance field has excited ρ_24 and ρ_42, and all other coherences are negligibly small. Retaining only the populations and the non-zero coherences compactifies Liouville space from 16 to only six dimensions. For computer coding, the compactification is conveniently done with a logical compactification matrix, l, with 1’s at the positions of non-zero elements of ρ and 0’s elsewhere.

We also define a "multiple-quantum" operator N© = Σ n_k |k)(⟨k| . (7.39)

The sum extends over all basis states |k) for which (7.37) is true. Then the density matrix, |ρ), in the "laboratory" frame is related to the density matrix |ρ_Q) in the "rotating" frame by |ρ) = e^{-i N© Ω t} |ρ_Q), or |ρ_Q) = e^{i N© Ω t} |ρ)).

(7.40)

Substituting (7.39) into (7.30), we find d/dt (e^{-i N© Ω t} |ρ_Q) = (-i H(0)/ħ - Λ) e^{-i N© Ω t} |ρ_Q) . (7.41)

Multiplying both sides of (7.41) on the left by e^{i N© Ω t} and dropping rapidly oscillating terms, in accordance with the secular approximation, we find the evolution equation for the rotating-frame density matrix, d/dt |ρ_Q) = (-i [H(0)/ħ - N© Ω] - Λ_Q) |ρ_Q) . (7.42)

The coupling terms due to the relaxation processes are described by the time-independent matrix Λ_Q = Σ G(m) · M_{n_k - n_j = m} . (7.43)

The secular-approximation matrices M_{n_k - n_j = m} are arrays of logical variables, designed to eliminate rapidly oscillating coupling terms from (7.42) and defined by (⟨k| M_{n_k - n_j = m} |j⟩) = δ(n_k - n_j, m) . (7.44)

Resonance In the steady state, (7.42) becomes (-i [H(0)/ħ - N© Ω] - Λ_Q) |ρ_Q) = 0 . (7.45)

In practice, we would solve (7.45) by letting |ρ_Q) be the null vector of (i H(0)/ħ - N© Ω + Λ_Q), normalized so the sum of the populations is 1. However, we can get some insight by noting that a formal solution of (7.45) is (⟨k|ρ_Q) = (⟨k|Q) / (γ_k + i (ω_k - n_k Ω)) . (7.46)

Here the source rate of the element (⟨k|ρ_Q) is (⟨k|Q) = - Σ_{j≠k} (⟨k|Λ_Q|j⟩ (⟨j|ρ_Q) , (7.47)

and the nominal damping rate is γ_k = ⟨k|Λ_Q|k⟩ . (7.48)

From (7.46) we see that the coherence (⟨k|ρ_Q) will be resonantly enhanced when its Bohr frequency, ω_k, is equal to the "drive frequency", n_k Ω = n_{f1} Ω_{f1} + n_{f2} Ω_{f2} + ... The energy to make the atom jump from the initial to the final state of the coherence comes from n_{f1} quanta of frequency Ω_{f1}, n_{f2} quanta of frequency Ω_{f2}, and so on. The multiple-quantum indices, n_{fj}, are positive or negative integers, or zero. For populations we have both ω_k = 0 and n_k = 0, so populations are always "resonantly enhanced" and are always part of the compactified, secular subspace.

With this introduction to the physics of modulation, we return to the discussion of quasi-steady-state optical pumping with negligible sti stimulated emission. The coupling matrix GQfeeg of (6.56) represents evolution under the influence of the excited-state Hamiltonian, collisions, spontaneous radiative decay, and stimulated emission. All of these evolution mechanisms except stimulated emission will be unmodulated, so if we neglect stimulated emission, the only nonzero Fourier amplitude of GQfeeg will be the unmodulated amplitude and we can set GQfeeg = GQfeeg(0). By analogous arguments we can set GQfgeg = GQfgeg(0), since without stimulated emission, only spontaneous radiative decay and quenching collisions, both unmodulated, contribute to GQfgeg. However, the coupling matrices GQfggg and GQfegg, both containing terms proportional to the potentially modulated absorption of light, should be written as the Fourier series

GQfggg = Σ_m GQfggg(m) e^{-imΩt} and GQfegg = Σ_m GQfegg(m) e^{-imΩt}. (7.49)

Then (6.64) must be modified to read

d/dt |ρ_{Qfeeg}(m) e^{-imΩt} = [GQfeeg |ρ_{Qfeeg}(m) e^{-imΩt} - Σ_{p,n} GQfegg(p) |ρ_{Qfggg}(n) e^{-i(p+n)Ωt}]. (7.50)

Equating terms of (7.50) with the same modulation factors, we find

d/dt |ρ_{Qfeeg}(m) = -(GQfeeg - imΩ) |ρ_{Qfeeg}(m) - Σ_n GQfegg(m-n) |ρ_{Qfggg}(n)). (7.51)

The formal solution of (7.51) is

|ρ_{Qfeeg}(m)(t) = ∫_{t0}^t dt' e^{-(GQfeeg - imΩ)(t-t')} GQfegg(m-n) |ρ_{Qfggg}(n)(t'). (7.52)

Assuming that the Fourier amplitudes are nearly constant for times comparable to the damping rates (radiative and collisional) of GQfeeg - imΩ, we can set

|ρ_{Qfggg}(n)(t') = |ρ_{Qfggg}(n)(t) (7.53)

in (7.52) to find

|ρ_{Qfeeg}(m) = (GQfeeg - imΩ)^{-1} GQfegg(m-n) |ρ_{Qfggg}(n)). (7.54)

Substituting (7.54) into the second row of (6.56) and equating terms with the same frequency factors, we find

d/dt |ρ_{Qfggg}(m) = imΩ |ρ_{Qfggg}(m) - Σ_n GQfggg(m-n) |ρ_{Qfggg}(n)) + GQfgeg (GQfeeg - imΩ)^{-1} GQfegg(m-n) |ρ_{Qfggg}(n)). (7.55)

From inspection of (7.56) and (6.69)–(6.71) we conclude that for situations where the density matrix has modulated components, the repopulation matrix becomes

A^{rp}_{jk} = Σ_n GQfgeg (GQfeeg - inΩ)^{-1} GQfegg(n) j_{Γfgg}(n) k. (7.56)

## 7.5 Examples

7.5.1 Isolated Magnetic Resonances

The simplest resonances are those where only one coherence, and its Hermitian conjugate, can be excited. We assume that the Bohr frequencies, ω, of the coherences differ from other Bohr frequencies of the atom by many times the resonance linewidth. The linewidth is determined by the relaxation processes, for example, the γ of (7.48), and in the case of magnetic resonance by the amplitude of the oscillating magnetic field. An example of an isolated resonance would be the microwave end resonance between sublevels |f,m⟩ = |b,b⟩ and |f,m⟩ = |a,a⟩, where a = I + 1/2 and b = I - 1/2. Assume a microwave magnetic field oscillating along the x axis with a frequency Ω, close to the Bohr frequency, ω, of the end resonance

B = B_z + B_x cos Ωt, or B(0) = B_z, B(1) = B(-1) = B_x/2. (7.57)

Then the modulated amplitudes of the Hamiltonian, (7.8), are

H(1) = H(-1) = -B_x μ_{fgg} / 2. (7.58)

The magnetic dipole moment matrix μ_{fgg} was specified by (2.11). The coupled (energy-basis) version of (2.11) should be used in (7.58). The coherent basis states (end states) that can be excited by the resonant microwave field are

|e⟩ = |a,a⟩⟨b,b| and |ē⟩ = |b,b⟩⟨a,a|. (7.59)

The multiple-quantum numbers n of (7.37) become n = 1 and n̄ = -1. (7.60)

Since there is only one drive frequency, we can simplify the vector index n to the scalar index n. No other coherences will be excited, and the compactified density matrix will have only populations with n = 0 and the coherences |e⟩ and |ē⟩. The effective spin operator of (7.39) is therefore

Ñ = |e⟩⟨e| - |ē⟩⟨ē|. (7.61)

The damping operator of (7.33) and (7.43) is

-G_Q = A_op . ( M{0} + iH(1)Ñ/ℏ ) . ( M{1} + M{-1} ), (7.62)

where the optical pumping operator, A_op, was given by (6.87) and the secular-approximation matrices, M{n}, were given by (7.44).

The unmodulated part of the Hamiltonian, which appears in the damping operator of (7.45), is

H(0)Ñ = H_{fgg}Ñ. (7.63)

When appended to the program ending with Code 6.1 on page 85, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate how the sublevel populations change when the frequency of a microwave field is tuned through the resonance frequency of the end resonance.

Code 7.1

Bx = input('Peak oscillating field in Gauss. Bx = '); Hp1 = -Bx * mug(:,:,1) / 2; % Fourier amplitude of H Hp1C = flat(Hp1) - sharp(Hp1); % Liouville version Lcp1 = fgl==a & mgl==a & fgr==b & mgr==b; % logical var. of |aa><bb| Lcm1 = fgl==b & mgl==b & fgr==a & mgr==a; % logical var. of |bb><aa| L = Lp | Lcp1 | Lcm1; % logical variable for secular elements of rho nk = Lcp1 - Lcm1; NL = diag(nk); % indices and effective spin operator Mp1 = nk * ones(1, gg*gg) - ones(gg*gg, 1) * nk' == 1; % secular approx. matrices M0 = nk * ones(1, gg*gg) - ones(gg*gg, 1) * nk' == 0; Mm1 = nk * ones(1, gg*gg) - ones(gg*gg, 1) * nk' == -1; tHmrC = Hp1C .* (Mp1 + Mm1); % secular part of mag. res. interaction Aop=Aop.*M0; % secular part of optical pumping matrix G0s=i*(HgC(L,L)+tHmrC(L,L))/hbar+Gmp*tAop(L,L); % no tuning om0=(Eg(1)-Eg(gg))/hbar; % resonance freq.

num=.1e6; nnu=201; % maximum detuning and number of samples om=2*pi*linspace(-num,num,nnu); % sample detunings rhosin=zeros(sum(L),nnu); % initialize secular density matrix for k=1:nnu Gs=G0s-i*(om0+om(k))*NL(L,L); % include tuning rhosin(:,k)=null(Gs); % evaluate steady state rhosin(:,k)=rhosin(:,k)/(Lp(L)’*rhosin(:,k)); end figure (2);clf subplot(2,1,1); plot(om/(2*pi*1e3),real(rhosin(Lp(L),:))’); grid on; xlabel(’Microwave detuning in kHz’); ylabel(’Sublevel populations’);

The results of running the code ending with Code 7.1 are shown in Figure 7.2a. For frequencies tuned well off resonance, the populations are in spin-temperature equilibrium, with a population ratio of 3, as in Figure 6.1. Although the populations of the two end sublevels are nearly equalized on resonance, the atoms remain partially spin polarized, since not all sublevels are equally populated.

7.5.2 Zeeman Magnetic Resonances

Low-field Zeeman resonances for alkali-metal atoms are complicated since the radio-frequency field easily drives multiple-quantum transitions in both the upper and the lower Zeeman multiplets. We will assume the same oscillating field (7.57) is applied to the atoms, but that the oscillation frequency is close to the nominal Zeeman resonance frequency, g μ B G/(h[I]) ≈ 2.8/[I] MHz G^-1. It is clear that no hyperfine coherences will be excited, so the secular density matrix will include all populations and Zeeman coherences, that is, the Liouville basis states, |k⟩ = |jfm_l ⟨fm_j| . (7.64)

States |k⟩ for which the left and right azimuthal quantum numbers are equal, m_l = m_r, are basis states for populations. The frequency indices n_k of (7.37) become n_k = (2f - I)(m_l - m_r). (7.65)

The factor (2f - I) = ±1 accounts for the different precession directions of angular momentum in the upper and lower hyperfine multiplets. So the analysis of Zeeman resonances differs from that of the isolated end resonance by having many more coherences included in the secular space, and in having multiple-quantum indices as large as |n_k| = [I], corresponding to a transition between the sublevels |αα⟩ and |α, −α⟩.

When appended to the program ending with Code 7.1 on page 103, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the steady-state values of the sublevel populations as the frequency of a radio-frequency field is tuned through the nominal Zeeman resonance frequency, the frequency of the [I]-quantum transition of the upper Zeeman multiplet. This frequency is strictly proportional to the value of the magnetic field B. Unlike the single-quantum transitions, it has no "quadratic shifts".

Code 7.2 L=fgl==fgr; % logical var. for Zeeman coherences and populations nk=2*(fgl-I).*mgl-2.*(fgr-I).*mgr; NL=diag(nk); % "spin"

Mp1=nk*ones(1,gg*gg)-ones(gg*gg,1)*nk’==1; % secular approx. matrices M0=nk*ones(1,gg*gg)-ones(gg*gg,1)*nk’==0; Mm1=nk*ones(1,gg*gg)-ones(gg*gg,1)*nk’==-1; tHmrC=Hp1C.*(Mp1+Mm1); % secular part of mag. res. interaction G0s=i*(HgC(L,L)+tHmrC(L,L))/hbar+Gmp*tAop(L,L); % no tuning om0=(Eg(1)-Eg(2*a+1))/(2*a*hbar); % resonance frequency rhosin=zeros(sum(L),nnu); % initialize secular density matrix for k=1:nnu Gs=G0s-i*(om0+om(k))*NL(L,L); % include tuning rhosin(:,k)=null(Gs); % evaluate steady state rhosin(:,k)=rhosin(:,k)/(Lp(L)’*rhosin(:,k)); end subplot(2,1,2); plot(om/(2*pi*1e3),real(rhosin(Lp(L),:))’); grid on xlabel(’Radiofrequency detuning in kHz’); ylabel(’Sublevel populations’);

The results of running the code ending with Code 7.2 are shown in Figure 7.2b. The static field B = 10 G is large enough to cause substantial quadratic splitting of the Zeeman multiplet with f = 1, so the two possible 1-quantum resonances, at approximate detunings of ±58 kHz, are well resolved. The peak value of the transverse oscillating field, B = 0.02 G, is large enough to nearly saturate the 1-quantum transitions, equalizing the populations of the pair of coupled sublevels. Some spin polarization is left at the 1-quantum resonance peaks, since all four sublevels are not equally populated. There is a much sharper 2-quantum transition at zero detuning. The field B is large enough to saturate the 2-quantum transition and also to nearly destroy the spin polarization of the atom, leaving each of the four sublevels with about one quarter of the population. Most of the resonance linewidth is due to radio-frequency power broadening, but from (7.46) we see that in the limit of negligible power broadening, the natural width of a resonance is γ / |n_k|. The "natural" frequency width is inversely proportional to the n 7.5.3 Push–Pull Pumping

When modulated light is used to excite CPT resonances of the “0–0 clock transition”, the size of the resonance signal is limited, because the circularly polarized light tends to pump the atoms into the end states, of maximum azimuthal angular momentum, leaving few atoms in the initial and final states, |a0⟩ and |b0⟩, of the 0–0 resonance. Jau et al. [49] pointed out that one can get around this problem with push–pull pumping, that is, pumping with light for which the circular polarization is modulated at the microwave frequency.

As a simple model of push–pull pumping, we assume the light is the sum of two modulated beams, both propagating along the z-axis, but with pure right and left circular polarization, respectively. The modulation peaks of one beam are delayed by half a modulation period, T = 2π/Ω, with respect to the other beam. We can model this situation by adding the high-pressure optical pumping produced by two beams of opposite circular polarization. According to (6.87), the combined optical pumping operator for the two beams is given by

2A_op = (A_sd,z / 2)f1(t)C + (A_sd,z / 2)f2(t). (7.66)

We have assumed D1 optical pumping with K = z/2 for beam 1 and K = −z/2 for beam 2. Beam 2 is delayed by half a coherence period with respect to beam 1, so modulation factors are related by f2(t) = f1(t + T/2). If we use the simple model (7.13) for f, we conclude that the Fourier components of the modulation factors are

f1(m) = f(m), f2(m) = f(m) e^{imπ} = (−1)^m f(m). (7.67)

Using (7.67) in (7.66), we find

A_op(m) = A_sd f(m) [1 + (−1)^m]/2 − (A_ex,z + 2iκ SC_z) f(m) [1 − (−1)^m]/4. (7.68)

The indices n for this problem have the values n = 0 for populations, |f m⟩⟨f m|, and n_c = 1 for |c⟩ = |a0⟩⟨b0| and n_c* = −1 for |c*⟩ = |b0⟩⟨a0|, the only nonnegligible coherences. So we need only retain terms A_op(m) with |m| ≤ 2 and we find

A_op = A_sd (M{0} + f(2)(M{2} + M{−2})) − (A_ex,z + 2iκ SC_z) (f(1)(M{1} + M{−1}))/2. (7.69)

When appended to the program ending with Code 7.2 on page 105, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate how the sublevel populations change when the light-modulation frequency of the push–pull pumping beam will induce a resonance between sublevels |a0⟩ and |b0⟩ of an alkali-metal atom (with half-integer nuclear spin quantum number I); see Figure 7.3.

Figure 7.3: (a) shows the 0–0 resonance modeled by (7.13) with α = 1. (b) and (d) show the analogous quantities for a very narrow pulse train with α = 200. As the width of the pulses approaches zero, the density matrix approaches a pure superposition state of |a0⟩ and |b0⟩. Relevant input parameters were I = 1.5, S = 0.5, J = 0.5, B = 10, Gmp = 1000, and κ = 0. The top curve shows the populations of the initial and final sublevels, |a0⟩ and |b0⟩, of the 0–0 transition. The bottom curve shows the population of each of the other sublevels. 108 7 Modulation

Code 7.3 (Continued) from the previous code.

Light Propagation

In this chapter we review some of the physics of light propagation in spin-polarized atomic vapors. For unpolarized vapors, the ideas of light propagation go back to pre-quantum-mechanical models of atoms as elastically bound electrons. Many of these early ideas, for example, oscillator strengths, were adapted and reinterpreted by quantum mechanics. Just as in Maxwell’s equations for classical optics, we will account for the interaction of light with atoms by introducing an electric dipole moment per unit volume (or per atom) that responds to the electric field of the light wave.

**8.1 Induced Electric Dipole Moment**

The pumping light will generate an oscillating electric dipole moment $\langle D \rangle$ in the atom. According to (5.5) this can be written as the sum of positive and negative frequency parts, \[ \langle D \rangle = \langle D^+ \rangle + \langle D^- \rangle.

\]

Using (6.7), we find \[ \langle D \rangle = \mathrm{Tr}\left[ (i\hbar) \dot{\rho}_{gg} D \right] = \mathrm{Tr}\left[ ((i\hbar) \dot{\rho}_{gg} W + W^\dagger (-i\hbar) \dot{\rho}_{gg}^\dagger) D \right] = \langle W^\dagger (i\hbar) W \rangle, \]

or \[ \langle D^\dagger \rangle = \mathrm{Tr}\left[ D^\dagger (i\hbar) \dot{\rho}_{eg} \right] = \mathrm{Tr}\left[ D^\dagger (W^\dagger (i\hbar) \dot{\rho}_{eg}^\dagger + (-i\hbar) \dot{\rho}_{eg}^\dagger W^\dagger) \right]

= \langle D^\dagger W^\dagger (i\hbar) W^\dagger \rangle.

\]

We can also write (8.2) and (8.3) as \[ \langle D \rangle = \mathcal{E}^* \langle \alpha_{eg} \rangle \mathcal{E} + \mathcal{E} \langle \alpha_{gg} \rangle \mathcal{E}^* \quad \text{and} \quad \langle D^\dagger \rangle = \langle \alpha_{eg}^\dagger \rangle \mathcal{E}^* + \mathcal{E}^* \mathcal{E} \langle \alpha_{gg}^\dagger \rangle.

\]

In deriving (8.4) from (8.2) and (8.3), we used (5.53) and (6.8) to write the ground-state and excited-state polarizability dyadics as \[ \alpha_{gg} = \mathrm{D} \left[ D^\dagger / \mathcal{E}_{egg} \right] \quad \text{and} \quad \alpha_{gg}^\dagger = \mathrm{D} \left[ D / \mathcal{E}_{egg}^\dagger \right] \mathrm{D}^\dagger, \]

\[ \alpha_{eg} = \mathrm{D} \left[ D^\dagger / \mathcal{E}_{egg} \right] \quad \text{and} \quad \alpha_{eg}^\dagger = \mathrm{D}^\dagger \left[ D / \mathcal{E}_{egg}^\dagger \right].

\]

The polarizability dyadics, \(\alpha_{gg}\) and \(\alpha_{eg}\), play a central role in optical pumping and light propagation, since they operate on both the spin states of the atoms and the polarization states of the light.

It will prove useful to write the polarizability operators as the sums of Hermitian and anti-Hermitian parts, \[ \alpha_{gg} = \alpha_{gg}^\prime + i \alpha_{gg}^{\prime\prime} \quad \text{and} \quad \alpha_{eg} = \alpha_{eg}^\prime + i \alpha_{eg}^{\prime\prime}.

\]

The Hermitian operators, \(\alpha_{gg}^\prime\), \(\alpha_{gg}^{\prime\prime}\), \(\alpha_{eg}^\prime\), and \(\alpha_{eg}^{\prime\prime}\) are \[ \alpha_{gg}^\prime = \frac{1}{2} (\alpha_{gg} + \alpha_{gg}^\dagger) \quad \text{and} \quad \alpha_{gg}^{\prime\prime} = \frac{1}{2i} (\alpha_{gg} - \alpha_{gg}^\dagger), \]

\[ \alpha_{eg}^\prime = \frac{1}{2} (\alpha_{eg} + \alpha_{eg}^\dagger) \quad \text{and} \quad \alpha_{eg}^{\prime\prime} = \frac{1}{2i} (\alpha_{gg} - \alpha_{eg}^\dagger).

\]

**Relation of Polarizability to Effective Hamiltonians**

From inspection of (8.5) and (8.6), we see that for monochromatic pumping light, the effective Hamiltonians (6.12) and (6.25) can be written as \[ \delta H_{gg} = -\mathcal{E}^* \cdot \alpha_{gg} \cdot \mathcal{E} \quad \text{and} \quad \delta H_{eg}^\dagger = -\mathcal{E} \cdot \alpha_{eg} \cdot \mathcal{E}^*.

\]

The light-shift operators become \[ \delta E_{gg} = -\mathcal{E}^* \cdot \alpha_{gg}^\prime \cdot \mathcal{E} \quad \text{and} \quad \delta E_{eg} = -\mathcal{E} \cdot \alpha_{eg}^\prime \cdot \mathcal{E}^*.

\]

The damping operators are \[ \delta \Gamma_{gg} = 2 \mathcal{E}^* \cdot \alpha_{gg}^{\prime\prime} \cdot \mathcal{E} \quad \text{and} \quad \delta \Gamma_{eg} = -2 \mathcal{E} \cdot \alpha_{eg}^{\prime\prime} \cdot \mathcal{E}^*.

\]

**Saturation**

The expectation values for the polarizability dyadics (8.5) and (8.6) for the saturated density matrix (6.39) are \[ \langle \alpha_{gg} \rangle = \frac{1}{g} \sum_{\mu, \mu'} \mu N_{\mu} \mu' N_{\mu'} \quad \text{and} \quad \langle \alpha_{eg} \rangle = -\frac{1}{g} \sum_{\mu, \mu'} N_{\mu} \mu' N_{\mu'}.

\]

Using (8.13) in (8.4), we see that for saturated atomic populations, \[ \langle D \rangle = 0.

\]

The light does not induce an electric dipole moment, because the dipole moment induced in the ground-state atoms is equal and opposite to that induced in the excited-state atoms.

**8.2 Absorption Cross Section**

Assuming that the time dependence of \(\langle D \rangle\) is given by \(e^{i(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{v} - \omega)t}\), we find that the average power dissipated in the atom by the optical electric field is \[ \frac{d}{dt} \langle P \rangle = \mathcal{E}^* \cdot \frac{d}{dt} \langle D \rangle = \mathcal{E}^* \cdot (-i\hbar \dot{\rho}_{eg}^\dagger) \mathcal{E} + \text{c.c.} = -i\omega_{egg} [\mathcal{E}^* \cdot \langle \alpha_{eg} \rangle \cdot \mathcal{E} + \mathcal{E} \cdot \langle \alpha_{gg} \rangle \cdot \mathcal{E}^*] + \text{c.c.} = -\hbar \omega_{egg} [\langle \delta \Gamma_{eg} \rangle - \langle \delta \Gamma_{gg} \rangle].

\]

In writing the last line of (8.15), we used (8.11) and (8.12). We define the photon absorption cross section as \[ \langle \sigma \rangle = \frac{\langle P \rangle}{S} = \frac{-\hbar \omega_{egg} [\langle \delta \Gamma_{eg} \rangle - \langle \delta \Gamma_{gg} \rangle]}{S}.

\]

The energy flux \(S = c |\mathcal{E}|^2 / 2\pi\) was defined by (5.61). From (8.15) we see that the power, \(\langle P \rangle\), absorbed from the beam has positive contributions owing to the absorption of photons of energy \(\hbar \omega_{egg}\) by ground-state atoms at the rate \(\langle \delta \Gamma_{gg} \rangle\) and negative contributions from stimulated emission of photons at the rate \(\langle \delta \Gamma_{eg} \rangle\). Normally, absorption prevails over stimulated emission, so the cross section is positive.

**Unpolarized Ground-State Atoms**

If the atoms are equally likely to be in any one of the \(g_{fg}\) sublevels of the ground state, the absorption cross section (8.16) will be \[ \sigma_{gg} = \frac{\hbar \omega_{egg}}{4\pi} \frac{\omega_{egg}}{g_{fg} c} \sum_{\mathbf{e}} \mathbf{e}^* \cdot \alpha_{gg}^{\prime\prime} \cdot \mathbf{e}.

\]

Noting that \[ \mathcal{E}_{\mu \mu'} = \frac{\hbar}{(\omega_{\mu \mu'} - \omega) + i\gamma/2}, \quad \text{with} \quad \omega_{\mu \mu'} = \omega_{egg} + (E_{\mu'} - E_{\mu})/\hbar + \mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{v}, \]

and taking \(\gamma\) to be real for simplicity, we find that the matrix elements of the polarization dyadic of (8.8) are \[ \alpha_{\mu' \mu'}^{\prime\prime} = \frac{\gamma}{2\hbar^2} \sum_{\mu} \frac{|\mu N_{\mu'}|^2}{(\omega - \omega_{\mu \mu'})^2 + (\gamma/2)^2}.

\]

Substituting (8.19) into (8.12) and (8.16) we find \[ \sigma_{gg} = \frac{\hbar \omega_{egg}}{g_{fg} c} \sum_{\mathbf{e}, \mu, \mu'} \frac{\mathbf{e}^* \cdot \Delta_{\mu \mu'} \Delta_{\mu \mu'}^\dagger \cdot \mathbf{e}}{(\omega - \omega_{\mu \mu'})^2 + (\gamma/2)^2} = \frac{2\pi r_e c_{fg} \gamma}{\hbar} \sum_{\mathbf{e}, \mu, \mu'} \frac{\mathbf{e}^* \cdot \Delta_{\mu \mu'} \Delta_{\mu \mu'}^\dagger \cdot \mathbf{e}}{(\omega - \omega_{\mu \mu'})^2 + (\gamma/2)^2}.

Sum over azimuthal quantum numbers for the expression on the left of (8.22) produces a multiple of the unit dyadic 1. The right side of (8.22) comes from noting that for a dyadic of the form \(M \mathbf{D} M_1\), where \(M\) is a scalar, we have \(3M \mathbf{D} \mathbf{x} \times \nabla M \times \mathbf{x}\). We can use angular momentum recoupling algebra, similar to that discussed in Section 11.3, to sum the matrix elements over the azimuthal quantum numbers to find \[ \sum_{m,m'} \left( \frac{2}{J S_1} \right) \Delta f m' I N m \left( \nabla \right) \Delta^\dagger f' N m' I f m \left( \nabla \right) = \left[ f' \right] \left[ f' N \right] f f' N I . \tag{8.23} \]

A simple code to evaluate the 6-j symbol was given by Code 5.1 on page 55. Substituting (8.22) and (8.23) into (8.20), we find \[ \sigma_{f f N} \mathbf{D} \sigma_{f f N}. \tag{8.24} \]

The contribution to the absorption cross section of transitions from the ground-state Zeeman multiplet \(f\) to the excited-state multiplet \(f_N\) is \[ \sigma_{f f_N} \mathbf{D} e^2 \left[ I \right] \left[ f \right] \left[ f_N \right] \left( \frac{J S_1}{2} \right)^2 \frac{\gamma}{C(\omega - \omega_{f_N f})^2} . \tag{8.25} \]

High-Pressure Limit. At high buffer-gas pressure, the coherence damping rate \(\gamma\) will be so large compared with differences in the optical Bohr frequencies and Doppler shifts that we can set \(\omega - \mu_N \mu'! \omega_{f e g}\) in the resonance denominator of (8.19). Then we can use (5.32) with (8.19) to find the absorption cross section for unpolarized atoms at high pressure, \[ \sigma_{f g g} \mathbf{D} e^2 \frac{\gamma}{2} \frac{1}{\gamma^2 + (\omega - \omega_{f e g})^2} . \tag{8.26} \]

When appended to the MATLAB Code 5.6 on page 65, the following statements will evaluate absorption cross sections for unpolarized atoms in their ground state, with and without Doppler broadening.

Code 8.1 ``` TK = input('Kelvin temperature, TK = '); sigv = (2*pi/lamJ)*sqrt(kB*TK*NA/MW); %Doppler variance Dnu1 = min(min(Heg/hP)) - sigv/2; Dnu2 = max(max(Heg/hP)) + sigv/2; ns = 2000; %number of sample frequencies Dnu = linspace(Dnu1, Dnu2, ns); %sample frequencies sigT = zeros(1,ns); sig0 = zeros(1,ns); for k=1:ns %Doppler broadened cross section z = -(Heg - hP*Dnu(k) - i*hbar/(2*te)) / (hbar*sigv*sqrt(2)); tWT = tV .* w(z) * i*sqrt(pi/2) / (hbar*sigv); dHgT = -tV' * tWT; sigT(k) = -(2*weg/Sl)*imag(trace(dHgT)/gg); %cross section for atoms at rest tW0 = tV ./ (Heg - hP*Dnu(k) - i*hbar/(2*te)); dHg0 = -tV' * tW0; sig0(k) = -(2*weg/Sl)*imag(trace(dHg0)/gg); end figure(1); clf subplot(2,1,1); plot(Dnu, sigT); grid on; ylabel('\sigma in cm^2')

subplot(2,1,2); plot(Dnu, sig0); grid on; ylabel('\sigma in cm^2')

xlabel('Detuning in GHz')

``` As illustrated in the code, we will think of the symbol \(\mathbf{w}\) as a matrix of values of the Faddeeva function, with element \(w_{\mu N \mu} \mathbf{D} w(z_{\mu N \mu})\) equal to the value of \(w\) for the corresponding value, \(z_{\mu N \mu}\), of the "dimensionless detuning" matrix, (6.117). Then the dot-slash products with the matrix of energy denominators \(E_{f e g g}\) are replaced by dot-star products with the matrix of values of the Faddeeva function: \[ \mathbf{W}_Q \mathbf{D} \mathbf{V}_Q \cdot / \mathbf{E}_{f e g g} ! \sqrt{\frac{i}{\pi 2}} \mathbf{V}_Q \cdot \star \mathbf{w} . \tag{8.27} \]
