## 8.4 Evolution of a Beam in Space and Time

The light used to optically pump atoms is itself modified as it passes through the spin-polarized atomic medium. If there is oscillating coherence in the atoms, the light can become modulated at the coherence frequency and its harmonics. For a medium with no free charge or current density, the Maxwell equations that couple the classical optical fields \(\mathbf{E}\) and \(\mathbf{D}\) with \(\mathbf{B} \mathbf{D} \mathbf{H}\) are \[ \nabla \times \mathbf{E} \mathbf{D} \left( - \right) \frac{1}{c} \frac{\partial \mathbf{B}}{\partial t} , \tag{8.28} \]

\[ \nabla \times \mathbf{B} \mathbf{D} \frac{1}{c} \frac{\partial \mathbf{D}}{\partial t} . \tag{8.29} \]

The Maxwell displacement vector \(\mathbf{D}\) can be written in terms of the polarization per unit volume \(\mathbf{P}\) as \[ \mathbf{D} \mathbf{D} \mathbf{E} \mathbf{C} 4\pi \mathbf{P}. \tag{8.30} \]

We consider plane waves of the form (7.16), propagating along the unit vector \(\mathbf{n}\), so that the electric-field amplitude is \[ \mathbf{E} \mathbf{D} \mathbf{E}_Q(p) e^{i(\mathbf{k}_p \cdot \mathbf{r} - \omega_p t)}. \tag{8.31} \]

The distance from the origin along the direction of propagation is \[ \zeta \mathbf{D} \mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{r} . \tag{8.32} \]

The temporal and spatial frequencies are \[ \omega_p \mathbf{D} \omega_0 \mathbf{C} p\Omega \quad \text{and} \quad k_p \mathbf{D} \omega_p / c , \quad \text{with} \quad p \mathbf{D} 0, \pm 1, \pm 2, \ldots \tag{8.33} \]

For simplicity, we consider a single modulation frequency \(\Omega\), but one can readily generalize these methods to more complicated modulation schemes. For unmodulated light, the only nonzero amplitude is \(E_Q(0)\). We assume that the amplitudes \(B\), \(D\), and \(P\) have expansions analogous to (8.31). Then we can replace the classical fields \(\mathbf{E}\), \(\mathbf{B}\), and so on by their Fourier amplitudes, \(E_Q(p)\), \(B_Q(p)\), and so on in (8.28) and (8.29), provided that we make the replacements \[ \nabla \mathbf{D} \left( \mathbf{C} i k_p \mathbf{n} \right) \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \mathbf{D} \left( - i\omega_p \right) , \tag{8.34} \]

and we find \[ \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial \zeta} \mathbf{C} i k_p \mathbf{n} \right) \times \mathbf{E}_Q(p) \mathbf{D} \frac{1}{c} \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \mathbf{C} i\omega_p \right) \mathbf{B}_Q(p) , \tag{8.35} \]

\[ \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial \zeta} \mathbf{C} i k_p \mathbf{n} \right) \times \mathbf{B}_Q(p) \mathbf{D} \frac{1}{c} \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \mathbf{C} i\omega_p \right) \mathbf{D}_Q(p) . \tag{8.36} \]

Multiplying both sides of (8.35) on the left by \(\nabla \times\), and using (8.36), we find \[ \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial \zeta} \mathbf{C} i k_p \right)^2 \mathbf{T} \cdot \mathbf{E}_Q(p) \mathbf{D} \frac{1}{c^2} \left( \frac{\partial}{\

## 8.5 First-Order Propagation Equation

For the conditions of optical pumping it is a good approximation to assume that

|∂_t E_Q(p)| ≪ |∂_t P_Q(p)| and |ω_p E_Q(p)| ≪ |∂_t P(p)|. (8.40)

Using (8.40) and equating the largest terms on the left and right of (8.39), we find

∂_z E_Q(p) = i2πk P_Q(p). (8.41)

The approximation (8.41) is analogous to the semiclassical Wentzel–Kramers–Brillouin approximation of quantum mechanics. As in (8.41), we will omit the transverse projection operator and let T E_Q(p) → E_Q(p), T P_Q(p) → P_Q(p) when no confusion is likely. There can be longitudinal components of the fields for anisotropically polarized vapor, but the longitudinal components are normally many orders of magnitude smaller than the transverse parts.

For simplicity, we assume that the light is weak enough for us to ignore contributions to the polarizability from excited atoms. Then the expectation value of the atomic polarizability (8.5) for light of frequency ω_p is

⟨α(ω_p)⟩ = Σ_m ⟨α(m)⟩ e^{-imΩt}. (8.42)

The amplitude is

⟨α(m)⟩ = Tr[α(ω_p) ρ(m)]. (8.43)

Here ρ(m) is the Fourier amplitude of the modulated ground-state density matrix (7.1). If the number density of polarized atoms is N, the dielectric polarization per unit volume is

P = N Σ_m ⟨α(m)⟩ E_Q(p) e^{i(k_p z - ω_p t + mΩt)} + P_Q(p) e^{i(k_p z - ω_p t)}. (8.44)

The Fourier amplitude is

P_Q(p) = N Σ_q ⟨α(p - q)⟩ E_Q(q) e^{i k'_0 (q - p) z}. (8.45)

Then for the steady state, the propagation equation (8.41) becomes

∂_z E_Q(p) = i2πN k Σ_q ⟨α(p - q)⟩ E_Q(q) e^{i k'_0 (q - p) z}. (8.46)

The oscillatory phase-matching factor e^{i k'_0 (q - p) z} is important for propagation distances comparable to or greater than the wavelength of the modulation, when the momentum conservation of the Raman scattering begins to be important.

## 8.6 Propagation of Weak Probe Light

In the next three sections we discuss three straightforward ways to detect the effects associated with optical pumping: (1) to observe changes in the attenuation of the pumping light, (2) to observe changes in the polarization of the transmitted light, or (3) to observe changes in the fluorescent light scattered out of the pumping beam.

A common method for monitoring optical pumping is to send a weak “probe” beam of light through the sample of atoms, and monitor its change in amplitude or polarization due to optical pumping. We shall assume that this monitoring beam has a negligible effect on the atoms. We shall also assume that the polarizability is quasistatic, so it does not change substantially over the propagation time through the sample of atoms. Then the probe propagation obeys

∂_z E_Q = i2πN k ⟨α^⊥⟩ E_Q, (8.47)

where the transverse polarizability is ⟨α^⊥⟩ = T ⟨α⟩ T.

The transverse polarizability has two spatial eigenvectors:

⟨α^⊥ | α λ) = α_λ |α λ). (8.48)

If we assume that the polarizability is spatially uniform, these modes provide a simple means of following the light propagation through the vapor. After propagation of a distance l, a probe electric field E_Q entering the vapor will become

E_Q = |α λ)(⟨α λ| E_Q) e^{i 2π N k α_λ l}. (8.49)

The imaginary parts of the eigenvalues α_λ produce attenuation of the probe beam, whereas differences between the real parts rotate the polarization axis. Both the real and the imaginary parts are quite sensitive to the atomic density matrix, so, in general, the polarization state of the light can be substantially altered by the optically pumped atoms.

## 8.7 Faraday Rotation

If the probe light is tuned far from the atomic resonance frequencies, so that the atomic hyperfine splittings, Zeeman shifts, and linewidths are negligible, the polarizability for alkali-metal atoms takes the simple form, analogous to (5.41),

⟨α⟩ = α_0 [1 + i ((2J-1)/[J]) (g_s/Δω) ⟨S_z⟩] = α_0 + i α_g ⟨S_z⟩, (8.50)

where α_0 ≃ -|d|^2/ℏ Δω is the scalar polarizability at the probe detuning Δω, and α_g is termed the gyrotropic polarizability. Then the eigenmodes of the transverse polarizability are

⟨α_g| J_z' = 0) = ∓ α_g. (8.51)

in a coordinate system whose z' direction is oriented along n. The probe electric field (8.49) becomes

E_Q = e^{i 2π N k α_0 l} [σ_- e^{i 2π N k α_g ⟨S_z⟩ l} + σ_+ e^{-i 2π N k α_g ⟨S_z⟩ l}] E_Q. (8.52)

This corresponds to a rotation of the polarization axis of the light by an angle

θ_FR = 2π N k α_g l ⟨n·S⟩ = -[(2J-1)e^2/(2m_e c [J] Δω/2π)] N l ⟨n·S⟩, (8.53)

where we have used (5.45) to express the polarizability in terms of atomic constants. This paramagnetic Faraday rotation of the polarization axis is proportional to the component of the electron spin polarization along the probe propagation axis. Note that the rotation angle in this large detuning limit is insensitive to the atomic line shape, which makes Faraday rotation angle measurements particularly attractive for making accurate absolute measurements of the atom density and/or polarization.

## 8.8 Specific Absorption

A convenient, dimensionless measure of the absorption of light is the specific absorption ⟨η⟩, which we define δΓfgg = δΓfeg hσi / σfgg, hηi = D. (8.54)

From (8.54) we see that the specific absorption is the ratio of the amount of light absorbed by a polarized atom to the amount of light that would be absorbed if the atom were in its ground state and completely unpolarized. The specific absorption can be greater than 1 if most of the atoms are pumped to the most strongly absorbing sublevels of the ground state. The specific absorption can be negative if there is a population inversion and the atoms amplify rather than attenuate the pumping light. The specific absorption is also the ratio of hσi, the absorption cross section (8.16) of optically pumped atoms, to σfgg, the absorption cross section (8.17) of unpolarized atoms in their ground state.

In accordance with (6.15) and (6.28) we find

hηi = Tr[ρ (Γfeg Aeeg − Γfgg Aggg)] / Γfgg = ⟨η|ρ⟩, (8.55)

where the density matrix |ρ⟩ without optical coherences was given by (6.55), and where the row vector for specific absorption is

⟨η| = (Γfeg/Γfgg) (⟨1feg| Aeeg, 1feg|A ggg). (8.56)

## 8.9 Fluorescent Light

For pumping with modulated light, the pumping matrices Aeeg and Aggg will be time-dependent. Also, for either magnetic resonance or coherent population trapping resonance experiments, the steady-state density matrix |ρ⟩ may have components oscillating at the drive frequencies or their harmonics.

As discussed extensively in the literature [1], the rate of emission of fluorescent photons of polarization vector ελ into the solid angle ΔΩ is

hΔΦλi = Tr[ρ Aeeg ΔΦλ]. (8.57)

The emission operator is

ΔΦλ = (ΔΩ ω0^3 / (2π c^3)) (ελ† · D) (D† · ελ) = (ΔΩ Γfgeg / (8π)) [D††W e−λ eλ]. (8.58)

To simplify the notation of (8.58) and subsequent formulas we will define the double-dot product of dyadics A and B by

A : B = Tr[A* · B] = Σ_ij A_ij B_ji. (8.59)

The double-dot product is a scalar with respect to spatial rotations. The electric dipole moment operators, D and D†, were defined by (5.6) and (5.7). Their dimensionless versions, Δ and Δ†, were defined by (5.17) and (5.20). For fluorescent photons propagating along the unit vector n, the two independent polarization vectors, ε1 and ε2, can be chosen to be orthogonal to each other and to n, such that the three unit vectors form an orthonormal basis and the unit dyadic is

ε1⊗ε1 + ε2⊗ε2 + n⊗n = 1. (8.60)

The operator for the total emission rate of photons per solid angle is

dΦ/dΩ = (1/ΔΩ) Σ_λ ΔΦλ = (Γfgeg / (8π)) [Δ†Δ : W(1−n⊗n)] = (Γfgeg / (4π)) [1 − ((−1)^J / √(6π)) ⟨[J]^{1/2} : T^{JJ}_2 : Y_2⟩]. (8.61)

Here Y_2 · T^{JJ}_2 is a scalar product like that of (5.34) between the spherical harmonic Y_2(n) and the spherical basis tensor T^{JJ}_2m for excited atoms, with the electronic angular momentum quantum number J. The atom decays to a ground state of electronic angular momentum quantum number S. To evaluate the second line of (8.61) we used the multipole expansion of Δ†Δ given by (5.33), and we noted that

n · (O^{10}) · n = p, (8.62)

n · (O^{1m}) · n = 0, (8.63)

n · (O^{2m}) · n = −(2/√(6)) Y_2m(n). (8.64)

9 Radiation Forces

An atom experiences forces from the pumping light. The force has a mean value associated with the mean scattering rate of photons and with gradients in the light shift. There is also a fluctuating component of the force that comes from the irregular recoils of the atom as it absorbs and emits photons propagating in various directions. This causes the atom to make a random walk in momentum space. The force depends strongly on the velocity of the atom with respect to the laser beam, and this velocity dependence plays an important role for laser cooling of atoms. The force also depends strongly on the applied electric and magnetic fields, which can be used to provide a controlled spatial dependence to the light force, allowing for stable confinement, or trapping, of the atoms. As we illustrate in this chapter, the cooling and confinement forces, as well as the fluctuations in the force, are dramatically enhanced by optical pumping processes. The discovery and exploitation of optical pumping effects in laser cooling resulted in the award of the 1997 Nobel Prize to Steven Chu [50], Claude Cohen-Tannoudji [51], and William Phillips [52].

## 9.1 Mean Force

A classical electric field E and a classical magnetic field B will exert a classical force ⟨Fi⟩ on the electric charge distribution, ⟨ρ⟩, and the current distribution ⟨ji⟩,

⟨Fi⟩ = ∫ d^3r ⟨ρ(r)⟩ E(r) + ⟨j(r)⟩ × B(r). (9.1)

Expanding the fields E and B as a power series around center of the atom and retaining only the first, nonvanishing terms of the expansion, we find that the force is

⟨Fi⟩ = ⟨D⟩ · ∇E + ⟨Ṗ⟩ × B + ... (9.2)

The electric dipole moment and its derivative are

⟨D⟩ = ∫ r ⟨ρ(r)⟩ d^3r, (9.3)

and

⟨Ṗ⟩ = d⟨D⟩/dt = ∫ ⟨j(r)⟩ d^3r. (9.4)

The first term on the right of (9.2) arises because an inhomogeneous electric field can pull harder on the positive end of the electric dipole moment ⟨D⟩ than it pushes on the negative end. The second term of (9.2) is the net magnetic force on the currents inside the atom. The first and second terms can have comparable magnitudes for resonant optical excitation of an atom.

It is convenient to use Faraday’s law, $$\nabla \times \mathbf{E} = -\frac{\partial \mathbf{B}}{\partial t}, \quad (9.5)$$ to write the force due to the first two terms on the right of (9.2) as $$\mathbf{F} = \mathbf{D} \cdot \nabla \mathbf{E} - \mathbf{D} \times (\nabla \times \mathbf{E}) + \mathbf{D} \times \mathbf{B}. \quad (9.6)$$ The third term of (9.6), being the time derivative of a bounded function, will average to zero after a few optical cycles. The sum of the first two terms gives the time-averaged force, $$\langle \mathbf{F} \rangle = \sum_{ij} x_i \langle D_j \frac{\partial E_i}{\partial x_j} \rangle. \quad (9.7)$$ Here $x_i$ is a unit vector along the $i$th axis of a Cartesian coordinate system, $D_j = \mathbf{D} \cdot \mathbf{x}_j$ and $E_i = \mathbf{E} \cdot \mathbf{x}_i$. Using (5.54) and (8.1)–(8.3) with (9.7), and dropping terms that oscillate at twice the laser frequency $\omega$, we find that the mean force can be written as $$\langle \mathbf{F} \rangle = \langle \mathbf{F}_{fg} \rangle + \langle \mathbf{F}_{eg} \rangle. \quad (9.8)$$ The ground-state and excited-state contributions to the mean force are $$\langle \mathbf{F}_{fg} \rangle = \langle f | \nabla V | g \rangle \rho_{fg} + \text{c.c.} \quad (9.9)$$ and $$\langle \mathbf{F}_{eg} \rangle = -\langle g | \nabla V | f \rangle \rho_{eg} + \text{c.c.} \quad (9.10)$$ One can readily verify that for the saturated density matrix of (6.39), the forces from the excited-state and ground-state components of the density matrix cancel and the total force is $\langle \mathbf{F} \rangle = 0$. For the weak optical pumping often used for optical traps, the force (9.10) from excited atoms can be negligibly small compared with the force (9.9) from ground-state atoms.

## 9.2 Forces from Monochromatic Light

Consider monochromatic light of the form $$\mathbf{E} = \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k}) e^{i(\mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{r} - \omega t)} + \text{c.c.} \quad (9.11)$$ This represents light of temporal frequency $\omega$, propagating with spatial frequencies $k = \omega n / c$, along the directions of various unit vectors $\mathbf{n}$.

Substituting (9.11) into (9.7) and using (8.13), we find that the contribution to the force from ground-state atoms is $$\langle \mathbf{F}_{fg} \rangle = -\nabla \langle \delta E_{fg} \rangle + \hbar \sum_{\mathbf{k}' \mathbf{k}} (\mathbf{k} + \mathbf{k}') \langle \delta \Gamma_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle e^{i(\mathbf{k} - \mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{r}}. \quad (9.12)$$ The first term on the right of (9.12) is a conservative force, the negative spatial gradient of a potential. The potential is the light shift, $$\langle \delta E_{fg} \rangle = \sum_{\mathbf{k}' \mathbf{k}} \langle \delta E_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle e^{i(\mathbf{k} - \mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{r}}, \quad (9.13)$$ with the Fourier amplitudes $$\langle \delta E_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle = -\frac{1}{\hbar} \mathbf{E}^*(\mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{\alpha}_{fgg'} \cdot \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k}). \quad (9.14)$$ The second term on the right of (9.12) is a dissipative force due to the scattering of photons. It is proportional to elements of the light-absorption operator $$\langle \delta \Gamma_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle = \frac{2}{\hbar} \text{Im} \left[ \mathbf{E}^*(\mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{\alpha}_{fgg''} \cdot \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k}) \right]. \quad (9.15)$$ The dissipative force is the net result of momentum impulses of magnitude $\hbar \mathbf{k}$, acquired or lost when the atom absorbs or emits a photon of spatial frequency $\mathbf{k}$.

The contribution to the force from excited-state atoms is $$\langle \mathbf{F}_{eg} \rangle = -\nabla \langle \delta E_{eg} \rangle - \hbar \sum_{\mathbf{k}' \mathbf{k}} (\mathbf{k} + \mathbf{k}') \langle \delta \Gamma_{feg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle e^{i(\mathbf{k} - \mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{r}}. \quad (9.16)$$ The light shift is $$\langle \delta E_{eg} \rangle = \sum_{\mathbf{k}' \mathbf{k}} \langle \delta E_{feg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle e^{i(\mathbf{k} - \mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{r}}, \quad (9.17)$$ with the Fourier amplitude $$\langle \delta E_{feg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle = -\frac{1}{\hbar} \mathbf{E}^*(\mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{\alpha}_{feg'} \cdot \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k}). \quad (9.18)$$ The elements of the light-emission operator are $$\langle \delta \Gamma_{feg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle = \frac{2}{\hbar} \text{Im} \left[ \mathbf{E}^*(\mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{\alpha}_{feg''} \cdot \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k}) \right]. \quad (9.19)$$

## Optical Earnshaw Theorem

If we take the spatial divergence of (9.12), we find $$\nabla \cdot \langle \mathbf{F}_{fg} \rangle = -\nabla^2 \langle \delta E_{fg} \rangle + \hbar \sum_{\mathbf{k}' \mathbf{k}} (\mathbf{k} + \mathbf{k}') \cdot \left[ \nabla \langle \delta \Gamma_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle e^{i(\mathbf{k} - \mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{r}} \right]. \quad (9.20)$$ We see that the divergence of the scattering forces, represented by the second term of (9.20), vanishes if $\nabla \langle \delta \Gamma_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle = 0$, that is, if the Fourier components $\langle \delta \Gamma_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle$ of the light-absorption operator are independent of position. Position independence is guaranteed if the ground state has only one sublevel, that is, if the atom has angular momentum quantum number $f = 0$. Then $\langle \delta \Gamma_{fgg}(\mathbf{k}', \mathbf{k}) \rangle = (2 \alpha_{00} / \hbar) \text{Im} [\mathbf{E}^*(\mathbf{k}') \cdot \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k})]$ is a position-independent scalar, and we will have $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{F} = 0$ for the part of the force due to photon scattering. A point of equilibrium, where $\mathbf{F} = 0$, can be stable only if there are positive restoring coefficients along all principal axes, that is, only if $\partial F_x / \partial x < 0$, $\partial F_y / \partial y < 0$, and $\partial F_z / \partial z < 0$. But this is impossible for a force with zero divergence. This is the essence of the optical Earnshaw theorem [53], which states that photon-scattering forces cannot stably confine an atom in three dimensions if the ground state of the atom is nondegenerate and the dielectric polarizability $\alpha_{00}$ is a position-independent scalar. An atom with many ground-state sublevels can be optically pumped in such a way that its spin polarization will vary with position to make $\nabla \cdot \mathbf{F} < 0$ and make it possible to optically trap atoms in three dimensions [53–55].

## 9.3 Forces in Magneto-Optical Traps

As an example of radiation forces, and of nearly collision free optical pumping, we will discuss the physics of magneto-optical traps (MOTs) [55]. To provide three-dimensional confinement, a MOT usually has three pairs of monochromatic, counterpropagating beams, for example, beams along the $x$, $y$, and $z$ axes of a Cartesian coordinate system. In most MOT situations, the lasers are intentionally kept weak enough to neglect stimulated emission, and to permit maximum cooling of the trapped atoms.

Figure 9.1 shows the energy levels of a $^{87}$Rb atom, with $I = 3/2$, in a small magnetic field. Also shown are the counterpropagating beams of a strong trap laser, which confines the atoms along the $z$ direction. A much weaker repump laser is normally present as well. The frequency $\omega$ of the trap laser is tuned a few linewidths less than the zero-field resonance frequency for the cycling transition from the $5S$ ground-state multiplet with spin quantum number $f = I + 1/2 = 2$.

The trap laser weakly drives off-resonant transitions to excited-state multiplets with f = I ± 1/2 = 2 and f = I ± 1/2 = 1, both of which can decay to the lower ground-state multiplet. To cope with these losses, the weak repump laser is frequency-tuned to cause maximum pumping from the lower ground-state multiplet with f = I ± 1/2 to the excited-state hyperfine multiplet with f = I + 1/2 = 2, where a large fraction of the atoms radiate back to the ground-state multiplet with f = I + 1/2 = 2, where they can again scatter photons from the trap laser. Thus, atoms spend most of their time in the multiplet with f = I + 1/2 = 2, where they strongly scatter the light of the trap laser. The trap laser keeps the atoms close to the origin of the coordinate system as a result of its mean force, and it also cools the atoms through the fluctuating components of its force.

Let the center of the trap be at the origin of a coordinate system, where z = 0. "Anti-Helmholtz" coils produce a magnetic field. For small displacements along the z axis, the field is well described by B = βz, where β = ∂B_z/∂z. (9.21) The field-gradient coefficient, β, which can be taken to be a constant for small displacements z, is proportional to the current in the coils, to the number of windings, and to geometrical factors. At the center of the trap, where B = 0, the atoms will be equally likely to scatter up-going or down-going photons. Let β > 0 and assume that the atoms move down into a region of negative magnetic field. Then there will be Zeeman shifts of the ground-state and excited-state sublevels of the cycling transition like those indicated in Figure 9.1b, such that σ⁺ transitions are more in tune with the "red-shifted" trap laser than σ⁻ transitions. Since we are assuming light of helicity η = 1, the σ⁺ transitions are associated with up-going photons, which carry positive momentum and therefore impart an upward (restoring) force to the atoms as they are scattered. This upward force is further enhanced by optical pumping, which will try to transfer the atoms to the right end state with |f, m⟩ = |2, 2⟩, which has the maximum scattering rate for σ⁺ light. For negative helicity, the momentum from the most strongly scattered photons will "detrap" the atoms. We see that for a trapping situation, the helicity η must have the same sign as β = ∂B_z/∂z.

MOTs also provide strong velocity damping, which narrows the momentum distribution of the atoms and cools them. If the atom has a positive velocity, v > 0, along the z axis, it will be more in resonance with the down-going beam, and the scattering of these down-going photons will tend to drive the atomic velocity back toward zero velocity. Because of the circular polarization of the down-going beam, the atoms will be pumped into a strongly absorbing end state, and this enhances the "viscous damping force". The force would reverse if v < 0, so the force always tries to drive the atomic velocity to zero. For zero magnetic field, this "viscous damping force" is independent of the helicity. As we will discuss in more detail below, strong transverse spin coherences are generated in atoms with nearly zero velocity, and these coherences are associated with even more rapid changes of the force with velocity [50–52].

Turning to a detailed analysis of the mean forces in a MOT, we consider the simple case of beams counter-propagating along the z axis. The three-dimensional case can be analyzed in a similar way, and to a first approximation, one can vectorially add the forces from three MOT beams counter-propagating along the three coordinate axes. The electric-field amplitude of the light at position z is E = E_q(r) e^{i(r k z - ω t)} where E_q = (1/√2) E_q [ (x + i η y) e^{i(kz - ωt)} + (x - i η y) e^{i(-kz - ωt)} ] = E_q [ x cos ψ + y sin ψ ] e^{-iωt}. (9.22) Here k = ω/c is the spatial frequency of the light, the up beam has the direction quantum number r = 1 and the down beam has r = -1. The laser frequency ω is often "red-detuned" to be a few radiative linewidths smaller than the resonance frequency of the atomic transition that is used for cooling or trapping. Equation 9.22 represents beams with perfect circular polarization and with the same helicity η, which we take to be η = 1 or η = -1. At any given instant of time, t, the tip of the vector E, of amplitude E_q, makes a "corkscrew" about the z axis. The rotation angle is proportional to z and is given by ψ = -η k z = -η k v t. (9.23) From inspection of (9.22) we see that the electric-field amplitudes for the up and down beams can be written as E_q(r) = - (1/√2) η E_q ε_{rη}. (9.24) The spherical basis vectors ε were defined by (5.9).

Using (9.22), we find that the interaction V of (5.53) becomes V = -E · D† = Σ V_q(r) e^{i(r k z - ω t)}, with V_q(r) = - (1/√2) r η E_q ε_{rη} · D†. (9.25)

The analysis of the simplest MOT pumping situation that we are considering here is facilitated by using the total azimuthal angular momentum operator of the atoms, F_z = F_{z}^{gg} + F_{z}^{eg}. (9.26) Since we assume that the externally applied magnetic field is directed along the z axis, we must have [F_fgg, H_fgg] = 0 and [F_feg, H_feg] = 0, where the ground-state and excited-state hyperfine Hamiltonians H_fgg and H_feg were given by (2.10) and (2.12). The Hamiltonians are implicit functions of the position z, because of the position dependence of the magnetic field, (9.21). The energy basis states, |μ⟩ of (3.14) and |μ_N⟩ of (3.19), will therefore be eigenstates of F with integer or half-integer azimuthal eigenvalues m_μ and m_μN, that is, F_fgg |μ⟩ = m_μ |μ⟩ and F_feg |μ_N⟩ = m_μN |μ_N⟩. (9.27)

We can write the angular momentum operators as F_feg = Σ m_μN |μ_N⟩⟨μ_N| and F_fgg = Σ m_μ |μ⟩⟨μ|. (9.28)

μ_N μ

Because of the azimuthal symmetry, the matrix elements of (9.25) have the selection rule ⟨μ_N|V_Q(r)|ν⟩ = ⟨μ_N|V_Q(r)|ν⟩ δ_{m_μN, η + m_ν}. (9.29)

Using (9.27) and (9.29), we see that ⟨μ_N| e^{-iψF_z} V_Q(r) e^{iψF_z} |ν⟩ = ⟨μ_N|V_Q(r)|ν⟩ e^{-iψ(m_μN - m_ν)} = ⟨μ_N|V_Q(r)|ν⟩ e^{i r k z}. (9.30)

Then the time-dependent interaction (9.25) becomes V = e^{-iΦ} V_Q e^{iΦ}, (9.31)

where the phase operator, the analog of (5.64), is Φ = ωtQ + ψF. (9.32)

The optical spin operator Q was defined by (2.16). The time-independent amplitude of (9.31) is V_Q = V_Q(r) = [E_x]_†. (9.33)

Not surprisingly, (9.31) shows that the interaction (9.25) corkscrews around the z axis, just like the electric field E of (9.22).

The dark part, (5.73), of the rotating-frame Hamiltonian becomes K = H_feg + H_fgg - ħ(ΔωQ - ηk v_F). (9.34)

The expression (9.34) for the counter-propagating MOT light beams differs from the expression (5.76) for a single light beam by having the Doppler shift term, -ηk v_F, rather than -k·v_Q, and variants of the subsequent code intended to model single pump beams should be modified accordingly. As in (6.8), we can define the operator W = V_Q / (E_fegg), where E_fegg = H_fegg - ħ Δω - ηk v_F F_fegg + i γ. (9.35)

The matrix F_fegg, which accounts for opposite Doppler shifts for the two beams, has elements analogous to those of H_fegg of (5.90)–(5.93), for example, (F_fegg)_{μN μ} = ⟨μ_N|F_feg|μ_N⟩ - ⟨μ|F_fgg|μ⟩ = m_μN - m_μ. (9.36)

The matrices V_Q and W_Q can be used to calculate the various optical pumping matrices given in Chapter 6, and the steady-state density matrix of the atoms in the MOT trap will be given by (1.34) and (1.35), with the damping operator given by G = i H_fgg / ħ + ηk v_F F_fgg / ħ + Γ_fgg A. (9.37)

We can substitute (9.31) into (9.9) to find the magneto-optical force F = - ⟨(∇V)† W + c.c.⟩ = -⟨( e^{-iΦ} V_Q e^{iΦ} )† e^{-iΦ} W_Q e^{iΦ}⟩ + c.c.

= -⟨ i [∇Φ, V_Q]† W_Q ⟩ + c.c.

= i ηk z [F_z, V_Q]† W_Q + c.c. = i ηk z (W_Q† [F_z, V_Q] + c.c.) (9.38)

When appended to the program ending with Code 5.4 on page 58, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the quantities needed for the quasi-steady-state analysis given in Chapter 6: the effective Hamiltonians δH_feg and δH_fgg, denoted by dHe and dHg, the light-damping operators δΓ_feg and δΓ_fgg, denoted by dGme and dGmg, the mean pumping rates Γ_feg and Γ_fgg, denoted by Gmpe and Gmpg, the matrices A_feg, A_feg, A_fge, and A_fgg, denoted by Apee, Apgg, Apeg, and Apge, and the matrices A_dp and A_rp, denoted by Adp and Arp.

Code 9.1 St = 1e4 * input('one-way trap flux in mW/cm^2, St = '); % to erg/(s cm^2)

eta = input('helicity, eta = '); v = input('velocity in cm/s, v = '); tE = sqrt(4*pi*St/c); % field amplitude in esu/cm^2 tV = -D * Dj(:,:,1)' * tE; % interaction for field along x axis Dnut = 1e6 * input('trap detuning in MHz, Dnut = '); Dw = (Eef(1)-Egf(1))/hbar + 2*pi*Dnut; Hee = Ee * ones(1,ge) - ones(ge,1) * Ee'; Heg = Ee * ones(1,gg) - ones(ge,1) * Eg'; Fee = me * ones(1,ge) - ones(ge,1) * me'; Feg = me * ones(1,gg) - ones(ge,1) * mg'; Eeg = Heg - hbar*(Dw - keg*v*eta*Feg + 0.5i/te); % complex energy differences tW = tV ./ Eeg; dHg = -tV' * tW; dHe = tV * tW'; dGmg = (dHg - dHg') * i / hbar; dGme = (dHe - dHe') * i / hbar; cdGmg = dGmg(:); rdGmg = cdGmg'; % row and column vectors cdGme = dGme(:); rdGme = cdGme'; Gmpg = rdGmg * cPg / gg; Gmpe = rdGme * cPe / ge; Apgg = (flat(dHg) - sharp(dHg)') / (-i*hbar*Gmpg); Apee = (flat(dHe) - sharp(dHe)') / (-i*hbar*Gmpe); Apeg = (kron(conj(tV), tW) - kron(conj(tW), tV)) / (i*hbar*Gmpg); Apge = (kron(tW.', tV') - kron(tV.', tW')) / (i*hbar*Gmpe); Adp = Apgg; Arp = (Asge/te) * (diag(i*Hee(:)/hbar + i*eta*keg*v*Fee(:) + 1/te))^(-1) * Apeg; Aop = Adp - Arp; HgC = flat(Hg) - sharp(Hg); FzgC = flat(Fzg) - sharp(Fzg); G = i*(HgC/hbar + eta*keg*v*FzgC) + Gmpg*Aop; L = fgl == fgr; % log. var. to neglect hfs coherences Lp = fgl == fgr & mgl == mgr; % log. var. for populations cLp = Lp(L); % log. var. for pops. in compactified space t = 10/Gmpg; % 10 optical pumping cycles rhoct = expm(-t*G(L,L)) * cPg(L) / gg; rhocin = null(G(L,L)); rhocin = rhocin / (rPg(L) * rhocin); % steady state Fmot = i*eta*keg*tW'*(Fze*tV - tV*Fzg)/kB; cFmot = Fmot(:); rFmot = cFmot'; Ft = rFmot(L) * rhoct; Ft = Ft + conj(Ft); % early-time force Fin = rFmot(L) * rhocin; Fin = Fin + conj(Fin); % steady-state force

We made use of the identities (5.94) to change some of the dot transforms to commutator transforms. In evaluating these coupling matrices, we used (9.33) for V_Q and (9.35) for W_Q. The MOT forces were evaluated with (9.38).

The sublevel populations and forces evaluated by the code ending with Code 9.1 on page 128 can be plotted with the following statements to generate Figure 9.2.

Code 9.2 figure(1); clf; mm = -a:1:a; ma = mg(1:2*a+1); mb = mg(2*a+2:gg); [xa, ja] = sort(ma); [xb, jb] = sort(mb); rho = rea rhoct(cLp); %early populations rhoa=rho(1:2*a+1); rhob=rho(2*a+2:gg); subplot(2,1,1)

P=[rhoa(ja),[0;rhob(jb);0]]; bar(mm,P,0.5)

ylabel(’\rho(t)’)

grid on; hold on; title([’F = ’ num2str(Ft) ’ k_B K/cm’] )

rho=real(rhocin(cLp)); %steady-state populations rhoa=rho(1:2*a+1); rhob=rho(2*a+2:gg); subplot(2,1,2)

P=[rhoa(ja),[0;rhob(jb);0]]; bar(mm,P,0.5); grid on; ylabel(’\rho(\infty)’); xlabel(’Azimuthal quantum number, m’)

title([’F = ’ num2str(Fin) ’ k_B K/cm’] )

Figure 9.2 (a) Population distribution of 87Rb early time, approximately three eighths of the atoms remain equally distributed in the lower hyperfine multiplet, just as they were at time t D 0. (b) Population distribution for the true steady state of 87Rb atoms. Most of the atoms in the upper hyperfine multiple have been “burned out” by the trap laser, and the force is a tiny fraction of its value for the early-time, quasisteady state of (a).

9.3.1 Repump Lasers For MOTs, the atoms are normally pumped with D2 light, detuned a few megahertz below the zero-field resonance frequency of the transition from the ground-state multiplet of total angular momentum quantum number f D I C 1/2 of the ground state to the multiplet with total angular momentum quantum number f N D I C 3/2 of the excited state. This type of transition is often referred to as a “cycling transition” since most of the atoms return to the initial multiplet with f D I C 1/2 by spontaneous or simulated emission. The atomic populations quickly reach a quasi steady state, similar to that shown in Figure 9.2a. However, the trap laser weakly excites atoms into excited-state multiplets with f N D I C 1/2 and f N D I (cid:2) 1/2 because there is some overlap of the laser frequency with the edges of the corresponding absorption lines. These excited multiplets can decay to the ground-state multiplet with f D I (cid:2) 1/2. As a result of this “Raman scattering”, the trap laser slowly pumps atoms into the lower multiplet with f D I (cid:2) 1/2. If no repump laser is used to reverse this, most of the atoms will eventually be pumped into the lower multiplet, where they scatter light very weakly since the absorption lines from the lower multiplet are very far from resonance with the frequency of the trap laser. This true steady state of the atoms with no repump laser is shown in Figure 9.2a. The effect of adding a weak repump laser is shown in Figure 9.3, which was generated by adding the following segment of code to that ending with Code 9.2 on page 129, followed by another copy of Code 9.2 for displaying the results.

Code 9.3 fr=input(’fractional intensity of repump laser, fr = ’); tV=sqrt(fr)*tV; %attenuate interaction Dw=(Eef(2)-Egf(2))/hbar; %repump detuning Eeg=Heg-hbar*(Dw-keg*v*eta*Feg+0.5*i/te); tW=tV./Eeg; dHg=-tV’*tW; dHe=tV*tW’; dGmg=(dHg-dHg’)*i/hbar; dGme=(dHe-dHe’)*i/hbar; cdGmg=dGmg(:); rdGmg=cdGmg’; %row and column vectors Gmpg=rdGmg*cPg/gg; Gmpe=rdGme*cPe/ge; Apgg=(flat(dHg)-sharp(dHg)’)/(-i*hbar*Gmpg); Apee=(flat(dHe)-sharp(dHe)’)/(-i*hbar*Gmpe); Apeg=(kron(conj(tV),tW)-kron(conj(tW),tV))/(i*hbar*Gmpg); Apge=(kron(tW.’,tV’)-kron(tV.’,tW’))/(i*hbar*Gmpe); Adp=Apgg; Arp=(Asge/te)*(diag(i*Hee(:)/hbar+i*eta*keg*v*Fee(:)+1/te))^(-1)*Apeg; Aop=Adp-Arp; G=G+Gmpg*Aop; %add repumping rhoct=expm(-t*G(L,L))*cPg(L)/gg; rhocin=null(G(L,L)); rhocin=rhocin/(rPg(L)*rhocin); %steady state Fmot=Fmot+i*eta*keg*tW’*(Fze*tV-tV*Fzg)/kB; cFmot=Fmot(:); rFmot=cFmot’; Ft=rFmot(L)*rhoct; Ft=Ft+conj(Ft); %early-time force Fin=rFmot(L)*rhocin; Fin=Fin+conj(Fin); %steady-state force

The code ending with Code 9.3 can be used to calculate how the force depends on the atomic velocity or on the magnetic field, by adding appropriate looping statements for the velocity or the field. For example, Figure 9.4 shows a rapid change of force with velocity for |v| < 5 cm/s. This is associated with the transverse coherence that can be created at very low velocities when the light appears to be linearly polarized to the slowly moving atoms. The transverse coherence has the same corkscrew pattern along the z axis as the electric field of the pumping light. For larger values of the velocity, there is little transverse coherence, and the force is less sensitive to changes in the atom velocity. The range of velocities where the coherence is important is proportional to the intensity of the trap laser. The scattering rate for the polarized atoms is nearly twice as large as that of unpolarized atoms because the repump laser has pumped most of the atoms from the lower Zeeman multiplet of the ground state to the upper multiplet, and because the trap laser has Azimuthal quantum number, m

Figure 9.3 This figure was generated with the same parameters as used for generation of Figure 9.2, except that a repump laser was used to prevent accumulation of atoms in the ground-state multiplet with f = I - 1/2. The intensity of the repump laser is 1% that of the trap laser and the frequency is that of the zero-field transition from the ground-state multiplet with f = I - 1/2 to the excited state multiplet with f' = I + 3/2. The late-pumped most of the atoms in the upper multiplet to the strongly scattering end states, with distributions of sublevel populations similar to those in Figure 9.3.

The time distribution shown in (b) has most of the atoms in the upper hyperfine multiplet with f = I + 1/2. Since most atoms are in the upper multiplet, where they strongly scatter the beam of the trap laser, the force is nearly twice as large for the true steady state as for the quasi steady state in (a), where three eighths of the atoms in the lower multiplet hardly interact with the strong trap laser.

In Figure 9.5 we show how the force on atoms with zero velocity depends on the magnetic field B. As for the velocity dependence, the force changes very rapidly with magnetic field near zero velocity because of the generation of transverse atomic coherence at fields close to zero.

## 9.4 Pointing Probability

Traditional plots of sublevel populations, like those in Figures 9.2 and 9.3, do not characterize the density matrix very well for small velocities v or for small fields B, where the atoms can have large transverse coherences. To get more insight into the nature of the spin polarization in such cases, it is useful to introduce the “pointing probability”, ⟨W⟩ = ⟨n|ρ|n⟩, or W = |n⟩⟨n|. (9.39)

The unit vector, n = x sinθ cosφ + y sinθ sinφ + z cosθ, (9.40)

is parameterized by the colatitude angle θ and the azimuthal angle φ. The ket vector |n⟩ is defined by |n⟩ = e^{-iφF_z} e^{-iθF_y} |z⟩, or |n⟩ = |a m⟩ = a . (9.41)

m_a Here F_z = F_z(f_gg) and F_y = F_y(f_gg) are z and y projections of the ground-state angular momentum operator. The coefficients D_{ma} = D_{ma}(φ,θ,0) are Wigner D functions. The ket |a m_a⟩ is a zero-field basis state |f m⟩ of (3.25) with f = a = I + 1/2. Sublevel |z⟩ = |a a⟩ has the maximum possible azimuthal quantum number m = a and the maximum possible total spin quantum number f = a, that is, |z⟩ = |a a⟩, where F·F|a a⟩ = a(a+1)|a a⟩ and F_z|a a⟩ = a|a a⟩. (9.42)

The ket |n⟩ represents an atom with maximum possible spin along the unit vector n, and one can readily show that F·F|n⟩ = a(a+1)|n⟩ and n·F|n⟩ = a|n⟩. (9.43)

The probability that atoms described by the density matrix ρ will be in the hyperfine multiplet a and will have their spins pointing into a small solid-angle increment dΩ = sinθ dθ dφ is ⟨W⟩ dΩ. One can use the orthogonality properties of the Wigner D functions to show that

∫ ∫ W dΩ = 1_fag = |a m⟩⟨a m|, or ⟨W⟩ dΩ = Tr[1_fag ρ]. (9.44)

Representative pointing probabilities for Figure 9.4 are shown in Figure 9.6. At zero velocity, the atoms are polarized along the electric field vector of the light, E = E x̂. For a velocity outside the capture range, v = 60 cm s⁻¹, the density matrix has little transverse coherence. The largest fraction (58%) of the atoms is in the end state |a, -a⟩ = |2, -2⟩, and these atoms make the strong, downward lobe of the pointing probability. But the next largest fraction (18%) of atoms is in the other end state, |a a⟩ = |2 2⟩, and this produces the smaller, upward lobe. The large damping coefficient, -∂F/∂z, at small velocity is associated with the transformation of the polarization from transverse to longitudinal.

## 9.5 Momentum Space

We turn now to the combined effects of the mean and fluctuation forces on the atom. The random nature of the absorbed and emitted photons leads to a momentum diffusion.

sorption and emission of photons, and the motion of the atom through gradients in the light shift lead to fluctuating forces that change the momentum distribution and heat or cool the atoms. To analyze the effects of these fluctuating forces, we include the atomic momentum as a quantum number and we introduce the spin-momentum basis states

In summary, for spin-momentum The text discusses the evolution of atomic states in spin-momentum space under optical pumping, leading to equations for density matrix evolution and steady-state solutions. It introduces the mean pumping rate, absorption cross-section, and Liouville-space formalism, with compactification methods for handling momentum-dependent matrix elements.

Key points: 1. Optical pumping adds radiative decay terms to the evolution equation, yielding steady-state density matrices.

2. The mean pumping rate Γ_fgg relates to photon absorption by unpolarized atoms, with cross-section formulas.

3. The Liouville-space representation simplifies the dynamics via superoperator formalism.

4. Compactification reduces the complexity of momentum-dependent optical pumping matrices.

The content is technical, focusing on quantum optical methods for manipulating atomic states, likely in the context of laser cooling or atomic physics.

situations of experimental interest, the density matrix is nonzero in only a finite number of tiles that are relatively close to the origin of pq space. For counterpropagating, circularly polarized beams of the same helicity, absorbing a photon increases the azimuthal spin quantum number m of a ket |f m⟩ by the helicity, η = +1. If the atoms start in noncoherent momentum states with p = q before they are exposed to the pumping light, the maximum momentum difference that can be generated is bounded by |p - q| ≤ 2f for integer quantum numbers f = 0,1,2,..., and by |p - q| ≤ 2f - 1 for half-integer quantum numbers with f = 1/2,3/2,5/2,.... Larger values of |p - q| would correspond to giving the atom an azimuthal spin quantum number m > f. Since the photons are absorbed or emitted in pairs, the differences in the momentum quantum numbers must be even, that is, p - q = 0, ±2, ±4,.... For the “lin-perp-lin” polarizations of the counterpropagating beams used in Sisyphus cooling, p - q must still be an even integer, but there is no restriction on the maximum value of |p - q|, and the density matrix extends into a larger region of pq space than for one-dimensional MOTs.

Cooling experiments are designed to limit the values of |p| and |q|. It is therefore possible to compactify pq space sufficiently to permit relatively speedy computations in the compactified space while obtaining answers that hardly differ from those one would obtain with the full, infinite-dimensional Liouville space. An example of a compactified pq space for MOT beams is shown in Figure 9.8. The diagonal boundaries where |p - q| = 2f = 4 are well defined and set by conservation laws of linear and angular momentum. The tile labeled 10 on the top skew boundary couples directly to the tiles with the labels 1, 2, 10, 11, 19, and 20. It has folded couplings to the tiles labeled 9, 18, and 27 at the opposite skew boundary, as though there were a “virtual” tile labeled 10 just outside the lower boundary. This “folding” of the couplings at the skew-diagonal boundaries ensures that there will be periodic boundary conditions in momentum space, and that atoms will be conserved in the compactified space. The two skew-diagonal boundaries can be chosen by trial and error so that the value of the density matrix in tiles along the skew boundaries is negligibly small. If doubling the size of the compactified space makes a negligible difference to the answer, the original compactified space was large enough.

9.8.2 Compactification within a Tile

Absorption of a photon with direction quantum number r changes the momentum quantum number p to p + r and it changes the spin quantum number m to m + rη. Because of this correlated change of momentum and spin indices for MOT pumping beams, if the atom has neither spin nor momentum coherence when the pumping light is first turned on, absorption and emission of pairs of photons will create correlated spin and momentum coherences. A tile on the main diagonal of pq space for which only the populations are nonzero is coupled to spin coherences of a nearest-neighbor northeast tile, such that the coupled spin coherences are displaced by η units from the main diagonal within the tile. Other couplings have similar selection rules. Thus, most of the density-matrix elements within any tile will be zero, and the density matrix will have the form ρ(p μ, q ν) = δ_{p-q, η(μ-ν)} ρ̃(p μ, q ν). (9.74)

In Figure 9.9 we illustrate some of the logical variables, δ_{p-q, η(μ-ν)}, for potentially nonzero elements of the density matrix in the case of an atom with ground-state spin quantum number f = 2. When appended to the program ending with Code 9.4 on pages 136 and 137, the following MATLAB statements will generate momentum labels pq of the tiles in the compactified space, and also the azimuthal quantum numbers mμ and mν of the spin states within each tile.

Code 9.5 mug=kron(ones(gg,1),(fg:-1:-fg)');%left spin indices of ground-state tile nug=kron((fg:-1:-fg)',ones(gg,1));%right spin indices of ground-state tile lpg=mug==nug;%logical variable for ground-state populations mue=kron(ones(ge,1),(fe:-1:-fe)');%left spin indices of excited-state tile nue=kron((fe:-1:-fe)',ones(ge,1));%right spin indices of excited-state tile np=2*ns+1;%number of tiles along diagonal of pq space p=[]; q=[];%initialize tile indices maxpmq=2*floor(fg);%maximum |p-q| for MOT for km=1:maxpmq+1 lpmq(:,km)=mug-nug==maxpmq+2-2*km;%log. var. of allowed coherences for kp=1:np%generate list of momentum indices p=[p;ns+maxpmq/2+2-km-kp];%left momentum index q=[q ;ns-maxpmq/2+km-kp];%right momentum index end end gpq=length(p);%number of pq tiles When appended to program ending with Code 9.5, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the left and right momentum quantum numbers p and q and the left and right spin azimuthal quantum numbers mμ and mν, denoted by pp, qq, mmu, and nmu.

Code 9.6 pmq=eta*(p-q);%azimuthal qn of spin coherences gmunu=2*fg-abs(pmq)+1;%number of spin coherences in pq tile km=maxpmq/2+1-pmq/2; jf=0;%azimuth index, initial spin index for k=1:gpq ji=jf+1;%initial coherence index of tile k jf=jf+gmunu(k);%final coherence index of tile k pp(ji:jf)=p(k);%replicate p for spin states qq(ji:jf)=q(k);%replicate q for spin states l=lpmq(:,km(k));%l.v. for spin states in tile k mmu(ji:jf)=mug(l);%mu for coherence in tile k nnu(ji:jf)=nug(l);%nu for coherence in tile k lmunu(:,k)=l;%save l.v. for coherence location in tile k infkg=(ji:jf)’;%save coherence indices for each tile k end pp=pp’; qq=qq’; mmu=mmu’; nnu=nnu’; in=in’;%rows to columns lp=pp==qq & mmu==nnu;%logical variable of populations gsm=length(pp);%number of spin-momentum basis states for km=1:maxpmq+1 lm(:,km)=pp==qq+maxpmq+2-2*km;%logical variables for coherences end When appended to the program ending with Code 9.6, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the numerators of the coupling coefficients of (9.72) that are illustrated in Figure 9.7.

Code 9.7 Nuu=flat(tV(:,:,1)’*tV(:,:,1))/(i*Gpg*hbar);%self coupling up Ndd=flat(tV(:,:,2)’*tV(:,:,2))/(i*Gpg*hbar);%self coupling down Nnw=-Asge*kron(conj(tV(:,:,1)),tV(:,:,1))/(i*hbar*Gpg);%nw Nse=-Asge*kron(conj(tV(:,:,2)),tV(:,:,2))/(i*hbar*Gpg);%se Nne=-Asge*kron(conj(tV(:,:,2)),tV(:,:,1))/(i*hbar*Gpg);%ne Nsw=-Asge*kron(conj(tV(:,:,1)),tV(:,:,2))/(i*hbar*Gpg);%se Nnn=flat(tV(:,:,2)’*tV(:,:,1))/(i*Gpg*hbar);%nn Nss=flat(tV(:,:,1)’*tV(:,:,2))/(i*Gpg*hbar);%ss When appended to the program ending with Code 9.7, the following MATLAB statements will evaluate the elements of the optical pumping matrix Aop (9.72).

Code 9.9 G=Aop+i*diag((pp.^2-qq.^2)/(2*rpg));%evolution operator rhoin=nullfast(G);%find steady state rhoin=rhoin/(lp’*rhoin);%normalize crhoin=zeros(gg^2,2*ns+1);%initialize rho versus momentum for k=1:maxpmq+1 x=rhoin(pp-qq==maxpmq+2-2*k); nx=length(x)/(2*ns+1); y=reshape(x,nx,2*ns+1); l=logical(lpmq(:,k)*ones(1,2*ns+1)); crhoin(l)=y; end Here we briefly discuss ways to display the physics of the very large spin-momentum density matrix j(cid:2) 1) that is the solution of (9.73).

## 9.10 Momentum Diffusion

We now discuss "momentum diffusion", the growth in the width of the momentum distribution due to the fluctuating parts of the forces from optical pumping and spontaneous emission of light. From considerations of the interplay between the mean force (9.7) and momentum diffusion, which we discuss in the next few sections, one can obtain much insight into the equilibrium momentum distributions of cooled atoms, though not with the quantitative detail that is provided by the use of spin-momentum space, which we have just discussed.

Let m = ħp be the longitudinal momentum of the atom and let Ndm be the probability of finding the atom with a momentum between m and m + dm at time t. We first assume that a mean force F and the fluctuating recoils of the atoms cause the probability density to satisfy the drift-diffusion equation ∂N/∂t = -∂(FN)/∂m + ∂²(DN)/∂m². (9.80)

If the force F is independent of velocity (i.e., independent of m), the solution of (9.80) that evolves from the distribution N = δ(m) at time t = 0 is N = (1/√(4πDt)) exp(-(m - Ft)²/(4Dt)). (9.81)

For times t > 0, the momentum has the nor mal distribution with the variance σ = 2Dt. Figure 9.4 showed that for MOT beams the force F is not constant but depends on velocity in a complicated way. For sufficiently small velocities v (or sufficiently small momenta) we can write the force as F = -γm = -Mγv, (9.82)

where γ is the low-velocity viscous damping rate of the atom, which we take to be independent of m, and M is the atomic mass. Then in the steady state, (9.80) becomes d/dm (γmN) + c = 0, (9.83)

where the constant c must be zero to ensure that both the density N and the diffusion current -D dN/dm approach zero as |m| → ∞. The normalized solution to (9.83) is N ∝ exp(-γm^2 / 2D) = exp(-m^2 / 2MkT). (9.84)

The effective temperature of the optically cooled atoms is T = D / kMγ. (9.85)

To get the coldest possible atoms, we want a large viscous damping rate γ, that is, a large value of -dF/dv at v = 0 in Figure 9.4, and we want the smallest possible momentum diffusion coefficient D. In the next two sections, we will show how to estimate values of D. It is customary to think of the momentum diffusion as the sum of diffusion due to spontaneous emission of light and the diffusion due to the absorption of photons going in various directions during optical pumping. As we will soon show, the diffusion due to spontaneous emission is very small compared with the diffusion due to optical pumping.

Problems We conclude this section by summarizing a few problems with the force–diffusion methods outlined above: * The detailed spin-momentum distributions, like those in Figure 9.10, are not Gaussian, as implied by (9.84).

* As shown in Figure 9.4, the damping rate γ = -dF/dm is not independent of m as assumed in the derivation of (9.84).

* Keeping track of the spin polarization of the atoms, and how that depends on momentum is a complicated task for the force–diffusion approach.

## 9.11 Momentum Diffusion Due to Spontaneous Emission

The momentum for an individual fluorescent photon propagating along the unit vector n is p = ℏkn. (9.86)

The recoil momentum of the atom is equal and opposite to the photon momentum (9.86). The rate of growth of the atom’s mean momentum due to spontaneous emission of photons is therefore d<hp>/dt = ∫ -ℏkn dΦ/dΩ dΩ = - (ℏ^2 k^2 Γ s f g e g / 8π) ∫ n n W(1 - n n) dΩ = 0. (9.87)

The spontaneous emission rate, dΦ/dΩ, of photons per solid angle was given by (8.61). Since the integral over all solid angles of any odd number of factors n must be zero, the fluorescent photons transfer no average momentum to the atoms. The mean force comes entirely from absorption of light.

Let N d^3p be the probability of finding the atomic momentum in a volume element d^3p of momentum space, centered on the momentum p at time t. We assume that the fluctuating recoils of the atoms cause the probability density to satisfy the anisotropic diffusion equation ∂N/∂t = Σ ∂^2 N / ∂p_i ∂p_j. (9.88)

ij The diffusion coefficient (a dyadic) can be defined as <h D_i j> = <d p_i p_j / dt> = -f e g D = (1/2) <d p p / dt>. (9.89)

From (9.89) and (9.86) we see that the diffusion-coefficient operator is D = (ℏ^2 k^2 / 2) ∫ n n dΦ/dΩ dΩ = (ℏ^2 k^2 Γ s f g e g / 6) [1 + ((-1)^J C S {J, J, 2}) / 5 * T{JJ, 2} 11 f<e e O> (tensor) T{JJ}]. (9.90)

To evaluate the second line of (9.90) we used the multipole expansion (8.61) for dΦ/dΩ and the identity ∫ n n dΩ = (8π / 3) Y_0^{(2)}(n) (traceless symmetric tensor part). (9.91)

The expectation value, <D>, of the diffusion coefficient is a real, symmetric tensor with positive eigenvalues, which are the principal diffusion coefficients. The eigenvectors are the principal directions of diffusion. The diffusion coefficient will be anisotropic if the excited atoms have non-zero components of the “alignment”, <T_{2m}>. This is possible if J > 1/2, but for J ≤ 1/2 the diffusion will always be isotropic.

Longitudinal Diffusion Coefficient For comparison with expressions in the literature, we will often be interested in the longitudinal diffusion coefficient due to spontaneous emission. From (9.90) and (8.64) we see that this is D_{se}^{zz} = <z D z> = (ℏ^2 k^2 Γ s f g e g / 6) [1 / f e g - ((-1)^J C S {J, J, 2}) / 20 * T{JJ, 2} f<e e O>]. (9.92)

## 9.12 Momentum Diffusion from Pumping

If spontaneous emission were the sole source of momentum diffusion, the large enhancement of the velocity-dependent force due to optical pumping would rapidly cool the atom to nearly the recoil energy ℏ^2 k^2 / 2M. However, optical pumping can greatly enhance the momentum diffusion, since the resulting spin polarization leads to large fluctuations of momentum transfer from the pumping light to the atoms. This enhanced momentum diffusion limits the cooling.

Here we discuss the simple but instructive model of Castin and Mølmer [56], who considered optical pumping of a cycling transition between an atom in a ground-state Zeeman multiplet with angular momentum quantum number f to an excited-state multiplet with quantum number f' = f, f ± 1. Counterpropagating, circularly polarized laser beams of the same helicity and intensity pump the atoms. The lasers are weak enough for us to ignore stimulated emission. The simplest Castin–Mølmer model assumes negligible coherence between either the spin or the momentum of the atomic ground state. We therefore We expect the Castin–Mølmer model to provide a good estimate of momentum diffusion for velocities outside the “capture range”, for example, for |v| > 10 cm s⁻¹ for the parameters shown in Figure 9.4. With this assumption we need only consider terms in (9.70) with p = q and with r = s. For simplicity we denote the diagonal matrix σ(pp) by σ(p), and the matrix elements ⟨p′p′|A_op|pp⟩ by A_{p′p}. Noting from (9.71) that H|pp⟩ = 0, we see that (9.70) becomes ∂σ(p)/∂t = Γ_fg { A_{p,p+1} [σ(p+1) − σ(p)] + A_{pp} σ(p) − A_{p,p−1} σ(p−1) }.

(9.93)

In the simple Castin–Mølmer model any dependence of the coupling matrices in (9.97) on the momentum index p is neglected. This would correspond to setting E(r) = E in (9.72). Then we can write A_{p,p+1} = A_{-1,0}, A_{pp} = A_{00}, and A_{p,p−1} = A_{1,0}.

(9.94)

The density matrix for the internal spin state of the atom, irrespective of the momentum, is |σ⟩ = ∑_p σ(p).

(9.95)

If we sum both sides of (9.97) over all p, we find ∂|σ⟩/∂t = Γ_fg A_op |σ⟩.

(9.96)

This is the same as (1.10) with the relaxation operator G = Γ_fg A_op, determined solely by optical pumping. There is no need to include the Hamiltonian in G since, H = 0 for populations. We also ignore collisional contributions to the damping operator. The total optical pumping operator is A_op = A_{-1,0} + A_{00} + A_{1,0}.

(9.97)

The right and left eigenvectors of A_op are defined by A_op |α_j⟩ = α_j |α_j⟩, ⟨⟨α_j| A_op = ⟨⟨α_j| α_j, and ⟨⟨α_j|α_k⟩ = δ_{jk}.

(9.98)

The eigenvalue index is j = 1, 2, …, g_fg, and we will assume that the eigenvalues α_j are ordered such that |α_j| ≥ |α_{j+1}|. For j = 1 we have |α_1⟩ = |σ_1⟩, ⟨⟨α_1| = (1|_{fg}, and α_1 = 0.

(9.99)

Here |σ_1⟩ is the steady-state density matrix for optical pumping alone, without momentum diffusion. Since we have compactified the Liouville space to the populations of the ground-state sublevels, (1|_{fg} is a 1 × g_fg row vector, all elements of which are 1.

Continuum Limit At sufficiently late times, we may assume that the momentum amplitudes (9.97) will vary slowly with the momentum index p. Then the integer variable p can be replaced by a continuous real variable. To order ∂²/∂p² we write the terms of (9.97) as σ(p±1) = σ(p) ± ∂σ(p)/∂p + (1/2) ∂²σ(p)/∂p² + ⋯ (9.100)

Substituting (9.100) into (9.97), we find ∂σ(p)/∂t = Γ_fg [ −A_op ∂/∂p + (1/2) A_op ∂²/∂p² ] σ(p).

(9.101)

Here we have introduced the repopulation operator A_rp = A_{-1,0} + A_{1,0} (9.102)

and the differential repopulation pumping operator δA_rp = A_{-1,0} − A_{1,0}.

(9.103)

Multiplying (9.101) on the left by ⟨⟨α_j|, we find ∂⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩/∂t = −Γ_fg α_j ⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩ + Γ_fg ⟨⟨α_j| ( −A_rp ∂/∂p + (1/2) A_rp ∂²/∂p² ) σ(p)⟩.

(9.104)

At late times the left side of (9.104) will get smaller and smaller as the distribution of atoms widens in momentum space at an ever slower rate. The only way (9.104) can remain valid for late times is for the terms on the right side to nearly cancel. Making the extreme approximation that the right side of (9.104) is zero, we find (for j ≠ 1 and α_j ≠ 0)

⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩ = −(1/α_j) ⟨⟨α_j| ( δA_rp ∂/∂p + (1/2) A_rp ∂²/∂p² ) σ(p)⟩.

(9.105)

From inspection of (9.104) we see that any spin-momentum amplitude ⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩ with j > 1 and α_j ≠ 0 will damp more rapidly than the amplitude ⟨⟨α_1|σ(p)⟩ with α_1 = 0. So at late times, we expect to have ⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩ ≪ ⟨⟨α_1|σ(p)⟩, for j > 1.

(9.106)

That is, we expect each momentum state, |σ(p)⟩, to have a spin distribution that differs only slightly from the steady-state distribution, |α_1⟩ = |σ_1⟩. As the momentum distribution gets smoother at late times, we expect to have ∂²⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩/∂p² ≪ ∂⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩/∂p.

(9.107)

In view of (9.105)–(9.107) we take the late-time approximation to (9.104) to be ⟨⟨α_j|σ(p)⟩ = −(1/α_j) (⟨⟨α_j|δA_rp|α_1⟩) ∂⟨⟨α_1|σ(p)⟩/∂p.

(9.108)

Substituting (9.108) into (9.104) and retaining only the largest terms on the right, we find the diffusion equation in momentum space, ∂⟨⟨α_1|σ(p)⟩/∂t = D_op ∂²⟨⟨α_1|σ(p)⟩/∂p², (9.109)

where the momentum diffusion coefficient due to optical pumping is D_op = ℏ²k² Γ_fg { ⟨⟨α_1|δA_rp|α_1⟩ + ∑_{j>1} (⟨⟨α_1|δA_rp|α_j⟩⟨⟨α_j|δA_rp|α_1⟩) / (2|α_j|) }.

(9.110)

The two terms in curly brackets are reminiscent of first-order and second-order corrections in perturbation theory.

Noting that the equilibrium density matrix of the excited state, summed over all momentum states, is |σ_fg⟩ = (Γ_fg / Γ_fge) A_op |α_1⟩, (9.111)

we can write the contribution (9.92) of spontaneous emission to the momentum diffusion coefficient as D_se = ℏ²k² Γ_fg (1/6) 1_{fg} Tr[ (1/2) {A_op, A_op^†} ] |α_1⟩.

(9.112)

The pumping-rate parameter Γ_s of Castin and Mølmer [56] is related to our mean pumping rate Γ_fg by Γ_fg = [2f_e + 1 / 3(2f + 1)] Γ_s.

(9.113)

Taking (9.113) into account, we find for f = 1 and f_e = 2, (D_op + D_se) = ...

5/9 D op se (225/88C23/220)，与Castin和Mølmer[56]的(2.7)式相同，对于f D0和f N D 1，(D CD ) D (1/2C1/5)，与Castin和Mølmer[56]的(2.8)式相同。

op se

光学泵浦对动量扩散的贡献(9.110)大于自发辐射的贡献(9.92)。对于更大的f值，光学泵浦的贡献完全主导了自发辐射的贡献。对于87Rb中从f D2到fND3的循环跃迁，相对值为D /D D 100.4。对于133Cs中从f D 4到f N D 5的循环跃迁，相对值为D /D D 865.9。正如Castin和Mølmer[56]指出的，在循环跃迁上，光学泵浦扩散系数的极大增强源于原子倾向于被泵浦到m D f或m D -f的子能级，类似于图9.1b中的情况。对于这些高|m|态，原子会连续吸收许多沿同一方向传播的光子，这些光子具有能被大|m|态原子强烈吸收的偏振。在动量空间中存在相对较长的定向行走序列，而非随机行走，这导致了大的扩散系数。这就是为什么我们在(9.65)式的最后一行忽略了自发发射光子随机方向带来的额外动量方差。

10 极化原子的弛豫光学泵浦的碱金属原子可能因其极化被各种弛豫机制破坏或改变。特别重要的包括与缓冲气体原子的碰撞、与其他碱金属原子的自旋交换碰撞、不同自旋极化区域之间的空间扩散，以及吸附在器壁上的自旋弛豫。我们将重点讨论基态原子的弛豫。

碱金属原子基态的退极化速率非常慢，以至于相对弱的碰撞相互作用就能对弛豫产生显著影响。相比之下，激发态原子的弛豫速率总是至少与自发辐射衰减率一样快，Γfgeg，因此只有强的碰撞相互作用才能对物理产生明显影响。

我们假设原子在公共基态哈密顿量Hfgg D H (2.10)的影响下演化。磁场可能是空间非均匀的，并且可能具有振荡分量，旨在诱导磁共振。公共哈密顿量H D H(r,t)因此可能依赖于原子的空间位置r和时间t。原子在公共哈密顿量H和碰撞自旋相互作用V的共同影响下演化，对于系综中的每个原子，V将具有不同的时间历程。如果V代表一个极化原子与扰动原子或分子之间的相互作用，它至少会依赖于核间距，也可能依赖于碰撞速度以及扰动原子或分子的内态。对于单次碰撞，V的效应可以用分波散射理论计算，或者假设原子及其碰撞伙伴的平动运动遵循明确的经典路径来计算。计算碰撞效应的这两种替代方法如图10.1所示。

经典路径近似对于光学泵浦中大多数重要的碰撞过程都适用，除了碱金属原子对之间的自旋交换碰撞外，它们最好用分波散射理论处理。

《Optically Pumped Atoms》. William Happer, Yuan-Yu Jau, and Thad Walker Copyright © 2010 WILEY-VCH Verlag GmbH & Co. KGaA, Weinheim ISBN: 978-3-527-40707-1 al-two methods give results that are within a few percent of each other. Classical-path calculations are less time-consuming.

## 10.1 S-Matrix

We describe the spin state of an individual alkali-metal atom with a wave function |ψ⟩. For our introductory discussion we assume that the partner’s internal state plays no role in the collision, so we ignore the wave function of the collision partner. In later sections on spin exchange, it will be necessary to include the wave function of the collision partner. We will also assume that the translational energies of the colliding partners are so large that we can ignore the changes in translational energies that are required to compensate for changes in the internal states of the colliding atoms during an inelastic collision. It will be convenient to call this the "infinite-temperature" approximation. For this situation the evolution of |ψ⟩ is defined by the Schrödinger equation

iℏ ∂/∂t |ψ⟩ = H_N |ψ⟩. (10.1)

The total Hamiltonian H_N acting during a collision is

H_N = H + V, (10.2)

where H is the Hamiltonian of the free atom and V is the collisional interaction.

If the buffer-gas pressure does not exceed a few atmospheres, we expect to find that V = 0 for relatively long periods of free flight between collisions. Binary collisions have a duration τ of a few picoseconds. Three-body collisions can lead to the formation of van der Waals molecules, for which the collision duration τ can be tens of nanoseconds at buffer-gas pressures of 1 Torr. When an atom hits the wall, the collision duration τ may also be relatively long, since the atom may hop along the surface for many nanoseconds before it is finally able to evaporate into the gas phase.

It is convenient to describe collisions in the gas with an S-matrix. Suppose that an atom has a collision just before time t_c. The collision duration is τ, in the sense that V(t) is negligibly small unless t_c - τ < t < t_c. At time t_c the atomic wave function has become |ψ_c⟩ as a result of the collision, whereas it would have been |ψ⟩ if there were no collision. The S-matrix is defined by

|ψ_c⟩ = S |ψ⟩. (10.3)

It is convenient to write the S-matrix as

S = U(τ) e^{i H τ / ℏ}. (10.4)

Note that e^{i H τ / ℏ} |ψ(t_c)⟩ = |ψ(t_c - τ)⟩ is the value of the wave function at the starting time of the collision. The evolution operator U = U(θ) is a function of the time, θ = t - (t_c - τ), after the start of the collision. From the Schrödinger equation we see that during the collision U is defined by

iℏ ∂U/∂θ = H_N U, with U(0) = 1. (10.5)

Here H_N = H + V(t_c - τ + θ). For a three-body collision, resulting in the formation of a van der Waals molecule in a particular vibration–rotation state, we can take V and H_N to be time-independent during the molecular lifetime, τ, and write the solution of (10.5), for 0 ≤ θ ≤ τ, as

U(θ) = e^{-i H_N θ / ℏ}. (10.6)

Then the S-matrix (10.4) for van der Waals molecules of lifetime τ is

S = e^{-i H_N τ / ℏ} e^{i H τ / ℏ}. (10.7)

For binary collisions, V will change gradually from negligibly small values at the beginning of the collision to large values near the time of closest approach to negligibly small values at the end of the collision. The integration of (10.5) to find U can be facilitated by writing

U(θ) = e^{-i H θ / ℏ} U_Q(θ). (10.8)

Substituting (10.8) into (10.5), we find that U_Q is the solution of

iℏ ∂U_Q/∂θ = V_Q U_Q, with U_Q(0) = 1. (10.9)

The interaction-picture potential, V_Q = V_Q(θ), is

V_Q = e^{i H θ / ℏ} V e^{-i H θ / ℏ}, with V = V(t_c - τ + θ). (10.10)

Using (10.8) in (10.3), we find

S = e^{-i H τ / ℏ} U_Q(τ) e^{i H τ / ℏ}. (10.11)

For binary collisions, one can ignore the factors e^{± i H τ / ℏ} in (10.11) and set

S = U_Q(τ). (10.12)

The omitted operators e^{± i H τ / ℏ} simply translate the wave function |ψ⟩ of a free atom along the time axis by ∓τ, so when we use (10.12) instead of (10.11), the collision appears to happen at the slightly earlier time, t_c - τ. The relaxation described by (10.12) is identical to that described by (10.11). For example, the probability |⟨ν|S|μ⟩|² for making a transition from the state |μ⟩ to the state |ν⟩ is the same for both expressions.

In conclusion, we recall that since the Hamiltonian evolution operators H and V are Hermitian, the S-matrix is unitary:

S† = S⁻¹. (10.13)

Liouville Space The Liouville-space version of (10.3) is

ρ_c = S ρ S†, or |ρ_c) = S_L |ρ). (10.14)

The Liouville-space S-matrix, S_L, is obtained from the Schrödinger-space S-matrix, S, by the transformation

S_L = S [ ⊗ S†] = S ⊗ S*. (10.15)

Equation 2.1.83 in [6], which corresponds to (10.15), has the order of S and S* reversed because the column vector |ρ) in [6] is assembled from the rows of the matrix ρ rather than from the columns, the convention we use in this book. One can use the identities (4.81) with (10.13) to show S_L of (10.15) is unitary:

S_L† = S_L⁻¹. (10.16)

Two easily verified identities for S_L are

⟨I| S_L = ⟨I| and S_L |I) = |I). (10.17)

The unit matrix |I) for the Schrödinger ground state was given by (2.17). The elements of the row vector ⟨I|, are 1 for populations and 0 for coherences. The left identity of (10.17) expresses the fact that the collision does not change the total population of the atom. The right identity expresses the fact that collisions with unpolarized atoms or molecules will leave an initially unpolarized atom unpolarized.

## 10.2 Collisions in the G

## 10.2 Collisions in the Gas

We introduce a nominal collision rate D N v σ, (10.18)

between an alkali-metal atom and buffer-gas atoms or molecules of number density N. The mean relative velocity between an alkali-metal atom and a buffer-gas atom or molecule is v, and σ is a cross section. For binary collisions we can set σ = π r², where r is an impact parameter that is large enough that the influence of V will be negligible during a collision. The choice of r is not critical, provided that it is large enough. For three-body collisions, σ will be proportional to N.

It is clear from the discussion of the previous section that the change, Δρ, in the density matrix, ρ = |ψ⟩⟨ψ|, in the small time interval Δt due to collisions is Δρ = ⟨ S ρ S† − ρ ⟩. (10.19)

Here the angle brackets ⟨...⟩ represent an ensemble average of collisions with all possible impact parameters and orbital planes – or if van der Waals molecules are formed, over all vibration–rotation states. The overall evolution of the density matrix due to collisions and due to the influence of the free-atom Hamiltonian H is ∂ρ/∂t = −(i/ħ) [H, ρ] + ⟨ S ρ S† − ρ ⟩. (10.20)

In Liouville space (10.20) becomes ∂|ρ⟩⟩/∂t = −(i/ħ) H© |ρ⟩⟩ + A |ρ⟩⟩, (10.21)

where the operator A is A = 1⟨⟨S£i. (10.22)

If we let S£ denote the S-matrix that describes a collision with a particular impact parameter and orbital plane, indicated by the subscript c, the average value of the S-matrix for an ensemble of collisions is ⟨S£⟩ = (1/g) ∑_c S£_c, (10.23)

where c labels a particular collision of the ensemble and g is the total number of sample collisions.

We will often call the operator A of (10.22) the accommodation operator because it (or more precisely each of its eigenvalues, α) is analogous to the thermal accommodation coefficient [57], α = (T_i − T_f) / (T_i − T_w), of gas molecules of temperature T_i impinging on a wall of temperature T_w and being desorbed from the wall at some final temperature T_f. If the final temperature is the same as the wall temperature, the accommodation coefficient is α = 1, and the wall is maximally efficient in destroying the temperature difference, T_i − T_w. If the final temperature of the molecules is the same as the initial temperature, the accommodation coefficient is α = 0, and the wall is completely unable to destroy the temperature difference.

In view of (10.17), the matrix A satisfies the identities ⟨1|f g| A = 0 and A |1|f g⟩ = 0. (10.24)

From inspection of (10.21), we see that the equation on the left of (10.24), ⟨1|f g| A = 0, ensures that ∂⟨1|f g|ρ⟩/∂t = ∂Tr[ρ]/∂t = 0. This is a completely general property of accommodation operators describing processes that simply redistribute atoms between the energy sublevels. The equation on the right of (10.24), A |1|f g⟩ = 0, ensures that if the atoms are unpolarized at time t = 0, the collision process described by A will leave the atoms unpolarized for all later times. This is not a general property of accommodation operators, and it comes from ignoring the internal states of the collision partner and from assuming the same collision rate 1/T for both endothermic and exothermic inelastic collisions, an approximation that is not valid if the energy intervals between spin sublevels become comparable to kT.

## 10.3 Weak Collisions

Weak collisions are those for which the collision time τ is short enough and the interaction V is small enough that for any pair of sublevels, |μ⟩ and |ν⟩, ∫_{t_c}^{t_c+τ} |⟨μ|V|ν⟩| dt ≪ ħ. (10.25)

Strong collisions are those for which the condition (10.25) is violated. In weak collisions, it is most likely that a collision will cause no change in the internal quantum numbers of the atom. With the notable exception of spin-exchange collisions between pairs of alkali-metal atoms, the most important interactions for binary collisions satisfy (10.25).

It will be convenient to work directly in Liouville space, where the analog of (10.9) is ∂U£/∂θ = (1/iħ) V© U£, with U£(0) = 1⟨⟨f g⟩⟩. (10.26)

The unit operator for Liouville space, 1⟨⟨f g⟩⟩, was given by (5.112). The Liouville-space time-evolution operator U£ and interaction V© are related to their Schrödinger-space equivalents by U£ = U[ ρ U†] = U ⊗ U* and V© = V[ ρ ⊗ V] . (10.27)

For weak collisions, we can use the familiar perturbation series to solve (10.26), S£ = U£(1) = 1 + (1/iħ) ∫ V©₀ dt₀ + (1/(iħ)²) ∫∫ V©₀ dt₀ V©₀₀ dt₀₀ + ... (10.28)

To simplify the form of the subsequent equations we have set V©₀ = V©(t₀) and V©₀₀ = V©(t₀₀), with V©(t) given in analogy to (10.10) by V© = e^{i H© θ/ħ} V© e^{-i H© θ/ħ}, with V© = V©(t − τ + θ). (10.29)

Since V© = 0 for times outside the collision interval, the time integrals can extend from −∞ to ∞. We write the interaction V© = V©(t) in terms of its Fourier transform Ṽ© = Ṽ©(ω), V©(t) = (1/2π) ∫ Ṽ©(ω) e^{-iωt} dω and Ṽ©(ω) = ∫ V©(t) e^{iωt} dt . (10.30)

Since V©(t) = V©†(t), we must have Ṽ©†(ω) = Ṽ©(−ω). (10.31)

Taking matrix elements of (10.28) between Liouville basis states |j⟩⟩ and |l⟩⟩, we find S£_{jl} = δ_{jl} + (1/iħ) Ṽ©_{jl}(ω_{jl})

+ (1/(2π iħ)²) ∫∫ dt₀ dω₀ Ṽ©_{jk}(ω₀) e^{-i(ω₀ − ω_{jk})t₀} k ∫∫ × (−) dτ dω₀₀ Ṽ©_{kl}(ω₀₀) e^{-i(ω₀₀ − ω_{kl})(t₀−τ)} + ... (10.32)

Changing the order of integrals...

ation, and carrying out the integrations over dt0 and dω0, we find

S_jl = δ_jl + i⟨VM©(ω_jl)⟩

Z∞ Z∞ (1/2π²) ∫ dω00 ⟨VM©(ω - ω00) VM©(ω00)⟩ dτ e^{i(ω00 - ω_kl)τ}

(10.33)

According to the secular approximation, which we discussed in Section 7.3, the basis states |j⟩ and |l⟩ will be coupled only if their Bohr frequencies are almost the same, ω_j ≈ ω_l. Any coupling terms that do not satisfy this criterion will have negligible influence on the evolution of the atoms. Therefore, we will set ω_j = ω_l in (10.33), and we will take its ensemble average to find

⟨S_jl⟩ = δ_jl + i⟨VM©(0)⟩_jl

Z∞ Z∞ (1/2π²) ∫ dω0 ⟨VM©(-ω00) VM©(ω00)⟩ dτ e^{i(ω00 - ω_kl)τ}

(10.34)

The last integral over dτ can be evaluated with the identity

Z∞ ∫ dt e^{iωt} = πδ(ω) + iP(1/ω)

(10.35)

Here δ(ω) is the Dirac delta function and P denotes the principal part. One can prove (10.35) by inserting a convergence factor e^{-ε|t|} in the integrand, and taking the limit of the result when the small positive parameter ε goes to zero. Using (10.35) with (10.34) and (10.31), we find to order V²,

⟨S_jl⟩ = δ_jl + i⟨VM©(0)⟩_jl - (1/2ℏ²) Σ_k ⟨VM©†(ω_jk) VM©(ω_kl)⟩

+ (i/π) ∫ dω00 ⟨VM©†(ω00) VM©(ω00)⟩ / (ω00 - ω_kl)

(10.36)

The part of (10.36) that is first order in V© represents shifts in the Bohr frequencies of the atom due to the time-averaged value of V©, which acts like a small, time-independent perturbation. The part of (10.36) that is second order in V© represents transfer of populations or coherences between the Liouville basis states |l⟩ and |j⟩ as well as a second-order frequency shift. The second-order shifts are dominated by the absorption and reemission of collisional "phonons" of frequency ω00 close to the frequency ω_kl of the transition from |l⟩ to the intermediate state |k⟩.

## 10.7 Hyperfine-Shift Interaction

We recall that the coefficient Afgg for the magnetic dipole interaction AfggI (cid:7)S in the 2S ground state of an alkali-metal atom is given by the Fermi–Segré formula 1/2 8πg μ μ AfggD S B Ijψfgg(0)j2 , (10.66)

3I where jψfgg(0)j2 is the probability density for the valence electron to be at the site of the nucleus. When a buffer-gas atom or molecule is a small distance R from an alkali-metal atom, it perturbs the value of jψfgg(0)j2, and makes it slightly larger or smaller than that for a free atom. From (10.66) we see that this will produce a corresponding perturbation, δAfgg D δAfgg(R), of the coupling coefficient Afgg.

This leads to the hyperfine-shift interaction: V DδAfggI (cid:7)S . (10.67)

Notable Aspects of the Hyperfine-Shift Interaction: 1. The interaction (10.67) conserves the total spin angular momentum, F D ICS, of the atoms, but it does not conserve S or I separately.

2. At small magnetic fields, the interaction (10.67) causes negligible relaxation of population differences.

3. At large magnetic fields, the interaction (10.67) tends to equalize the populations of sublevels jμi and jνi with the same azimuthal quantum numbers, mμ D mν. The overall effect is similar to that of spin exchange between pairs of alkali-metal atoms.

4. The interaction (10.67) is responsible for almost all of the pressure shift of the microwave resonance frequencies.

5. The frequency shift from the interaction (10.67) is so large that atoms in parts of the cell with slightly different temperatures can have substantially different microwave resonance frequencies. The resulting line broadening from temperature inhomogeneities can be much larger than the homogeneous collisional broadening from the same interaction.

6. The interaction (10.67) causes negligible homogeneous or inhomogeneous line broadening for low-field Zeeman resonances.

Some of the key features of the hyperfine shift interaction are shown in Figure 10.3.

Binary Collisions In practice, the shift coefficients δAfgg of (10.67) are small enough that for binary collis ions the criterion (10.25) for weak collisions is very well satisfied. For magnetic fields >1 T, the criterion (10.43) for sudden collisions is also satisfied. We will consider sudden, weak, binary collisions in this section.

The zero-frequency Fourier amplitude (10.30) of (10.67) becomes VM(0) = D, φ(I - S), (10.68)

where the phase angle is φ = δA f_gg dt. (10.69)

Using (10.68) in (10.45), we find the accommodation operator A = I + φ(I - S) + φ^2/2 (I - S)^2. (10.70)

Low Fields For the limit of vanishing magnetic field, B → 0, the Liouville basis states |f m, f' m'⟩ are eigenstates of A, A |f m, f' m'⟩ = α_f m f' m' |f m, f' m'⟩. (10.71)

The eigenvalues are α_f m f' m' = i φ [I](f - f') - φ^2/2 [I]^2 (f - f')^2. (10.72)

One can verify (10.72) by noting that (I - S) |f m, f' m'⟩ = [I](f - f') |f m, f' m'⟩. (10.73)

From (10.71) and (10.72), we see that the hyperfine-shift interaction has no effect on populations |f m, f m⟩ or Zeeman coherences |f m, f m'⟩. The hyperfine-shift interaction shifts the resonance frequency of the hyperfine coherence |a m, b m'⟩ by δω_a m b m' = Im(α_a m b m') = φ [I]/2T_c. (10.74)

The contribution to the damping rate of the coherence is δγ_a m b m' = Re(α_a m b m') = φ^2 [I]^2 / 8T_c^2. (10.75)

High Fields At higher magnetic fields, where the magnetic interaction -μ_f gg • B of (2.10) becomes comparable to the hyperfine interaction A_f gg I • S, the hyperfine-shift interaction couples populations with the same azimuthal quantum number m, and it has some of the same qualitative effects on the observed spin relaxation as spin-exchange collisions between pairs of alkali-metal atoms. By measuring spin relaxation at high magnetic fields, Walter and Griffith [60] were able to make some of the first accurate measurements of Carver rates by observing the "decoupling" of the relaxation rates in large magnetic fields of several thousand gauss. As has been pointed out by Oreto et al. [21], it is difficult to measure Carver rates at low field, since the homogeneous relaxation rate of the microwave coherence predicted by (10.76) can easily be confused with inhomogeneous relaxation of the coherence due to the frequency shift (10.74) in cells with small temperature gradients.

Experimental Measurements From inspection of (10.66), we see that the phase angle φ of (10.69) should be proportional to μ/I for different isotopes of the same chemical element, for example 39K and 41K or 85Rb and 87Rb. It is useful to quote measured values of quantities involving the hyperfine-shift interactions with an isotope-independent parameter. For spin relaxation, a convenient, isotope-independent parameter is the Carver rate Γ_C, with the theoretical, isotope-independent value Γ_C = φ^2 / 2T_c, where η = I μ_N / (μ I_N). (10.76)

The efficiency factor, η, accounts for different isotopes of the same element. For a hypothetical isotope with nuclear spin I = 1/2 and with a nuclear magnetic moment μ = μ_N of one nuclear magneton, we would have η = 1. Physically, Γ_C is the rate at which the electron spin ⟨S_z⟩ and the nuclear spin ⟨I_z⟩ of the hypothetical atom would relax to a common value at magnetic fields large enough to decouple the hyperfine interaction. From inspection of (10.76), (10.70), and (10.21), we see that the spin relaxation rate of the density matrix due to the hyperfine-shift interaction is ∂ρ/∂t = -Γ_C η^2 I^2 (I - S)^2 ρ. (10.77)

A convenient parameter to characterize experimental measurements of relaxation due to the hyperfine-shift interaction is the coefficient γ_C = dΓ_C / dN. (10.78)

Here N is the number density, usually quoted in amagats, of the perturbing gas. From (10.74) we see that the ratio of the hyperfine shift δω to the unperturbed hyperfine frequency ω = A_f gg [I]/(2π) should be the isotope-independent fraction, δω / ω = π h φ i / (T_c A_f gg). (10.79)

A convenient parameter to characterize experimental measurements of the frequency shift due to the hyperfine-shift interaction is the coefficient λ_C = d(δω/ω) / dN. (10.80)
