## 10.8 Spin–Rotation Interaction

To the extent that contributions from van der Waals molecules can be neglected, both ζ and λ are independent of the gas pressure and have the same values for different isotopes of the same chemical element. However, λ and ζ depend on temperature. Our current knowledge of the values of these parameters is summarized in Tables 10.1 and 10.2.

As first pointed out by Bernheim [70], the spin–rotation interaction, V = γ N · S, (10.81)

couples the electron spin S to the relative angular momentum N of an alkali-metal atom and a colliding buffer-gas atom or molecule. For most experimental conditions in gas cells, representative values of N are very large compared with the values of S or I, typically N ≈ 100. Therefore, we can usually consider N to be a time-independent, classical vector, N = N n. (10.82)

We assume that the unit vector n is equally likely to point in any direction, so ⟨n⟩ = 0 and ⟨n_i n_j⟩ = δ_ij. (10.83)

Here n_i = n · x_i is the projection of n along the Cartesian basis vector x_i. The angle brackets denote an average over the directions of n.

Notable Aspects of the Spin–Rotation Interaction

1. The interaction (10.81) is responsible for most of the longitudinal spin relaxation induced by collisions with buffer-gas atoms or molecules in low magnetic fields where the hyperfine-shift interaction of (10.67) has little effect.

2. The interaction (10.81) leads to multiexponential relaxation transients. To make accurate measurements, one must distinguish the contributions of the different transients to the observed relaxation signal.

3. The interaction (10.81) has an especially large effect on longitudinal relaxation rates at low magnetic fields and at low pressures for buffer-gas atoms or molecules that can form van der Waals molecules.

4. The interaction (10.81) is responsible for most of the homogeneous broadening of Zeeman resonance lines at low magnetic fields. Together with the hyperfine-shift interaction (10.67), it also contributes to the homogeneous linewidths of microwave resonances.

5. In contrast to the hyperfine-shift interaction (10.67), where the longitudinal relaxation rates increase with increasing magnetic field, many of the longitudinal rates for the interaction (10.81) decrease with increasing magnetic field.

Some of the key features of the spin–rotation interaction are shown in Figure 10.4.

(a) (b)

Figure 10.4 The spin–rotation interaction during a binary collision (a) can flip the electron spin S with negligible effect on the nuclear spin I. Such “S-damping collisions” lead to the relative collisional transition rates between low-field energy sublevels shown in (b).

10.8.1 Binary Collisions

In practice, the coupling coefficients γ N of (10.81) are small enough that the criterion (10.25) for weak collisions is well satisfied. For magnetic fields > 1 T, the criterion (10.43) for sudden collisions is also satisfied. The zero-frequency Fourier amplitude (10.30) of (10.81) becomes V_M(0) = ⟨φ n · S⟩, (10.84)

where the electron spin operator in Liouville space is S = S[⊗ - S]. The phase angle, φ = ∫_{-1}^{1} γ N dt, (10.85)

depends on the classical trajectory. Using (10.84) and (10.83) in (10.45), we find A = ⟨S · S⟩/⟨φ²⟩ = ⟨φ²⟩/3 A_sd, (10.86)

where the S-damping operator, A_sd = S · S/2, was introduced in (6.88). Some useful identities involving A_sd are (S|A_sd|S) = 1, (I|A_sd|I) = 0 and (|fgg⟩|A_sd|fgg) = 0. (10.87)

The 1×[I]2[S]2 row vectors, (S|, (I|, and (|fgg|, are defined by (4.27) and (4.25), with |fgg⟩ given by (2.17).

Combining (10.86) with (10.21), we find that the relaxation due to the spin–rotation interaction is ∂/∂t (ρ) = (-iH/ħ + Γ_sd A_sd) ρ, (10.88)

where the S-damping rate from the spin–rotation interaction is Γ_sd = ⟨φ²⟩ / 3T. (10.89)

Populations Using (6.88) with (4.85), we see that the non-zero matrix elements of (6.88) between population basis states are (μμ|A_sd|μ'μ') = (μμ| (S_x, S_y, S_z) |μ'⟩⟨μ'|)

= ⟨μ| (S_x, S_y, S_z) |μ'⟩⟨μ'|μ⟩ = δ_{μμ'} - ⟨μ|S_j|μ'⟩⟨μ'|S_j|μ⟩. (10.90)

In (10.90) the repeated Cartesian indices j are summed over, but there is no sum over the repeated labels μ and μ'.

The relative transfer rates, (μμ|A_sd|μ'μ'), of populations between different low-field state population basis states |μ⟩ and |μ'⟩ are shown in Figure 10.4b. The projection of the accommodation operator in the population subspace can be expanded on the population eigenvectors |j⟩ of A_sd, A_sd^{NN} = ∑_j α_j |j⟩⟨j|. (10.91)

Since A_sd^{NN} = A_sd^{NN†}, we can take the eigenvectors |j⟩ of (10.91) to be orthonormal, with ⟨j|k⟩ = δ_jk.

In the limit that the externally applied magnetic field vanishes, the eigenvectors |j⟩ have a well-defined multipolarity L. The normalized, nonrelaxing mode with α = 0 is simply |j=0⟩ = |fgg⟩/√(g_fg), where the atom is equally likely to be in any of the g sublevels |μ⟩ of the ground state. Along with the pure, unpolarized eigenstate, with α = 0, Figure 10.5 shows the density matrices |1 fg⟩⟨fgg|/g_fg.

with maximum initial content of the relaxing mode \(|j\rangle\). For \( \alpha \neq 0 \), some of the amplitudes \(\langle\mu\mu|j\rangle\) must be negative, and one of the negative amplitudes, \(\langle\mu\mu|j\rangle \ll \langle\mu\mu|j\rangle\), will be most negative. The coefficient \[ c_j = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{\mu\mu} \langle\mu\mu|j\rangle}} \]

is chosen to make the minimum sublevel population of \(j, \alpha\) zero. A special case is the unpolarized, nonrelaxing mode with \( \alpha = 0 \), where we take \( c_j = 0 \) in (10.92).

The relaxing modes, \(|j\rangle\), for atoms with no nuclear polarization are completely destroyed in each S-damping collision and they therefore have eigenvalues (accommodation coefficients) \( \alpha = 1 \). These are the modes shown along the bottom of Figure 10.5. Modes with nuclear polarization can be written as \(\langle\mu\mu|j\rangle = k \langle \mu_j | T_{II} | \mu_j \rangle\), where \( k \) is a constant coefficient and \( T_{II} \) is a nuclear basis tensor, defined in analogy to (5.21). The nuclear polarization is conserved in an S-damping collision. It partially regenerates the polarization mode in the intervals between collisions, leading to accommodation coefficients \( \alpha < 1 \).

**Figure 10.5** The density matrices \(|j, \alpha\rangle\) fields for alkali-metal atoms with nuclear spin quantum number \( I = 3/2 \). There are two independent modes of different relaxation rates for the multipolarities \( L = 0, 1, 2 \) and one mode each for the multipolarities \( L = 3, 4 \). The density matrices of (10.92), with the maximum possible content of the modes \(|j\rangle\) of (10.91). These are the modes for S-damping caused by the spin–rotation interaction (10.81) at low magnetic fields.

**10.8.2 Experimental Measurements** By analyzing experimentally observed data on spin relaxation with models based on (10.88), one can deduce values for the characteristic rate \( \Gamma_{sr} \). It is convenient to summarize these measurements with a cross section \( \sigma_{sr} \), defined by \[ \Gamma_{sr} = N \sigma_{sr} v, \]

where \( N \) is the number density of the perturbing gas and \( v \) is the mean relative velocity of the colliding pairs. Experimental measurements and theoretical estimates of these parameters for various alkali-gas pairs are summarized in Table 10.3.

There are striking, order-of-magnitude variations in the sizes of the cross sections for different alkali–noble gas pairs. For the heavy noble gases, the cross sections vary dramatically from Ne to Xe but show a much smaller variation as the alkali-metal partner is changed. In these cases, the spin–rotation interaction comes primarily from spin–orbit coupling in the core of the noble gas atom [71]. In contrast, the alkali-metal–He relaxation rates correlate well with the spin–orbit interaction in the alkali-metal core [72].

**10.9 Spin Exchange between Alkali-Metal Atoms and Noble Gas Atoms** We turn now to spin-exchange processes that involve optically pumped alkali-metal atoms. Some of the most important exchange processes are sketched in Figure 10.6. The interaction primarily responsible for spin exchange between optically pumped alkali-metal atoms and noble gas nuclei is the isotropic, magnetic dipole hyperfine interaction, \[ V = \alpha \mathbf{S} \cdot \mathbf{K}, \]

between the electron spin \( \mathbf{S} \) of the alkali-metal atom and the nuclear spin \( \mathbf{K} \) of the noble gas atom. We will take (10.95) to be the interaction potential of (10.2). The coupling coefficient \( \alpha = \alpha(R) = A f\!\!/\!/g \) is given by the Fermi–Segré formula (10.66), with \( \psi\!\!/\!/g(0) \) being the amplitude of the valence electron wave function at the site of the noble gas nucleus. Consequently, \( \alpha \) is a rapidly decreasing function of the internuclear separation \( R \) of the colliding pair. Other interactions that couple the spins of the two atoms, for example, the anisotropic magnetic dipole interaction, are much smaller than (10.95) and can be ignored. The translational motion of the colliding pair is hardly affected by spin interactions, so we can use a semiclassical analysis, where the orbital motion of the atoms about each other during a collision is treated classically, and the spin evolution of the two atoms is treated quantum mechanically. Before the collision, let the spin wave function of the ground-state alkali-metal atom be \(|\psi_{f1}g\rangle = |\psi_{f}g\rangle\) and let the spin wave function of the noble gas atom be \(|\psi_{f2}g\rangle\). We will take the initial spin wave function of the colliding pair as the Kronecker product \[ |\psi_i\rangle = |\psi_{f2}g\rangle \otimes |\psi_{f1}g\rangle.

\]

It will be convenient to expand the wave function (10.96) on the basis states \[ |\mu_1 \mu_2\rangle = |\mu_1\rangle \otimes |\mu_2\rangle.

\]

The common Hamiltonian of (10.2) becomes \[ H = H_{f1}g + H_{f2}g.

\]

**Figure 10.6** Spin exchange: (a) the exchange of electron spin polarization in a binary collision of a pair of alkali-metal atoms; (b) the exchange polarization of a noble gas atom in a binary collision of an alkali-metal atom with the nuclear spin; (c) there is an especially high probability of spin exchange when a long-lived van der Waals molecule is formed in a three-body collision.

The Hamiltonian H^(1) of the free alkali-metal atom was given by (2.10). The Hamiltonian of the free noble gas atom, with nuclear spin quantum number K and nuclear magnetic moment μ, is H^(2) = μ^(2) · B, where μ^(2) = γK. (10.99)

As we discussed in Section 3.1, free-atom Hamiltonians of (10.98) should be thought of as the Kronecker products H^(1) → H^(1) ⊗ 1^(2) and H^(2) → 1^(1) ⊗ H^(2). (10.100) Here 1^(1) = 1^{1} and 1^(2) are the unit operators for the Schrödinger spaces of the alkali-metal atom and the noble gas atom. Similarly, the interaction (10.95) can be identified with the interaction V of (10.3), which we write as V = α Σ_j K_j ⊗ S_j, (10.101) where the Cartesian index j takes on the values x, y, and z. We will think of the coupling coefficient α = α(t) as that of a collision that occurs at time t = 0 so that α(t) is strongly peaked at t = 0 and approaches zero rapidly as t → ±∞.

In accordance with (10.20), the change in the density matrix caused by a single collision is Δρ = SρS† - ρ, (10.102) where S = S is the S-matrix, defined by (10.3) or (10.11). The initial density matrix of the colliding pair, before the collision, is ρ = |ψ⟩⟨ψ| = ρ^(2) ⊗ ρ^(1), (10.103) where ρ^(1) = |ψ^(1)⟩⟨ψ^(1)| and ρ^(2) = |ψ^(2)⟩⟨ψ^(2)|. (10.104)

The post-collision density matrix, SρS†, of (10.102) has correlations between the spin-observables of the alkali-metal atom and the noble gas atom. These correlations are normally lost when an ensemble average is taken over all types of collisions. We can therefore assume that the collisional contributions to the time evolution of the density matrices of the two species of atom are  dρ^(1)/dt = -Γ^(1) [ρ^(1), Tr_2[SρS†]]  and  dρ^(2)/dt = -Γ^(2) [ρ^(2), Tr_1[SρS†]] . (10.105) In (10.105), the angle brackets denote an average over an ensemble of collisions. The partial trace Tr_2[X] = Σ_{m_K} ⟨μ_m|X|μ_m⟩ converts an operator X of dimension [I][S][K] × [I][S][K] for the product spin space of the alkali-metal atom and the noble gas atom to a matrix of dimension [I][S] × [I][S] operating within the spin space of the alkali-metal atom. Similarly, Tr_1[X] = Σ_{m_I m_S} ⟨μ_{m_I m_S}|X|μ_{m_I m_S}⟩.

The collision rates for the alkali-metal atoms and noble gas atoms are Γ^(1) = N^(1)vσ and Γ^(2) = N^(2)vσ, (10.106) where N^(1) is the number density of alkali-metal atoms and N^(2) is the number density of noble gas atoms.

10.9.1 Binary Collisions

In practice, the coupling coefficients α of (10.95) are small enough that the criterion (10.25) for weak collisions is well satisfied. We will assume that the magnetic field is small enough, typically < 1 T, that the criterion (10.43) for sudden collisions is also satisfied. The zero-frequency Fourier amplitude (10.30) of (10.95) becomes V_M(0) = φ S · K. (10.107) The phase angle, φ = ∫_{-∞}^{∞} α dt , (10.108) depends on the impact parameter of the classical trajectory.

Substituting (10.107) into (10.46), and writing products of angular momentum operators as, K_i K_j = 1/2 {K_i, K_j} + 1/2 [K_i, K_j] = 1/2 {K_i, K_j} + i Σ_k ε_{ijk} K_k , (10.109) with an analogous expression for S_i S_j, we find that the rate of change of the density matrix ρ of alkali-metal atoms due to spin-exchange collisions with noble gas atoms is  dρ^(1)/dt = -i Γ^(1) ⟨φ⟩ [⟨K⟩ · S, ρ^(1)] + Γ^(1) ⟨φ²⟩/4 { {S·S, ρ^(1)} - 2 S·ρ^(1)S , S·⟨K⟩ } . (10.110) An analogous equation for ∂ρ^(2)/∂t can be obtained from (10.110) by making the substitutions S ↔ K and (1) ↔ (2). The symmetric, dyadic operators of (10.110) are constructed from the anticommutators of the angular momentum operators, K K = Σ_{i,j} x_i {K_i, K_j} x_j and S S = Σ_{i,j} x_i {S_i, S_j} x_j . (10.111) The double-dot product of two dyadics was defined by (8.59). The quantum-mechanical expectation values of (10.110) are ⟨K⟩ = Tr_2[K ρ^(2)] and ⟨K K⟩ = Tr_2[K K ρ^(2)] . (10.112)

Noble Gases with K = 1/2

The noble gases 3He and 129Xe are often polarized by spin exchange with optically pumped alkali-metal atoms, and both have nuclear spin quantum numbers K = 1/2. Of course, the electron spin of the alkali-metal atom is S = 1/2. Then the dyadics in (10.110) simplify to K K = S S = 1/2, where the unit dyadic is 1 = x x + y y + z z, and we find  dρ^(1)/dt = -i Γ^(1) ⟨φ⟩ [⟨K⟩ · S, ρ^(1)] + Γ^(1) ⟨φ²⟩/4 [ {S·S, ρ^(1)} - 2 S·ρ^(1)S ] + i (⟨φ⟩/⟨φ²⟩) [⟨K⟩ · S, ρ^(1)] . (10.113)

Multiplying both sides of (10.110) by S and taking the trace of the result, we find d⟨S⟩/dt = Tr_1[S dρ^(1)/dt] = - Γ^(1) ⟨φ⟩ ⟨K⟩ × ⟨S⟩ - Γ^(1) ⟨φ²⟩/2 {η_K · ⟨K⟩ - η_S · ⟨S⟩} . For K = 1/2 or S = 1/2, η_K = η_S = 1/2.

10.9.3 Experimental Measurements

Spin-exchange rate coefficients have been measured for a number of alkali metals and noble gases. We briefly review the most common methods of measurement here. It is convenient to define a spin-exchange rate coefficient:

k_{se} = \frac{\Gamma_{\{1\}} \langle \phi_2 \rangle}{4N\{2\}} = \frac{\Gamma_{\{2\}} \langle \phi_2 \rangle}{4N\{1\}} \ . \ (10.141)

Then, for axial symmetry (10.119) becomes

h\dot{K}_z i = N\{1\}k_{se} 2 \langle \eta \rangle h S_z i - N\{1\}k_{se} + \Gamma_w h K_z i. \ (10.142)

In writing (10.142), we noted that h\eta i = 1/2, and we added a relaxation rate \Gamma_w that is independent of the density N\{1\} of alkali-metal atoms and that is usually dominated by relaxation at the walls. For the steady state, (10.142) implies that

h K_z i = \frac{2 \langle \eta \rangle h S_z i k_{se}}{k_{se} N\{1\} + \Gamma_w} \ . \ (10.143)

If h\eta i h S_z i can be considered time-independent once pumping light has been turned on, we see from (10.142) that the nuclear spin, h K_z i, will build up to the equilibrium value (10.143) with an exponential rate:

\gamma = N\{1\} k_{se} + \Gamma_w \ . \ (10.144)

The rate coefficient is most often determined by measuring the rate \gamma for a series of increasing cell temperatures. The cells usually contain condensed alkali metal, liquid or solid, and N\{1\} will therefore increase with increasing temperature. The number density, N\{1\}, of alkali-metal atoms is often several tens of percents smaller than what one would infer from the saturated vapor pressure curves of the metals, so N\{1\} must be measured directly in some way and not inferred from the temperature. If both k_{se} and the wall relaxation rate \Gamma_w are known to have negligible dependences on temperature, the rate coefficient k_{se} is simply the slope of a plot of experimentally measured values of \gamma versus N\{1\}. This method does not work well for 3He because \Gamma_w is usually strongly temperature dependent for 3He cells.

One way to cope with temperature-dependent quantities is to use the “rate balance method” [86]. If the rate \gamma of (10.144) as well as the steady-state values of the spin polarizations h S_z i and h K_z i, and the number density, N\{1\}, are measured with sufficient accuracy at a fixed temperature, it follows from (10.143) that the rate coefficient at that temperature is given by

k_{se} = \frac{\gamma h K_z i}{2 h\eta i h S_z i N\{1\}} \ . \ (10.145)

For the spin-1/2 nuclei 3He and 129Xe we have 2 h\eta i = 1.

One can use the “repolarization method” [77] to measure k_{se}, where the steady-state spin polarization h S_z i of alkali metal is measured with very weak pumping light, so all the polarization is due to spin-exchange collisions with noble gas nuclei of spin polarization h K_z i. For axial symmetry (10.114) becomes

h\dot{S}_z i = N\{2\} k_{se} h K_z i - (2 \langle \eta \rangle k_{se} N\{2\} + \Gamma_s) h S_z i. \ (10.146)

In writing (10.144), we added a relaxation rate, \Gamma, that accounts for collisional spin relaxation mechanisms of h S_z i other than spin exchange, usually the collisional spin–rotation interaction. For the steady-state (10.144) implies that

h S_z i = \frac{k_{se} N\{2\} h K_z i}{k_{se} N\{2\} 2 \langle \eta \rangle + \Gamma_s} \ . \ (10.147)

In analogy to (10.143), the rate coefficient is given by

k_{se} = \frac{\gamma h S_z i}{N\{2\} h K_z i} \ . \ (10.148)

The exponential rate coefficient for the polarization h S_z i to build up owing to spin-exchange collisions with noble gas nuclei is the denominator of (10.147), or

\gamma = k_{se} N\{2\} 2 \langle \eta \rangle + \Gamma_s \ . \ (10.149)

The number density N\{2\} of the noble gas is normally known to high accuracy, but one must make accurate measurements of h S_z i, h K_z i, and \gamma to determine k_{se} with (10.148). A summary of measured spin-exchange rate coefficients is given in Table 10.4.

From (10.110) we see that the spin-exchange collisions cause the electron spin of the alkali-metal atom to rotate at the rate \delta \omega = \Gamma_{\{1\}} \langle \phi \rangle h K i, the same rotation rate that would be produced by a magnetic field shift \delta B = \hbar \delta \omega / (g_S \mu_B). The resulting shifts of the magnetic resonance frequencies of alkali-metal atoms can serve as a convenient and precise way to measure the spin polarization h K i of the noble gas nuclei. The frequency shift is conveniently parameterized by a dimensionless factor \chi = \delta B / \delta B_c that measures how much larger \delta B is than the classical field \delta B_c = 8 \pi \mu_K h K i / (3K) produced by a noble gas with a spin polarization h K i inside a spherical container. If \nu is the frequency of some field-dependent resonance

frequency of the alkali metal atom, then the polarized noble gas will shift that frequency by δν_j D j Δ K_z (10.150)

Here ∂ν_j/∂B is the rate of change of the magnetic resonance frequency of the transition j. For example, for low-field Zeeman transitions, ∂ν_j/∂B D g_μ B/(h[I]). If the coefficient Δ is known, one can determine the spin polarization of the noble gases very precisely by simply measuring the shift, δν_j, in the magnetic resonance frequency, ν_j, when ⟨K_z⟩ is driven to zero by a magnetic resonance field, or is reversed in sign by adiabatic fast passage. Romalis and Cates [88] measured Δ to high accuracy by studying the frequency shifts of polarized ³He gas in a cylindrical cell at different orientations. This allowed Δ for Rb–³He to be deduced to 1.5% accuracy without an accurate measurement of the ³He polarization. Using cells with atom mixtures, Stoner and Walsworth [89] and Babcock et al. [90] used ratios of frequency shifts to extend this result to [Rb–²¹Ne], [K–³He], and [Na–³He]. Table 10.5 summarizes experimental measurements of Δ.

## 10.10 Spatial Diffusion

The density matrix ρ(r) will normally depend on the location r within the cell. Spatial diffusion and bulk relaxation, represented by the evolution operator G, will cause the density matrix, ρ, to change at the rate ∂ρ/∂t D Dr²ρ – Gρ. (10.151)

Here D D λv (10.152)

is the classical diffusion coefficient. The mean free path of atoms in the gas is λ and mean velocity is v. A representative mean velocity for a heavy alkali metal atom is v ≈ 3 × 10⁴ cm s⁻¹. (10.153)

A representative value of the diffusion coefficient at a buffer-gas density of [B] D 1 amg is D ≈ 0.1 cm² s⁻¹, so a representative value of λ is λ ≈ 10⁻⁴ [B]⁻¹ cm amg. (10.154)

The ratio of the cell volume V to the surface area S is a characteristic length l, l D V/S. (10.155)

The diffusion equation (10.152) will be a good approximation to the spatial transport of atoms in a cell provided that λ ≪ l. (10.156)

The criterion (10.156) is usually satisfied if the buffer-gas pressure exceeds a few tens of millitorr.

Eigenmode Expansion The evolution operator G represents evolution due to the spin evolution, collisional relaxation, optical pumping, and any other bulk processes that would operate in the absence of spatial diffusion. We suppose that G is independent of position, and has eigenvectors |γ⟩ given by (4.49). Then we can substitute the expansion ρ D Σ_γ |γ⟩⟨γ|ρ (10.157)

into (10.151) to find the diffusion equation for the spatially dependent amplitude ⟨γ|ρ⟩, ∂⟨γ|ρ⟩/∂t D D∇² – ⟨γ|G|γ⟩⟨γ|ρ⟩. (10.158)

We assume we can find exponentially damping solutions, ⟨γ|ρ⟩ D ⟨γ|Γ⟩e⁻Γt. (10.159)

Substituting (10.159) into (10.158), we find the time-independent mode amplitude satisfies the Helmholtz equation (∇² + k²)⟨γ|Γ⟩ D 0, (10.160)

where the spatial frequency is k² D (Γ – γ)/D. (10.161)

Boundary Conditions To solve the Helmholtz equation (10.160), we need boundary conditions for the density matrix at the cell walls. On the scale of an atomic diameter, the surface at the walls may be very rough, but we will assume that there is a well-defined unit vector n, normal to the local “macroscopic” surface, and pointing into the material of the wall.

According to kinetic theory [96], the current density of polarized atoms hitting the wall at a point with surface normal n is JJC) D N (v/4) [v/6 – λ n·∇] ρ. (10.162)

Here N is the number density of atoms in the volume element of the cell adjacent to the surface. The density matrix of the diffusing atoms is ρ. As indicated in (10.162), it is convenient to discuss the physics in Liouville space. The current of atoms desorbed from the surface is JJJ)) D ⟨S⟩ JJC). (10.163)

Here ⟨S⟩ is the ensemble-averaged S-matrix, defined in analogy to (10.23) for collisions with the wall. An expression for ⟨S⟩ is given by (10.189) in Section 10.11.

The net current density into the wall is JJC) – JJJ)) D (1 – ⟨S⟩)JJC) D A JJC). (10.164)

The accommodation operator A was defined by (10.22). Equating the current density (10.164) to the classical diffusion-current density at the wall, JJC) D –N n·∇ρ, we find the boundary condition A ρ D –(2 – A) n·∇ρ / (2λ), (10.165)

or in terms of the normal gradient operator M, n·∇ρ D –M ρ. (10.166)

We can write the normal gradient operator as M D 3A / [2λ(2 – A)] D Σ_μ μ |μ⟩⟨μ|, with ⟨μ|μ'⟩ D δ_μμ'. (10.167)

The eigenvalues and eigenvectors of A and M are related by μ D 3α / [2λ(2 – α)], with |μ⟩ D |α⟩. (10.168)

We assume that the eigenvectors can be normalized in analogy to (4.52).

The boundary condition (10.166), often called a Robin boundary condition, was introduced by Masnou-Seeuws and Bouchiat [96] in connection with their studies of cells for which the walls caused little spin depolarization. To account for coherent interactions of spin-polarized atoms with the cell walls, Wu et al. [97] showed that it is necessary to think of M D M(r) as an operator in Liouville space that may be a function of position r along the surface.

Weakly Depolarizing Walls A weakly depolarizing wall has |α| ≪ 1 for all eigenvalues α. Then (10.167) becomes M ≈ ∑_α |α⟩⟨α| (10.169) / (4λ).

Using (10.169) and (10.152), we write the current density of polarized atoms at a weakly depolarizing wall as jJ⟩ = (ND/(2π)) n⋅∇r ρ = ∑_α α |α⟩⟨α|ρ⟩ (10.170).

From kinetic theory, the current of polarized atoms hitting the walls is N v ρ⟩/4.

According to (10.170), the current carried by the component |α⟩⟨α|ρ⟩ of the density matrix is suppressed by a factor α from the atomic current N v/4 that hits the wall. Physically, this occurs because a fraction, 1-α, of the atoms are desorbed in the same state |α⟩ in which they were adsorbed, so the current into the wall is nearly canceled by the current out of the wall.

Unconfined Polarization If the depolarization at the walls is so slow that the atoms can diffuse freely throughout the entire volume V of the cell before substantial relaxation occurs, the density matrix ρ⟩ will be nearly independent of the position in the cell and almost equal to the volume-averaged value ⟨ρ⟩_av = ∫ ρ⟩ dV / V. (10.171)

Masnou-Seeuws and Bouchiat [96] call this the limit of “unconfined polarization”. Using (10.151), (10.166), and (10.169) with the divergence theorem to evaluate the time rate of change of ⟨ρ⟩_av due to diffusion to the wall, we find d⟨ρ⟩_av / dt = -∫ M ρ⟩ dS / V = -∑_α ∫ α |α⟩⟨α|ρ⟩ dS / (4V). (10.172)

Here dS is an element of surface area along the wall. Multiplying both sides of (10.172) on the left by ⟨α|, we find that adsorption on the wall causes the elements ⟨α|⟨ρ⟩_av of the density matrix to relax at the rate d⟨α|⟨ρ⟩_av / dt = -γ_Nα ⟨α|⟨ρ⟩_av, (10.173)

where the damping coefficient is γ_Nα = v_α ∫ dS / (4V). (10.174)

For the condition of unconfined polarization to be valid, the diffusion rates in the gas, which are of order v λ / l², must be much faster than the wall-damping rates (10.174), which are of order |α| v / l. The criterion for unconfined polarization is therefore |α| ≪ λ / l. (10.175)

We must have λ / l ≪ 1 for the diffusion equation (10.151) to be valid, so the accommodation coefficients α must be extremely small for conditions of unconfined polarization. The bulk relaxation rates γ must also be exceptionally small. Such conditions can occur for alkali-metal atoms at low buffer-gas pressures with very carefully prepared wall coatings. Unconfined polarization is a much more common phenomenon for noble gases with spin-polarized nuclei, where all the relaxation processes are much slower than for alkali-metal atoms.

Neumann Boundary Conditions In the limit of walls that cause no depolarization at all, α → 0 for all |α⟩, so M → 0 and the Robin boundary condition (10.166) reduces to the Neumann boundary condition: n⋅∇r ρ⟩ = 0. (10.176)

For walls that cause finite but small depolarization, one can treat the normal gradient operator M as a perturbation [97].

Strongly Depolarizing Walls We can expand ρ⟩ on the eigenvectors of M to find ρ⟩ = ∑_μ |μ⟩⟨μ|ρ⟩. (10.177)

Substituting (10.177) into (10.166) and multiplying on the left by ⟨μ|, we find the amplitudes of ρ at the cell walls are given by ⟨μ|ρ⟩ = ⟨μ| n⋅∇r ρ⟩, for μ ≠ 0. (10.178)

Combining (10.178) with (10.177), we find that the density matrix at the wall is given by ρ⟩ = |0⟩⟨0|ρ⟩ + ∑_{μ≠0} |μ⟩⟨μ| n⋅∇r ρ⟩, where |0⟩ = |1⟩⟨1| + |g⟩⟨g|. (10.179)

K 0.45 0.19 0.31 – – – 52°C [99] 27°C [99] 117°C [76]

Rb 0.35 0.16 0.14 0.12 0.13 0.159 80°C [78] 27°C [100] 27°C [100] 27°C [59] 47°C [101] 60°C [102]

For strongly depolarizing walls with |α| ≈ 1 – except for α = 0 for |0⟩ – we see from (10.168) that 1/μ ≈ λ. For situations of practical interest we will usually have ⟨μ| n⋅∇r ρ⟩ ≈ ⟨μ|ρ⟩/l, where the characteristic length l was given by (10.155). Then all terms in the sum of (10.179) will be smaller than the first term, |0⟩⟨0|ρ⟩, by a factor of order λ / l ≪ 1. We can therefore ignore all but the first term in (10.179) and use the resulting equation, ρ⟩ = |0⟩⟨0|ρ⟩, (10.180)

as a Dirichlet boundary condition to replace the Robin boundary condition (10.166) for strongly depolarizing walls.

Slowest Diffusion Mode For walls that are sufficiently depolarizing that (10.180) is an appropriate boundary condition, it is often adequate to represent the spatial variation of ρ⟩ with the most slowly relaxing diffusion mode, of spatial frequency k ≈ π / l, where l is the characteristic length (10.155). Then ∇² ρ⟩ = -k² [ρ⟩ - |0⟩⟨0|ρ⟩], and (10.151) reduces to dρ⟩ / dt = -Γ_d [ρ⟩ - |0⟩⟨0|ρ⟩]. (10.181)

The diffusional damping coefficient is Γ_d = k² D. (10.182)

Relaxation like that described by (10.181) is often called uniform relaxation. In the case of an atom without coherence, it is the kind of relaxation one would get if atoms were collisionally removed from each sublevel |μ⟩ at the rate Γ_d, and transferred with equal likelihood (uniformly) to each of the |g⟩ sublevels of the atom.

mic ground state. Some values of D are shown in Table 10.6.

## 10.11 Adsorption on the Walls

During the time the atom is adsorbed on the wall, it will be subject to the perturbation V_CF, the sum of a time-independent part, V, that plays much the same role as the time-independent potential V of a van der Waals molecule and a part, F, that fluctuates while the atom is hopping along the surface. In analogy to (10.50), we assume that the density matrix, σ, of atoms stuck to the wall evolves at the rate: |σ̇⟩ = -G|σ⟩ + |σ₀⟩. (10.183)

Free atoms close to the wall have a density matrix σ₀. They are adsorbed on the walls at the rate 1/τ_f. The damping operator for the adsorbed atoms is: G = γ_d H_N^{eff} + Γ = (γ_d + γ_{kN}) |kN⟩⟨kN|. (10.184)

Atoms are desorbed from the wall at the rate γ_d = 1/τ_d, where τ_d is the mean lifetime on the wall. The static Hamiltonian for atoms on the wall is: H_N^{eff} = H_N^{0} + V_N. (10.185)

As we will discuss below, the fluctuating perturbation, F, leads to polarization damping, described by the superoperator Γ, which we will discuss in more detail below. The eigenvectors |kN) and eigenvalues (γ_d + γ_{kN}) of G are determined by the eigenvalue equation: G|kN) = (γ_d + γ_{kN})|kN). (10.186)

In analogy to (10.55), the exact solution to (10.184) is: |σ(t)) = ∫_{-∞}^t dt' exp{-G(t-t')}|σ₀(t')). (10.187)

Here σ = σ(t) is the density matrix of atoms adsorbed at some surface element of the wall at time t and σ₀ = σ(t₀) is the density matrix of nearby free atoms at an earlier time t₀ < t. We assume that the desorption rate, γ_d, is so large compared with 1/τ_f that we can use (10.57) for |σ₀⟩ in (10.186). As in (10.57), we find: |σ⟩ = γ_d τ_f |σ₀⟩. (10.188)

In analogy to (10.58), we find that the S-matrix for adsorbed atoms is: ⟨S⟩ = γ_d ∫_0^∞ dt exp{-Gt} exp{i(H_N^{0}/ħ)t} = Σ_{kN} |kN⟩⟨kN|⟨k|S|k⟩ / [1 + (γ_{kN} - iω)τ_d]. (10.189)

The free-atom basis states |k⟩ and Bohr frequencies ω were given by (10.62). Using (10.189) with (10.22), we find the accommodation operator: A = 1 - ⟨S⟩ = Σ_{kN} |kN⟩⟨kN|⟨k|⟨k| [γ_{kN} / (1 + (γ_{kN} - iω)τ_d)]. (10.190)

Populations We are often interested in the populations of the atomic sublevels |kN)(kN|. For populations, the Bohr frequencies vanish, ω = 0, so we can write (10.190) for the population subspace as: A = Σ_{kN} α_{kN} |kN)(kN|, where α_{kN} = γ_{kN} τ_d / (1 + γ_{kN} τ_d). (10.191)

The wall will be a weak depolarizer of the population eigenobservable |kN)(kN|, that is, α_{kN} ≪ 1, when the dwell time τ_d is sufficiently short, or the wall-damping rate γ_{kN} is sufficiently slow that γ_{kN} τ_d ≪ 1. In the other extreme of long dwell times and rapid wall-damping rates, when γ_{kN} τ_d ≫ 1, the accommodation coefficient will approach its maximum value, α_{kN} = 1, and the component, |kN)(kN|σ⟩, of the density matrix will be destroyed at every collision with the wall.

The Fluctuating Wall Interaction F We assume the mean value of ⟨F⟩ is zero, and that we can define an autocorrelation function: ⟨⟨FF⟩⟩_τ = lim_{T→∞} (1/T) ∫_{-T/2}^{T/2} F(t-τ)F(t) dt = ⟨F^2⟩_0 C(τ). (10.192)

The relative autocorrelation function, C(τ), is a scalar function of the delay, τ, and C(0) = 1, C(-τ) = C(τ) and C(τ) → 0 as τ → ∞. (10.193)

For the statistical analysis of F, suppose that we define a finite sample of the fluctuating perturbation as F_T = F(t) for |t| ≤ T/2 and F_T = 0 for |t| > T/2. Then we can write F_T as the Fourier transform: F_T(t) = ∫ [F_M(ω)/(2πT)] e^{-iωt} dω. (10.194)

Substituting (10.194) into (10.192), we find: ⟨⟨FF⟩⟩_τ = (1/(2πT)) ∫ |F_M(ω)|^2 e^{iωτ} dω = ⟨|F_M(0)|^2⟩ / (2πT) ∫ J(ω) e^{iωτ} dω = ⟨F^2⟩_0 ∫ J(ω) e^{iωτ} dω / (2πT) = ⟨F^2⟩_0 C(τ). (10.195)

In analogy to (10.37), we have defined the relative power spectrum, J(ω), by: ⟨|F_M(ω)|^2⟩ = ⟨|F_M(0)|^2⟩ J(ω). (10.196)

We note that J(0) = 1 and J(ω) = J(-ω). (10.197)

Equating the last two lines of (10.195), we find a version of the Wiener–Khintchine theorem: ∫ J(ω) e^{iωτ} dω = C(τ) ∫ J(ω) dω. (10.198)

Integrating both sides of (10.198) over τ and using (10.197), we find the “uncertainty principle”: Δτ Δω = 2π, (10.199)

where the full width of the autocorrelation function, Δτ, and the full width of the power spectrum, Δω, are Δτ = ∫ C(τ) dτ and Δω = ∫ J(ω) dω. (10.200)

Relaxation on the Wall The evolution of the density matrix, |σ⟩ = |σ(t)⟩, of a single adsorbed atom will be given by: ∂|σ⟩/∂t = (1/iħ)(H_N^{eff} + F)|σ⟩. (10.201)

The small, time-independent part of the wall perturbation, V, can be ignored compared with H for this analysis. The interaction-picture density matrix, |σ̃⟩ = |σ̃(t)⟩ = e^{iH_N^{0}t/ħ} |σ⟩, (10.202)

evolves at the rate: ∂|σ̃⟩/∂t = (1/iħ) F̃ |σ̃⟩, (10.203)

where F̃ = e^{iH_N^{0}t/ħ} F e^{-iH_N^{0}t/ħ}. (10.204)

The perturbation-series solution to (10.203) is: |σ̃⟩ = [1 + (1/(iħ)) ∫_0^t F̃(t₀) dt₀ + (1/(iħ)^2) ∫_0^t F̃(t₀) dt₀ ∫_0^{t₀} F̃(t₀') dt₀' + ...] |σ̃₀⟩, (10.205)

where |σ̃₀⟩ = |σ̃(0)⟩, F̃(t₀) = F̃(t₀), and F̃(t₀') = F̃(t₀'). The angle brackets indicate an ensemble average. It is convenient to use the same symbol |σ̃⟩ to denote the density matrix of a single adsorbed atom in (10.203) and the ensemble average in (10.205).

For small t, we find to second order in F, |σ̃⟩ = (1 - X) |σ̃₀⟩, (10.206)

where X = (1/ħ²) ∫_0^t F̃(t₀) dt₀ ∫_0^{t₀} F̃(t₀') dt₀'. (10.207)

Letting t₀' = t₀ - τ, we find that the matrix elements, X_{jl} = (j|X|l), of (10.207) are: X_{jl} = (1/ħ²) ∫_0^t dt₀ e^{iω_{jl}t₀} ∫_0^{t₀} dτ ⟨F_{jl}(t₀) F_{lj}(t₀-τ)⟩.

The element of the damping matrix of the last line are

Γ_jk = ⟨F_j F_k⟩ [J(ω_kl) − iK(ω_kl)]

Δω → 2

⟨F_jM(0) F_kM(0)⟩ [J(ω_kl) − iK(ω_kl)] / (2T^2)

Then we can let t′ → ∞ in the upper limit of the integral of the second line. To derive the last line, we interchanged the order of integration over τ and ω in the preceding line, and used the identity (10.35). The frequency-shift coefficient K(ω_kl) was defined in terms of the relative power spectrum J(ω) by (10.41).

Multiplying both sides of (10.206) on the left by ⟨j_α|, differentiating the resulting equation with respect to time, and letting Δt → 0 for small time, we find

d⟨j_α⟩/dt = −i ω_jl Γ_jl ⟨l_α⟩. (10.210)

The matrix elements Γ_jl of (10.209) can be used to construct the operator Γ:

Γ = Σ Γ_jl |j⟩⟨l|. (10.211)

Then we can substitute (10.202) into (10.210) to find

d⟨σ⟩/dt = − (i/ħ) H′ C Γ ⟨σ⟩, (10.212)

as anticipated by (10.183) and (10.184).

In the analysis of (10.208) we have assumed that it is possible to choose a time t that is small enough that |X| << 1 but that is large enough that t >> Δt. This is the familiar assumption used to derive Fermi’s golden rule, a slightly generalized example of which is (10.212). Comparing (10.212) with (10.21), we see that the nominal collision rate 1/T for weak collisions in a gas plays the same role as the sampling rate 1/T for a fluctuating perturbation on the wall, and TΓ plays the same role as the accommodation operator A. Comparing (10.209) with (10.40), we see that the potential V for a weak binary collision plays the same role as the sampled, fluctuating potential F for the wall relaxation.

## 10.12 Spin Exchange between Pairs of Alkali-Metal Atoms

Binary collisions between alkali-metal atoms lead to very efficient exchange of the electron spins because of the large difference between the triplet and singlet potentials of the atomic pair. The potentials, similar to those shown in Figure 10.7, are much too large for the semiclassical analysis of the preceding sections to work. Both the translational and the spin states should be treated quantum mechanically with partial-wave scattering theory.

10.12.1 Partial-Wave Analysis

We consider two alkali-metal atoms, 1 and 2, with masses M₁ and M₂, and with reduced mass M = M₁M₂/(M₁+M₂). Let the nucleus of the first atom be located at r₁ and the nucleus of the second atom be located at r₂. Atom 2 is displaced from atom 1 by r = r₂ − r₁. We write the wave function of the pair as the column vector |ψ⟩ = Σ |μ₁μ₂⟩ ⟨μ₁μ₂|ψ⟩. (10.213)

The basis states, |μ₁μ₂⟩ = |μ₂⟩ ⊗ |μ₁⟩, are Kronecker products analogous to those of (10.97). The amplitude ⟨μ₁μ₂|ψ⟩ will depend on the interatomic separation r. The relative velocity v and the kinetic energy E of the atoms before the collision are related to the spatial frequency k of the initial state by

v = ħk / M and E = ħ²k² / (2M). (10.214)

The pair wave function |ψ⟩ obeys the Schrödinger equation:

iħ d|ψ⟩/dt = − (ħ²/2M) ∇² |ψ⟩ + V |ψ⟩. (10.215)

The Laplacian operator ∇² acts on the translational part of |ψ⟩, but has no effect on the spin parts. The interaction potential, V, acts on both the translational and the spin parts of |ψ⟩.

Potentials: The interaction potential

V = V_d + V_e, (10.216)

is the sum of a direct potential V_d and an exchange potential V_e. We ignore other much smaller contributions to V, for example, the magnetic dipole interaction of the electronic spins. The exchange potential has singlet and triplet parts and can be written as

V_e = Π[0] V[0] + Π[1] V[1] = V_d + V_e Π_e. (10.217)

As can be seen from Figure 10.7, the ¹Σ singlet potential V[0] = V[0](r) and the ³Σ triplet potential V[1] = V[1](r) differ by about 0.5 eV near the classical turning points. The projection operators of (10.217) are given in terms of the electronic spin matrices, written in analogy to (10.100) as S₁ → (1/2) ⊗ S₁ and S₂ → S₂ ⊗ (1/2), of the two atoms:

Π[0] = (1/4) − S₁·S₂ = (1/2)(1 − Π_e) (10.218)

and

Π[1] = (3/4) + S₁·S₂ = (1/2)(1 + Π_e). (10.219)

The exchange operator is

Π_e = +2 S₁·S₂ = Π[1] − Π[0]. (10.220)

Since Π[0] and Π[1] are projection operators we must have

Π[0] Π[0] = Π[0], Π[1] Π[1] = Π[1], and Π[0] Π[1] = Π[1] Π[0] = 0. (10.221)

From (10.218)–(10.221) we see that

Π_e² = Π[1] + Π[0] = 1. (10.222)

Figure 10.7 Rb–Rb potentials, from [107]. The electronvolt-scale difference between the singlet and triplet potentials leads to efficient spin exchange even though the duration of a binary collision is only a few picoseconds.

The direct and exchange potentials of (10.217) are

V_d = (V[1] + V[0])/2 and V_e = (V[1] − V[0])/2. (10.223)

The spin-axis potential V_sa of (10.216) is much smaller than the exchange potential V_e and is given by [108, 115]

V_sa = (2λ/3) [S·(n₀n₀ − 1)·S]. (10.224)

The total electronic spin of the two atoms is

S = S₁ + S₂, (10.225)

and the unit vector pointing from atom 1 to atom 2 is

n₀ = r / |r|. (10.226)

For the time being, we will ignore the small spin-axis interaction (10.224). Its effects can be analyzed either by a classical-path calculation for an orbit governed by the triplet potential \(V_{\{1\}}\) or with the aid of the distorted-wave Born approximation [115].

Scattering State To calculate the relaxation of the spins of atoms of species 1 with number density \([N_1]\) as a result of collisions with atoms of species 2 with number density \([N_2]\) we write the scattering state as

\[ |\psi\rangle = [N_1] e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}} C F |\psi_i\rangle, \]

(10.227)

where \(|\psi_i\rangle\), the initial spin state of the atomic pair is given by (10.96) with \(|\psi_i\rangle\) and \(|\psi_i\rangle\) now representing the spin states of two alkali-metal atoms rather than the spin states of an alkali-metal atom and the nuclear spin of a noble gas atom. Species 2 might be K and species 1 might be Rb. The scattering-amplitude operator, \(\mathbf{F} = F(\mathbf{n}, \mathbf{n}')\), depends on the directions \(\mathbf{n}\) and \(\mathbf{n}'\) of the incident and scattered waves, and we write it as

\[ \mathbf{F} = \Pi_{\{0\}} f_{\{0\}} + \Pi_{\{1\}} f_{\{1\}} = f_d + \Pi_e f_e.

\]

(10.228)

The direct and exchange scattering amplitudes, \(f_d\) and \(f_e\), are related to the triplet and singlet scattering amplitudes, \(f_{\{1\}}\) and \(f_{\{0\}}\), by

\[ f_d = \frac{f_{\{1\}} + f_{\{0\}}}{2} \quad \text{and} \quad f_e = \frac{f_{\{1\}} - f_{\{0\}}}{2}.

\]

(10.229)

The singlet (\(s = 0\)) and triplet (\(s = 1\)) amplitudes are given by the partial-wave expansion

\[ f_{\{s\}} = \frac{2}{ik} \sum_{l} (e^{2i\delta_{ls}} - 1) Y_l(\mathbf{n})^* Y_l(\mathbf{n}').

\]

(10.230)

The scattering amplitudes, \(f_{\{0\}}\), \(f_{\{1\}}\), \(f_d\), and \(f_e\), depend implicitly on the relative energy \(E\) (or spatial frequency \(k\)) of the colliding pair, and on the directions \(\mathbf{n}\) and \(\mathbf{n}'\) of the incident and scattered waves. The unit vector \(\mathbf{n}'\) was defined by (10.226) and the unit vector \(\mathbf{n}\) is

\[ \mathbf{n} = \frac{\mathbf{k}}{k}.

\]

(10.231)

We have written the spherical harmonics as \(Y_l(\mathbf{n}) = Y_{lm}(\theta, \phi)\), where the co-latitude angle \(\theta\) and the azimuthal angle \(\phi\) parameterize the unit vector \(\mathbf{n}\), as in (5.57). Similarly, \(Y_l(\mathbf{n}') = Y_{lm}(\theta', \phi')\). The generalized dot product of the spherical harmonics is related to the Legendre polynomial \(P_l\) by the addition theorem,

\[ Y_l(\mathbf{n})^* Y_l(\mathbf{n}') = Y_{lm}(\mathbf{n}) Y_{lm}^*(\mathbf{n}') = \frac{2l+1}{4\pi} P_l(\mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{n}').

\]

(10.232)

The phase shifts, \(\delta_{ls}\) for the \(l\)th partial wave can be found by solving the radial Schrödinger equations for singlet and triplet waves of orbital angular momentum \(l\),

\[ \left[-\frac{d^2}{dr^2} + \frac{l(l+1)}{r^2} + \frac{2M}{\hbar^2} \left(V_{\{s\}} - k^2\right)\right] g_{ls} = 0.

\]

(10.233)

The boundary conditions of the radial wavefunction for small and large \(r\) are

\[ g_{ls}(r) \to 0 \quad \text{as} \quad r \to 0 \quad \text{and} \quad g_{ls}(r) \sim \sin(kr - \pi l/2 + \delta_{ls}).

\]

(10.234)

Equation of Continuity From the Schrödinger equation (10.215) we see that

\[ \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \rho + \frac{1}{i\hbar} [\mathbf{H}, \rho] = \frac{1}{i\hbar} [V, \rho], \]

(10.235)

where the current operator is

\[ \mathbf{j} = \frac{1}{2iM} \left(|\psi\rangle \langle\psi| \nabla - \text{h.c.} \right).

\]

(10.236)

The equation of continuity (10.235) includes a source term on the right which represents the transfer of atoms between spin states by the potential \(V\). We find the rate of change of the spin density matrix due to spin-exchange scattering by integrating the current density over the surface of a sphere of radius \(r \gg 1/k\), where the scattered wave is well described by the asymptotic expression (10.227) and where \(V\) is negligibly small:

\[ \dot{\rho} = \int r^2 d\Omega' \mathbf{n}' \cdot \mathbf{j}_0 = \frac{\hbar k N}{2M r^2} \int r^2 d\Omega' \mathbf{n}' \cdot \mathbf{n} \left| e^{i k \mathbf{r} \cdot (\mathbf{n} - \mathbf{n}')} + \mathbf{n}_0 F_0 |\psi_i\rangle \right|^2 = \frac{\hbar k N}{2M} \int d\Omega' \left( F_0 - F_0^\dagger \right)

= \frac{\hbar k N}{2M} \int d\Omega' \mathbf{n}' \cdot (\mathbf{n} + \mathbf{n}') \left[ e^{-i k r (1 - \mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{n}')} F_0^\dagger - e^{i k r (1 - \mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{n}')} F_0 \right].

\]

(10.237)

The element of the surface is \(r^2 \mathbf{n}' d\Omega'\), where \(d\Omega' = \sin\theta' d\theta' d\phi'\) is an increment of solid angle, centered on the direction \(\mathbf{n}'\) of the scattered wave. We use h.c. to denote the Hermitian conjugate of the preceding expression. The current density at the surface increment is \(\mathbf{j}_0 = \mathbf{j}(r\mathbf{n}')\), and the lateral-scattering amplitude is \(F_0 = F(\mathbf{n}, \mathbf{n}')\). The density matrix, \(\rho\), before scattering is related to the wave function, \(|\psi_i\rangle\), before scattering by (10.103).

For \(kr \gg 1\), the contributions to the integrals containing the factors \(e^{\pm i k r (1 - \mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{n}')}\) come almost entirely from the forward direction where \(\mathbf{n}' \approx \mathbf{n}\), the phase is "stationary", and we can write

\[ \int d\Omega' \mathbf{n}' \cdot (\mathbf{n} + \mathbf{n}') e^{\pm i k r (1 - \mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{n}')} F_0 \approx F_N \int d\Omega' (1 + \mathbf{n}' \cdot \mathbf{n}) e^{\pm i k r (1 - \mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{n}')} \approx \frac{4\pi i}{k r} F_N.

\]

(10.238)

The forward-scattering amplitude is

\[ F_N = f_{Nd} + f_{Ne} \Pi_e, \]

(10.239)

where \(F_N = F(\mathbf{n}, \mathbf{n})\), \(f_{Nd} = f_d(\mathbf{n}, \mathbf{n})\) and \(f_{Ne} = f_e(\mathbf{n}, \mathbf{n})\).

Optical Theorem Using (10.238) in (10.237), we find the fundamental equation for the evolution of the density matrix,

\[ \dot{\rho} = \frac{2\pi i \hbar [N]}{M} \left( F_N \rho - \rho F_N^\dagger \right) + \frac{\hbar k [N]}{2M} \int d\Omega' F_0 \rho F_0^\dagger.

\]

(10.240)

Scattering conserves the total number of atoms, so we must have \(\text{Tr}[\dot{\rho}] = 0\) in (10.240). This leads to the optical theorem,

\[ 2\pi i \left[ F_N - F_N^\dagger \right] + k \int d\Omega' F_0^\dagger F_0 = 0.

\]

(10.241)

Substituting the scattering amplitude (10.228) into (10.241), and carrying out the integrations, we find the identities

\[ k(\sigma_d + \sigma_e) = 4\pi f_{Nd}'', \]

(10.242)

\[ k(\sigma_e + \sigma_d) = 4\pi f_{Ne}''.

\]

(10.243)

We have used single and double primes to denote the real and imaginary parts of a complex number, for example, \(f_{Nd} = f_{Nd}' + i f_{Nd}''\). The cross sections are defined by

\[ \sigma_{ij} = \int f_i^* f_j d\Omega' = \ Partial-Wave Cross Sections Using the optical-theorem identities (10.242) and (10.243) and the scattering amplitude (10.228) in (10.240), we find $\dot{\mathcal{P}}_D v[N] \sigma_0(\Pi \cdot \Pi -) \text{Ci} \sigma_{00}[\Pi,]$. (10.245)

The relative velocity of the colliding pair is $v = \hbar k/M$, and the number density of atoms of species 2 is $[N]$. Following Kartoshkin [109], we have defined a complex, spin-exchange cross section, $\sigma = \sigma_0 + \text{Ci} \sigma_{00} = \frac{\pi}{2k^2} \sum (2l+1)(1-e^{-2i\delta_l})$. (10.246)

The difference between the triplet and the singlet phase shift for the $l$th partial wave, which we will call the differential phase shift, is $\delta_l = \delta_{\{1\}} - \delta_{\{0\}}$. (10.247)

The real part of the cross section (10.246) is $\sigma_0 = \sigma = \frac{\pi}{k^2} \sum (2l+1)\sin^2\delta_l$, (10.248)

and the imaginary part is $\sigma_{00} = f_N^0 + \text{Ci} \sigma_{00} = \frac{2\pi}{k^2} \sum (2l+1)\sin\delta_l \cos\delta_l$. (10.249)

We can write (10.245) in the same form as the collisional part of (10.20): $\dot{\mathcal{P}}_D \Gamma \sum_l \{S_l \cdot \mathcal{P} S_l^\dagger - \mathcal{P}\}$ $= \Gamma \sum_l \{\sin^2\delta_l (\Pi \cdot \Pi -) + i\sin\delta_l \cos\delta_l [\Pi, ]\}$. (10.250)

The partial rates are $\Gamma_l = \frac{\pi(2l+1)}{k^2} Nv$, (10.251)

and the S-matrix for the $l$th partial wave is $S_l = e^{i\delta_l \Pi_e} = \cos\delta_l + i\sin\delta_l \Pi$. (10.252)

In writing (10.252), we expanded the exponential as a power series in $i\delta_l \Pi$, and we noted that $1 = \Pi^2 = \Pi^4 = \cdots$ and $\Pi = \Pi^3 = \Pi^5 = \cdots$.

Taking the partial trace of (10.245) over the spin coordinates of the second atom, in accordance with (10.105), we find $\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{\{1\}} = \text{Tr}_2[\dot{\mathcal{P}}] = -\Gamma_{\{1\}} (\mathcal{P}_{\{1\}} \cdot S - \langle S \rangle - \{ \mathcal{P}_{\{1\}}, S \} \cdot 2iS - \mathcal{P}_{\{1\}} S \cdot 2i) + \langle S \rangle \cdot S, (\mathcal{P}_{\{1\}})$. (10.253)

Here the exchange rate $\Gamma_{\{1\}}$ and the frequency-shift parameter $\Delta$ are given by $\Gamma_{\{1\}} = Nf_2 gv\sigma_0 \quad \text{and} \quad \Delta = \frac{\sigma_{00}}{\sigma_0}$. (10.254)

An analogous equation for $\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{\{2\}}$ can be obtained from (10.253) by interchanging the subscripts, $\{1\} \leftrightarrow \{2\}$.

Like Atoms For a vapor of identical alkali-metal atoms, we have $N_{\{1\}} = N_{\{2\}} = N$, $\mathcal{P}_{\{1\}} = \mathcal{P}_{\{2\}} = \mathcal{P}$, $\Gamma_{\{1\}} = \Gamma_{\{2\}} = \Gamma$, and $S_1 = S_2 = S$, so for self-spin-exchange, (10.253) becomes $\dot{\mathcal{P}} = -\Gamma (\mathcal{P} \cdot S - \langle S \rangle - \{ \mathcal{P}, S \} \cdot 2iS - \mathcal{P} S \cdot 2i) + \langle S \rangle \cdot S$. (10.255)

Unlike expressions leading up to (10.255), where the density matrix $\mathcal{P}$ without a subscript denoted a pair of atoms, the density matrix $\mathcal{P}$ of (10.255) is that of a single atom.

Spin Temperature The evolution equation (10.253) is identical to (10.113) if we make the replacements $\langle S_1 \rangle \rightarrow \langle S \rangle, \quad \langle S_2 \rangle \rightarrow \langle K \rangle, \quad \Gamma_{\{1\}} \rightarrow \frac{\Gamma_{\{1\}} h\phi^2 i}{h\phi i},$ $\Delta \rightarrow -2 \frac{\Delta}{h\phi^2 i}$. (10.256)

We already demonstrated that the spin-exchange distribution, $\mathcal{P}_{\{1\}}$, of (10.126) is the steady-state solution of (10.110), of which (10.113) is a special case. Therefore, the steady-state solutions of (10.253) and (10.255) are spin-temperature distributions like (10.126) [110]. For unlike alkali-metal atoms, the spin-temperature parameter $\Delta$ is the same for both species.

Evolution in Liouville Space The Liouville-space analog of (10.255) is $| \dot{\mathcal{P}} ) = -\Gamma_{se} \langle \mathcal{A}_{se} | \mathcal{P} )$, (10.257)

where the dimensionless spin-exchange operator is $\langle \mathcal{A}_{se} | = \mathcal{A}_{sd} - \langle S \rangle \cdot \mathcal{A}_{ex} - 2i\Delta \langle S \rangle \cdot S^\circ$. (10.258)

The S-damping operator, $\mathcal{A}_{sd}$, was defined by (6.88), and the exchange operator, $\mathcal{A}_{ex}$, was defined by (6.90). From (4.85) we see that $S^\circ = S[ \cdot S]$. We note that $\mathcal{A}_{ex}^\dagger = S[ +S] + 2iS[\Delta S]$, (10.259)

and that $\mathcal{A}_{ex}^\dagger | M ) = | \{M, S\} + 2iS \Delta MS )$. (10.260)

Some useful identities that follow from setting $M = S_i$, $M = I_i$, and $M = 1_{fgg}$ in (10.260) and taking the Hermitian conjugate of the result are $(S_j | \mathcal{A}_{ex, j} = \delta_{ij} (1_{fgg} |, \quad (I_i | \mathcal{A}_{ex, j} = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad (1_{fgg} | \mathcal{A}_{ex, j} = 0$. (10.261)

Analogous identities for $S^\circ$ are $(S_i | S^\circ_j = i\Delta_{ijk} (S_k |, \quad (I_i | S^\circ_j = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad (1_{fgg} | S^\circ_j = 0$. (10.262)

There is an implicit sum over the repeated index $k$ of (10.262).

Multiplying (10.257) on the left by $(S_j|$ and $(I_j|$, respectively, and using the identities (10.261), (10.262), and (10.87), we find $(S_j | \dot{\mathcal{P}}_{se}) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad (I_j | \dot{\mathcal{P}}_{se}) = 0$. (10.263)

Spin-exchange collisions conserve both $\langle S \rangle = (S | \mathcal{P})$ and $\langle I \rangle = (I | \mathcal{P})$. The magnetic dipole hyperfine interaction $A_{fgg} I \cdot S$, together with a nonzero, longitudinal magnetic field, must act in the intervals between spin-exchange collisions to drive the density matrix to the spin-temperature distribution (10.126).

We can write (10.257) as the matrix Riccati equation: $| \dot{\mathcal{P}} ) = -\Gamma_{se} \mathcal{A}_{sd} | \mathcal{P} ) + \Gamma_{se} \mathcal{B}_{se} | \mathcal{P} ) \rangle \langle \mathcal{P} |$. (10.264)

The bilinear exchange operator is $\mathcal{B}_{se} = (S | \cdot \rangle \langle \cdot | \mathcal{A}_{ex} + 2i\Delta S^\circ = \sum_{j=1}^3 (S_j | \cdot \rangle \langle \cdot | \mathcal{A}_{ex, j} + 2i\Delta S^\circ_j$. (10.265)

Here $\mathcal{B}_{se}$ is an $[I]^2[S]^2 \times [I]^2[S]^2$-dimensional matrix. But experimental conditions are often simple enough that it is possible to use much smaller, compactified versions of $\mathcal{B}_{se}$.

Spin-Temperature Limit of Relaxation Processes Let the evolution of the atoms be dominated by the common Hamiltonian $H = H_{fgg}$ of (2.10), spin exchange, and a linear relaxation process that destroys spin polarization. The evolution on of the density matrix in such a situation is then given by \[ \frac{d\rho}{dt} = -i[H, \rho] - \Gamma A \rho, \quad (10.266)

\]

Here \(\Gamma\) is the characteristic rate of the relaxation process, and \(A\) is the corresponding dimensionless operator that accounts for transitions between the various sublevels, dephasing collisions, and so on. Multiplying (10.266) on the left by \(\langle F_z \rangle\), and noting that \(\langle F_z H \rangle = D([H, F_z]) = 0\), we find \[ \frac{d\langle F_z \rangle}{dt} = -\Gamma \langle F_z A \rangle - \alpha \Gamma \langle F_z \rangle, \quad (10.267)

\]

Equation 10.267 says that \(\langle F_z \rangle = \langle F_z \rangle\) decays at the rate \(\alpha \Gamma\), where the slowing-down factor (the accommodation coefficient) is \[ \alpha = \frac{\langle F_z A \rangle}{\langle F_z \rangle}. \quad (10.268)

\]

If the spin-exchange collisions are sufficiently rapid, it will be a good approximation to write \(\rho \propto e^{-\beta F_z}/Z\), where the spin-temperature parameter \(\beta\) will depend on time. Then the slowing-down factor, \(\alpha = \alpha(\beta)\), will be a well-defined function of the spin-temperature parameter \(\beta\) and can be written as \[ \alpha = \frac{\langle F_z | A | F_z \rangle}{\langle F_z | F_z \rangle}. \quad (10.269)

\]

We can readily evaluate the low-polarization limit of (10.269) by making the approximation \(e^{-\beta F_z} \approx 1 - \beta F_z\) for \(\beta \to 0\), and noting that \(\langle F_z \rangle = \text{Tr}[F_z] = 0\), and \(A |0\rangle = 0\) for relaxation processes that cannot create polarized atoms. Then we find \[ \alpha \to \frac{\langle F_z A F_z \rangle}{\langle F_z F_z \rangle}, \quad \text{for } \beta \to 0. \quad (10.270)

\]

For example, suppose that the spin is lost by S-damping in the presence of much more rapid spin-exchange collisions. Then \(A = A_{sd}\), where \(A_{sd}\) was given by (6.88). Since \(\langle F_z A_{sd} \rangle = \langle S_z \rangle\) from (10.87), we see that the low-polarization limit of the slowing-down factor is \[ \alpha_{sd} = \frac{\langle S_z F_z \rangle}{\langle F_z F_z \rangle} = \frac{3}{[I]2C2}. \quad (10.271)

\]

**10.12.2 Semiclassical Calculation of Partial-Wave Cross Sections**

For optical pumping experiments at room temperature and higher, several hundred partial waves may have non-negligible phase shifts, \(\delta(s)\). Finding these phase shifts from numerical solutions of the radial Schrödinger equation (10.233) is time-consuming. The de Broglie wavelengths of the colliding atoms are short compared with the range of the potentials, so the phase shifts can be found accurately and much more simply by semiclassical methods. In the Wentzel–Kramers–Brillouin approximation, the phase shifts are [111]

\[ \delta_l(s) = \int_{r_0}^{\infty} (q_l(s) - k) dr + (l + 1/2) - k r_l(s). \quad (10.272)

\]

Here the superscript \(s = 0\) for the singlet phase shift and \(s = 1\) for the triplet phase shift. The classical radial component of the momentum (in units of \(\hbar\)) is \[ q_l(s) = \left[ 2M(E - V_l(s)/\hbar^2 - (l + 1/2)^2/r^2) \right]^{1/2}.

\]

Singlet and triplet potential curves \(V_l(0)\) and \(V_l(1)\) can be found in the literature [107]. The classical turning point, \(r_l(s)\), is the value of \(r\) for which \(q_l(s) = 0\). For some angular momenta and energies, there are three turning points. The two smallest are the turning points of a potential well in which metastable dimer molecules can be formed. For these cases the outermost turning point is used in (10.272), since the effects of tunneling into the well can be neglected.

Partial cross sections calculated from (10.248) and (10.249) with the Wentzel–Kramers–Brillouin phase shifts of (10.272) are shown in Figure 10.8. The differential phase shifts \(\delta_l = \delta_l(1) - \delta_l(0)\) are hundreds of radians, and are mostly due to the singlet potential. They vary rapidly with \(l\), producing oscillations in the partial cross sections, until the effective singlet potential \(V_l(0) = V_l(0) + \hbar^2 (l + 1/2)^2/2\mu r^2\) produces a barrier with a large-\(r\) turning point at the collision energy. This is illustrated in Figure 10.7. For angular momenta beyond this, the atoms no longer sample the strong attractive singlet well and the phase shifts become relatively small and rapidly approach zero.

Partial cross sections for Rb–Rb spin exchange, at a collision energy of 35 meV. The straight line denotes the unitarity limit \(\pi(2l + 1)/k^2\).

From Figure 10.8 we see that the imaginary part of the spin-exchange cross section, \(\sigma_l^{00}\), oscillates about zero with increasing \(l\) and that the real part, \(\sigma_l^0\), is never negative. Therefore, the spin-exchange frequency shifts, proportional to \(\sigma_l^{00} = \sigma_l^0\), are much smaller than the spin-exchange rates, which are proportional to \(\sigma_l^0 = \sigma_l\).

Experimental Measurements We have collected some experimental measurements of spin-exchange cross sections in Table 10.7.

While alkali–alkali collisions conserve spin to a first approximation, the spin-axis coupling (10.224) causes some loss of spin to the orbital angular momentum of the colliding pair. The coupling \(\lambda\) can exceed 10 GHz at typical turning points of alkali–alkali collisions. For the case of short-duration collisions, the effect of the spin–axis interaction can be accurately described as an S-damping process with a rate analogous to (10.94). It was recently discovered experimentally through magnetic decoupling experiments that the formation of triplet van der Waals molecules contributes to relaxation at a rate that is comparable to the electron randomization rate [108,113]. The molecular contribution to the relaxation couples to the nuclear spins and therefore cannot properly be characterized as S-damping.

**10.13 Pressure Dependence of Relaxation in the Dark**

To illustrate some of the ideas of the previous sections, in this section we discuss a problem that is frequently encountered in practice: how spin-relaxation rates vary with pressure when both the spatial diffusion to the cell walls and gas-phase collisions are important.

There are two primary methods to prevent rapid depolarization of alkali-metal atoms by cell walls. The cell walls can be coated with hydrocarbons such as paraffin that allow for many wall collisions before relaxation occurs. This is quite effective for alkali-metal cells with no buffer gas. Alternatively, diffusion to the walls can be slowed down with a buffer gas. Increasing the buffer-gas pressure reduces the diffusion to the walls at the expense of increasing the spin-relaxation rates due to collisions with the buffer-gas atoms or molecules.

Here we consider the trade-offs between these two methods of suppressing wall relaxation. We suppose that we have prepared a spherical cell of radius a, and that the cell has been filled with a small amount of alkali metal and a buffer gas. For simplicity we will assume that the density of the alkali-metal vapor is large enough that spin-temperature prevails, and that the polarization is small. Then the mode amplitude of (10.159) can be chosen to be ((γ + j)D <hF_i. (10.274)

The mode amplitude must satisfy the Helmholtz equation (10.160), which is most conveniently written for a spherical cell as [∂/∂r (r^2 ∂/∂r) - L^2 + Ck^2] <hF_i> D 0. (10.275)

Although (10.275) describes the spatial dependence of a diffusion mode, it is also the equation of motion for a free particle, and a solution can therefore be written as a linear combination of the partial waves: <hF_i> D j_l (kr)Y_lm (θ,φ). (10.276)

Here Y_lm (θ,φ) is a spherical harmonic function of the colatitude and azimuthal angles θ and φ of the position vector r, and j_l (kr) is a spherical Bessel function.

The possible values of the spatial frequency k are determined by the boundary condition (10.166), which we write at r D a as j'_l D -μ j_l . (10.277)

dr l l For l D 0, when j_0 (x) D sinx/x, the boundary condition becomes μa D 1 - ka cotka . (10.278)

There are an infinite number of positive spatial frequencies, k, that are solutions of (10.278). We denote them in order of increasing magnitude by k_1, k_2, k_3, ... The first 10 values of k obtained by numerical solution of (10.278) are shown in Figure 10.9.

From (10.161) we see that the relaxation rate of the spherically symmetric mode n is the sum of a diffusional contribution proportional to the square of the spatial frequency and a contribution γ due to collisional relaxation, Γ_n D k_n^2 D Cγ . (10.279)

For S-damping collisions at the rate Γ_sd we would have γ_sd D α_sd Γ_sd , (10.280)

where the slowing-down factor α_sd for the spin-temperature equilibrium at low polarization was given by (10.271).

Once the k_n are found, the general solution for <hF_i> is <hF_i> D <hF_i_n> e^{-Γ_n t} , (10.281)

where <hF_i_n> D ∫_0^a <hF_i(r,0)> j_n (k_n r) r^2 dr (10.282)

gives the amplitude of the nth diffusion mode at time t D 0.

Weakly Depolarizing Walls Let us focus on the lowest spherically symmetric mode in a cell where the wall has such a small accommodation coefficient, α (<<) 1, that there is a range of low pressures where μa (<<) 1, but where the mean free path λ is still much smaller than the cell radius a so that the diffusion equation is a valid description of spatial transport of atomic polarization. For ka !0 one can readily verify that (10.278) gives k_1^2 ! (3μ)/(4a) ! (9α)/(4λa). (10.283)

The relaxation rate (10.279) becomes Γ_1 D C[X] - α_sd. (10.284)

The first term of (10.284) is independent of gas pressure and it is the relaxation rate for unconfined polarization (10.174) for a sphere of radius a. The second term is the contribution of collisions in the gas. The S-damping rate coefficient is D_ - D Γ_sd / [X], and the slowing-down factor was given by (10.271). Strongly Depolarizing Walls For walls with poor coatings or no coating at all, the accommodation coefficients α are not small enough to get the conditions of unconfined polarization mentioned above. 1 a 3σ k We have expressed the diffusion coefficient in terms of an effective diffusion cross section, σk, via D[X0] = v/3σk. For a wall coating to give the same relaxation time as the optimum number density (10.286) of a buffer gas, we equate the first term on the right of (10.284) to the minimum rate (10.287) to find that the accommodation coefficient of the wall must be 8π σ α 1 α(cid:8) sd sd (cid:6) , (10.289)

3 3σ 4000 where the numerical value assumes σsd = 10^−22 cm^2 and σk = 10^−15 cm^2, the approximate values for N2 buffer gas. Thus, the wall coating must allow several thousand collisions to give relaxation rates that are slower than cells filled with buffer gas. Note that although this conclusion is independent of the cell size, the minimum attainable relaxation rate is inversely proportional to the cell size. The density dependence of the lowest mode is shown in Figure 10.10.

## 10.14 Collisions of Excited Atoms

Binary depolarization and quenching cross sections of optically excited alkali-metal atoms in buffer gases are very much larger than those of the alkali-metal atoms in the ground state. For 2P_{3/2} states, the spin depolarization cross sections can be larger than gas kinetic cross sections. For 2P_{1/2} states, with their spherically symmetric charge distributions, as shown in Figure 2.3, the depolarization cross sections are large compared with those of the 2S_{1/2} ground state, but they can be substantially smaller than gas kinetic cross sections, especially for Cs (and presumably Fr), where the fine structure splitting between 2P_{3/2} and 2P_{1/2} states is many times larger than the mean thermal energy kT. The depolarization cross sections for atoms in 2P_{3/2} states, which have nonspherically symmetric charge distributions, are always large and comparable to gas-kinetic cross sections.

Collisions of alkali-metal atoms in the 2P_{3/2} state with molecular buffer gases such as N2 and H2 are particularly efficient in transferring the atoms to other electronic states, for example, to the other member, 2P_{1/2}, of the same fine-structure doublet, or to the 2S_{1/2} ground state. Vibrational and rotational degrees of freedom of the molecules absorb or release the energy needed for the atomic transition. The fine-structure splittings for the lighter alkali-metal atoms K, Na, and Li are small enough that collisions with monatomic noble gas atoms, especially He, can efficiently transfer atoms between the 2P_{3/2} and 2P_{1/2} states.

Notable Effects of Excited-State Collisions 1.  A binary collision of an excited atom with a buffer-gas atom or molecule lasts for only a few picoseconds, and there is not enough time for the relatively weak magnetic dipole or electric quadrupole interactions to change the polarization of the nucleus appreciably.

2.  The axis–orbit interactions are large enough to substantially change the electron polarization of an excited atom during a single collision. However, the collisions occur with all possible impact parameters and orbital planes. If the external magnetic field is zero, we see by symmetry that the collisions cannot change an electronic dipole polarization to a quadrupole polarization, and so on. For magnetic fields larger than a few teslas, there can be collisional mixing of the electronic multipolarities.

Detailed Analysis We write the collisionally induced relaxation of excited atoms, in accordance with (10.21), as @t jρ) = −Γ c Ac jρ). (10.290)

The collision rate of excited atoms with buffer-gas atoms or molecules is Γ c. In accordance with the two properties mentioned above, we write the accommodation operator as Ac = Σ_{lJJ'} α_l{JJ'} Π_l{JJ'}. (10.291)

The multipole transfer operators Π_l{JJ'} are defined by (11.4) in Chapter 11. Using (11.12) with (10.290), we find @/∂t (1J{Jj | ρ{JJ}) = −Γ c Σ_{J'} α_l{JJ'} (1J{J'j | ρ{J'J'}). (10.292)

We see that α_l{JJ'} is the probability per unit time that an atom, initially in the electronic state J, has been collisionally removed from that state and transferred to another electronic state. Similarly, we can interpret −α_l{JJ'} as the probability per unit time that an atom initially in the electronic state J' ≠ J is transferred to the state J. We assume the collisions conserve the number of atoms, so the accommodation coefficients must satisfy the constraint Σ_{lJJ'} α_l{JJ'} = 0. (10.293)

The coefficients α_l{JJ'} must satisfy additional constraints, for example, they must lead to a Boltzmann distribution of atoms among the energy sublevels.

If the orbit–axis potentials are known, the multipole transfer coefficients, α_l{JJ'}, can be calculated by a partial-wave analysis similar ar to that outlined for spin exchange. These parts of these potentials that are diagonal in J (the Born–Oppenheimer potentials) are fairly well known from calculations and experiment. However, little is known about the off-diagonal parts. We will simply regard the α_{JJ'} as parameters to be determined from experimental measurements. If the collisional transfer rate from the electronic state J to other states is negligible, we can set α_{l JJ'} = δ_{JJ'} α_l and Π_l = Π_{JJ} in (10.291) to find A_{cl} = α_{l} Π_{l}. (10.294) For (10.293) to remain valid, we must have α_{c} = 0.

If the excited state is ^2P_{1/2}, the only non-zero term in the sum (10.294) has l = 1, and (10.290) becomes ∂/∂t |j_d) = -Γ_{jd} |j_d) = -Γ_{jd} (2/3) J·[J] |j_d)), (10.295) where Γ_{jd} = Γ_{c} α_{1} is the J-damping rate, and we used (11.22) to write the dipole projection operator Π_{1} in terms of the electronic angular momentum operator J of the excited state. Table 10.8 gives experimental results for J-damping cross sections, defined via Γ_{jd} = N σ_{jd} v.

Fine-structure-changing collisions involve both l = 0 and l = 1 multipoles. These cross sections (Table 10.9) are quite small for collisions with noble gas atoms and the heavy alkali-metal atoms, but are a significant fraction of the total collision cross section for collisions with the light alkali-metal atoms and for collisions between the alkali-metal atoms and N_2 molecules (Table 10.10). For the heavy alkali metals the fine-structure splitting can be comparable to kT, so the cross sections are strongly temperature dependent [128] and obey the detailed balance relation σ_{3/2,1/2} = 2 σ_{1/2,3/2} e^{-ΔE_{FS}/(kT)}. (10.296)

Quenching of resonance fluorescence is essential for allowing optical pumping of optically thick vapors, since the resonance fluorescence is effectively unpolarized, so absorption of fluorescent light is an efficient depolarization mechanism. Without a quenching gas, the density of alkali-metal vapor that can be optically pumped is strongly limited, though it can be increased by using a large magnetic field [129]. The near resonance between rovibrational levels of N_2 and the alkali-metal resonance lines plus the non-reactivity of N_2 with the alkali metals make N_2 the usual choice for the quenching gas. The 1.4–2.1-eV excitation energy carried off by the N_2 molecules can result in substantial heating of the cell under high-density pumping conditions [130]. The l = 0 cross sections for quenching by N_2 are shown in Table 10.10.

Relaxation within the P_{3/2} Zeeman levels involves l = 1,2,3 multipoles. Using strong magnetic fields to isolate the Zeeman sublevels and decouple the atomic hyperfine structure, one can measure state-to-state cross sections (defined via Γ_{c l} = N σ_{l} v) and from them determine the multipole cross sections. A summary of the experimental knowledge of these processes is given in Table 10.11.

11 Mathematical Appendix In this chapter we discuss some specialized mathematical tools that are useful for analyzing optical pumping and collisional relaxation.

## 11.3 Recoupling Example

We will consider only the indices k = 0,1,...,2J and l = 0,1,...,2J, so the M_{kl} will be the elements of a [J]×[J] matrix. Since (11.27) must be true for any density matrix ⟨J|J⟩), it is equivalent to the linear equations ∑_{l=0}^{2J} M_{Π_{f}⟨J|J⟩} = X_{k} . (11.30)

Inverting the matrix equation (11.30), we find the desired projection operators for arbitrary electron spin quantum number J, ∑_{k=0}^{2J} Π_{f}⟨J|J⟩ = ∑_{k} (M^{-1})_{lk} X_{k} . (11.31)

One can readily verify that (11.21) and (11.22) are special cases of (11.31) for J = 1/2. For the ^{2}P_{3/2} excited state of an alkali-metal atom, with J = 3/2 we find from (11.31)

Π_{f}⟨J|J⟩ = 1/5760 [495 – 972X + 464X^2 – 64X^3]. (11.35)

Here we outline representative steps to derive (5.32) and similar recoupling identities. Using (5.16) and (5.19), we find ⟨ΔΔ†⟩ = ⟨–3[f|Δ] [O|Δ]†⟩ = ⟨–3 [f|Δ] [f|O|Δ]†⟩ = –3 ∑_L <1 1 0 | L L> [0][0][L][L] {1 1 0; L L 0} ⟨f|f|O⟩ ΔΔ† L L = – ∑_L (–1)^L ⟨f|f|O⟩ ΔΔ† . (11.36)

To go from the first line to the second line of (11.36) we used the fundamental definition of the 9j symbol as the coefficient for recoupling four angular momenta (see (5) in Section 10.1 in [15]). The last line comes from evaluating 9j symbols in which some of the angular momentum arguments are zero (see (2) in Section 10.9 in [15]) and when some of the angular momentum arguments of the resulting 6j symbol are zero (see (1) in Section 9.5.1 in [15]). The scalar product of the two spherical tensors of the last line was defined by (5.34).

Using (5.17), (5.20), and (5.21), we find ⟨ΔΔ†⟩ = (–1)^{J–S} ⟨J|S|f|J⟩ ⟨J|f|S|J⟩ = (–1)^{J–S} ∑_{KN} <J S 1 | K N> [1][1][K][N] {J S 1; K N 1} × ⟨J|f|J⟩ ⟨S|f|S⟩ . (11.37)

Since |S⟩ and ⟨J| operate in different spin spaces, we used the identity (⟨J|f|S|J⟩) = (–1)^{S+J–1} ⟨J|f|J|S⟩ (11.38)

in going from the first to the second line of (11.37). Since ⟨J| and |J⟩ operate in the same spin subspace, we find ⟨J|f|J|J⟩ = ∑_{KM} C_{JM0|J,M–M0} ⟨J|J|J,M–M0⟩ = δ_{M0} ∑_{K} C_{K0} (–1)^{J+M0} C_{JM0|J,–M0} = δ_{M0} δ_{K0} [J] (–1)^{2J}. (11.39)

We inserted the Clebsch–Gordan coefficient C_{JM0|J,–M0} in the third line with the aid of (5.18). Substituting (11.39) back into (11.37), we find ⟨ΔΔ†⟩ = – [1][1][J][L] {J S 1; 0 L 1} ⟨J|f|J⟩ ⟨S|f|S⟩ = 1/3 T(SS) (–1)^{S+J} C_{L}. (11.40)
